# Research for Joan of Arc

# Research for Joan of Arc

## 010 france-divided

Coverage: primary (the trial record of 1431 in Barrett; the depositions at Domrémy of 1455 in Murray; Monstrelet's Chronicles in Johnes's translation; the Journal d'un bourgeois de Paris; the chaplain's Gesta Henrici Quinti; Perceval de Cagny; the Chronique de la Pucelle; Boulainvilliers's letter in Quicherat 5) and scholarly (Ramsay's Lancaster and York, Wylie's Reign of Henry the Fifth vol. 2, Wylie and Waugh's vol. 3, Oman's Art of War, Beaucourt's Histoire de Charles VII vols. 1-2, Lowell's Joan of Arc, Lang's Maid of France, Thurston's signed article), with Wikipedia for the modern estimates at Agincourt, Cravant and Verneuil. All the scholarly works read are of 1881 to 1929: the modern counts of Agincourt (Curry, Barker, Sumption, Rogers) and the strengths at Cravant are known here only through Wikipedia. No modern study of the civil war of 1407-1419 was read. Wylie and Waugh's ch. 69 gives the ground of Baugé; Oman, Ramsay and Wylie give the ground and the movements of both sides at Agincourt; Ramsay, Oman, the Bourgeois and the Chronique de la Pucelle give both lines at Verneuil; Ramsay gives both banks of the Yonne at Cravant.

- 010.1 Event: Louis, Duke of Orléans, the king's only brother, is murdered in Paris on Wednesday 23 November 1407, St Clement's day, near the Porte Barbette in the rue Vieille-du-Temple, as he rides on his mule from Queen Isabeau's hôtel. agreed. "The  duke  was,  on  a  Wednesday,  the  feast-day  of  pope  St.  Clement,  assassinated  in  Paris" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 1, the murder of the Duke of Orleans, l. 7652, primary) "Le mereredi 23 novembre 1407, le due d'Orléans avait sonpé chez la reine Isabeau, et, au sortir de l'hôtel Barbette" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-1-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-201-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 1, ch. 5, l. 11842, scholarly) "on  Wednesday Orleans  was  set  on  in  the  dark  by  the  emissaries  of  Burgundy" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1299, scholarly)
- 010.2 Figure: the band that killed Louis of Orléans, about eighteen men lodged near the Porte Barbette, Monstrelet. single (Monstrelet). The Wikipedia article on Louis (Assassination) gives fifteen masked men led by Raoulet d'Anquetonville, a servant of the Duke of Burgundy; its file name holds a comma and cannot be cited here, and its footnote is Theis 1992, not read. "This  murder  was  committed by  about  eighteen  men" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 1, the murder of the Duke of Orleans, l. 7653, primary)
- 010.3 Event: three days before, on Sunday 20 November 1407, the two dukes had sworn friendship and taken communion together. agreed. "Sunday,  November  20,  1407,  they  received  together  the  Holy Communion" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1298, scholarly) "One  Sunday  in  November,  1407, Louis  and  John  together  partook  of  the" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 324, scholarly) "Trois jours auparavant, le duc de Bourgogne avait juré une éternelle amitié à son cousin d'Orléans" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-1-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-201-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 1, ch. 5, l. 11850, scholarly)
- 010.4 Event: John the Fearless, Duke of Burgundy, owns that he ordered the murder, to the princes and then openly, withdraws from Paris, and has the deed praised before the great men of the kingdom. agreed. "who  had  commanded  them  to  do  this  deed,  as  he  afterward  publicly  confesaed" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 1, the murder of the Duke of Orleans, l. 7709, primary) "C'est moi qui ai fait le cas; le diable m'a tenté" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-1-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-201-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 1, ch. 5, l. 11866, scholarly) "Burgundy  acknowledged  his  guilt,  and  retired  to  Lille." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1304, scholarly) "the  principal  men of  the  kingdom  assembled  soon  afterwards  with  the  duke of  Burgundy  to  hear  a  panegyric  on  the  murder" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 329, scholarly)
- 010.5 Person: Charles VI, King of France, mad at intervals since 1392; Louis of Orléans, his younger brother; John the Fearless, Duke of Burgundy, his cousin and Louis's rival. agreed; the year 1392 single (Lang). "Since  1392  the  intermittent  madness,  and  the  constant  folly  of Charles  VI" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1271, scholarly) "Louis, duke  of  Orleans,  the  king's  younger  brother" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 297, scholarly)
- 010.6 Event: the feud of the two houses becomes the civil war; from 1410 Bernard, Count of Armagnac, father-in-law of the young Charles of Orléans, leads the Orléans party, which takes the name of Armagnacs. agreed. "giving  the  name  of  Armagnacs  to  the  Orleanist partisans" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 351, scholarly) "led  by  Bernard,  Comte  d'Armagnac, was  the  party  of  France  south  of  the  Loire" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1314, scholarly)
- 010.7 Border: the lands of the dukes of Burgundy about 1407: the cities of Flanders to the north of France and the duchy and county of Burgundy to the east (Lowell), with Picardy and much of northern and eastern France in their power (Lang); the Armagnac party is the party of France south of the Loire (Lang, 010.6). Both give the lands by province, with no line. agreed. "they  ruled  the  trading  cities  of  Flanders  to  the  north  of France,  and  both  the  duchy  and  county  of  Burgundy  to the  east" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 305, scholarly) "huge cantles  of  Flanders,  Picardy,  northern  and  eastern  France" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1280, scholarly)
- 010.8 Person: Joan was born at Domrémy, which with Greux made one parish, the daughter of Jacques d'Arc and Isabelle, and baptised in the church of Domrémy (Saint-Remy): her own answer of 21 February 1431, and Jean Morel, her godfather, in 1455. agreed. "she was born in the village of Domrémy, which is one with the village of Greux … her father's name was Jacques d'Arc, and her mother's Isabelle" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Barrett, first public session, 21 February 1431, primary) "Jeanne was born at Domremy and was baptised at the Parish Church of Saint Remy, in that place. Her father was named Jacques d'Arc, her mother Isabelle" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray Part II, depositions at Domrémy 1455, Jean Morel, primary)
- 010.9 Event: she was born about 1412: in February 1431 she said she thought she was nineteen; Lowell calls 1412 the commonly accepted date and not quite sure, Lang says the evidence points to 1410-1412. agreed for about 1412; open for the exact year. "Asked how old she was, she replied she thought nineteen." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Barrett, first public session, 21 February 1431, primary) "1412  is  the  commonly accepted  date,  though  it  is  impossible  to  be  quite  sure" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 2, note, l. 1056, scholarly) "The  birth-year  of  the  Maid  is  not  known with  certainty:  all  evidence  proves  that  it  was  in  1410-1412" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 2, l. 1754, scholarly)
- 010.10 Event: her birthday, the night of the Epiphany (6 January), rests on one letter, Perceval de Boulainvilliers's to the Duke of Milan of 1429; Lang, Lowell and Thurston take the day from it. single (Boulainvilliers). "In  nocte  Epiphaniarum  Domini" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, Boulainvilliers to the Duke of Milan, l. 6161, primary) "PerceVal  de  Boulainvilliers,  in  a letter  to  a  foreign  prince,  told  the  following  tale" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 2, l. 1760, scholarly) "About the  feast  of  the  Epiphany,  1412 … Isabel  gave birth  to  another  daughter" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 2, l. 1028, scholarly) "Born at Domremy in Champagne, probably on 6 January, 1412" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, opening, scholarly)
- 010.11 Person: her father Jacques d'Arc, born at Ceffonds in Champagne, and her mother Isabelle, of Vouthon, called Romée, were labourers of good repute and not rich; Lowell makes Jacques the dean of the village. agreed. "He  was  born  at Ceffonds  in  Champagne … he  married  Isabel  of  Vouthon" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 2, l. 983, scholarly) "In  Domremy,  about  1410,  dwelt  Jacques  d'Arc,  a  native  of Ceffonds  in  Champagne,  with  his  wife,  from  Vouthon,  named Isabelle" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 2, l. 1681, scholarly) "Jeannette was born, at Domremy, of Jacques d'Arc and Isabelle, his wife, labourers, good and true Catholics, honest folk and worthy, according to their ability, but not rich" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray Part II, depositions at Domrémy 1455, Beatrix Estellin, primary)
- 010.12 Event: her childhood as she told it in 1431: her mother taught her the Paternoster, Ave Maria and Credo; she learned to sew and spin, and "feared no woman in Rouen" at it; the witnesses of 1455 tell of the same work. agreed. "Asked if in her youth she had learned any craft, she said yes, to sew and spin: and in sewing and spinning, she feared no woman in Rouen" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Barrett, second public session, 22 February 1431, primary) "duties of women, such as spinning, and other things" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray Part II, depositions at Domrémy 1455, Jean Morel, primary)
- 010.13 Event: whether she kept the beasts. disputed: she told the court in 1431 that at home she saw to the household and did not go to the fields after the sheep and other animals; Jean Morel in 1455 says she followed the plough and sometimes minded the cattle; Lowell has her tend the cattle with the other children; Thurston calls quite unfounded the idea that she spent her childhood alone in the pastures with the sheep and cattle. "she did not go to the fields to look after the sheep and other animals" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Barrett, second public session, 22 February 1431, primary) "Up to the time she left her parents she followed the plough and sometimes minded the cattle in the fields" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray Part II, depositions at Domrémy 1455, Jean Morel, primary) "she  played  with  the  other  children  on  holidays, and  with  thein  she  tended  the  cattle  at  pasture" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 2, l. 1065, scholarly) "the popular idea that she spent the days of her childhood in the pastures, alone with the sheep and cattle, is quite unfounded" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, childhood, scholarly)
- 010.14 Border: the overlord of Domrémy. disputed: Lowell sets the village, south of the Three Fountains brook, in the duchy of Bar, in the part that "moved" from the kingdom, so that Joan was born a subject of the Duke of Bar and only remotely of the king, and calls the question one of endless controversy; Lang has her father's house on the Lorraine march or just across it, the villagers speaking of "going into France"; Thurston has the village on the confines of Burgundian land, always loyal to Charles; Wikipedia, citing Lowell, has it in the duchy of Bar until 1766. "Joan  was  born,  therefore,  a  subject  of  the  duke  of  Bar,  and,  only remotely,  of  the  king  of  France" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 2, note, l. 915, scholarly) "From  the  Duchy  of  Loraine  the  house  of  the  father  of  Jeanne was  separated  only  by  a  little  burn,  or  it  was  even  on  the  Loraine side  of  the  march" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 2, l. 1663, scholarly) "The village of Domremy lay upon the confines of territory which recognized the suzerainty of the Duke of Burgundy" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, opening, scholarly) "Until 1766, Domrémy was part of the Duchy of Bar" (Domrémy-la-Pucelle.wiki, History, wikipedia)
- 010.15 Border: Vaucouleurs, the walled town and castle down the Meuse from Domrémy, held for the Dauphin by Robert de Baudricourt, an Armagnac partisan; its castellany, Domrémy and Greux with it, an island of loyalty in a land Anglo-Burgundian or outside France; Champagne to the west Anglo-Burgundian. agreed. "then  held  for  the  Dauphin  by  a  stout,  rough,  humorous  captain, Robert  de  Baudricourt … a  kind  of  island  of  loyalty  in  a  region either  Anglo-Burgundian" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 2, l. 1659, scholarly) "partisan  of  the  Armagnacs,  and  a  soldier  of  fortune,  who held  the  little  city  of  Vaucouleurs  for  the  dauphin" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 2, l. 1095, scholarly) "Domremy had always remained loyal to Charles" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, opening, scholarly)
- 010.16 Border: Maxey, across the Meuse from Domrémy, was Burgundian; Joan knew only one Burgundian in Domrémy, and the boys of Domrémy fought the boys of Maxey, Armagnacs against Burgundians. agreed. "she answered that she only knew one Burgundian … Asked if at Maxey the people were Burgundians or enemies of the Burgundians, she answered they were Burgundians" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Barrett, public session of 24 February 1431, primary) "The  boys of  Joan's  age  at  Domremy  used  to  fight  in  the  meadows with  the  boys  of  Maxey,  the  former  as  Armagnacs,  the latter  as  Burgundians" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 2, l. 1291, scholarly)
- 010.17 Event: after Troyes (1420) the war of the two parties reaches the Meuse: the Burgundians invade Bar, the Cardinal of Bar resigns the duchy to René of Anjou under the guardianship of the Duke of Lorraine, and Charles of Lorraine swears allegiance to Henry V; by 1428 the Duke of Lorraine has acknowledged Henry VI. single (Lowell). "was  soon  persuaded  to  swear  allegiance  to  Henry" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 2, l. 1123, scholarly) "The  duke  of  Lorraine  had been  cajoled  and  bullied  into  acknowledging  Henry  VI." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 664, scholarly)
- 010.18 Event: Henry V, claiming the crown of France as Edward III had, takes his chance in the civil war and in 1415 enters Normandy. agreed. "found  his  chance to  assert  what  he  believed  to  be  his  right  to  the  throne, and  in  1415  entered  Normandy" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 367, scholarly) "The more  daring  Henry  V,  in  1413,  revived  the  pretensions  of Edward  III" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1320, scholarly)
- 010.19 Event: Henry's fleet anchors at the mouth of the Seine near the Chef de Caux on Thursday 13 August 1415, about three miles from Harfleur, and lands unopposed from the next day, the vigil of the Assumption, between Harfleur and Honfleur. agreed. "On  Thursday,  13th  August,  about  five  o'clock  p.m.,  the … King  dropped  anchor  at  the  mouth  of  the  Seine" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 14222, scholarly) "On  the  vigil  of  the  Assumption  of  Our  Lady,  they  made  in  the  night-time  a  harbour … lies  between  Harfleur  and  Honfleur,  where  the  river  Seine  enters  the  sea,  and  landed  without any  effusion  of  blood" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 1, Henry lays siege to Harfleur, l. 24780, primary)
- 010.20 Figure: Henry's army at the landing, about 6,000 men-at-arms and 23,000 archers, Monstrelet. single. "about  six  thousand  helmets  and  twenty-three  thousud archers" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 1, Henry lays siege to Harfleur, l. 24791, primary)
- 010.21 Event: Harfleur, bombarded and mined, surrenders on 22 September 1415, St Maurice's day, when no relief has come from the French army at Rouen. agreed. "So  when  the  noon  of  Sept.  22" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift02wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift02wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie 2, ch. 30, l. 3753, scholarly) "it  was  surrendered to  the  king  of  England  on  St.  Maurice's  day" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 1, Henry lays siege to Harfleur, l. 24893, primary) "the  place  having  been  actually  reduced  by  steady bombardment,  though  mining  co-operated  in  the  result" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 18853, scholarly)
- 010.22 Figure: the cost of the siege: a fifth of Henry's army dead, more of camp fever than of fighting, Oman; about 5,000 sick sent home besides the dead, the chaplain's Gesta. single (each author). "this  modest  success  had  cost  the  lives  of  a  fifth  of" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 18855, scholarly) "qui erant numero circiter quinque millia" (archive.org-download-henriciquintian00willgoog-henriciquintian00willgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Gesta, after Harfleur, l. 4682, primary)
- 010.23 Event: against most of his council Henry marches from Harfleur for Calais, lightly equipped, on 8 or 9 October 1415 (the Gesta's date contradicts itself; Elmham gives the 9th, Chastellain the 10th, Ramsay). agreed for the march; disputed for the day. "the  8th  or  9th  October  Henry  left  Harfleur" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 14372, scholarly) "The  king,  however,  announced  that  he intended  to  march  to  Calais  across  Picardy" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 18871, scholarly)
- 010.24 Event: the fords of the Somme are held (Blanche-Taque on 13 October, Ramsay); the army crosses only on 19 October at Béthencourt above Péronne, the tenth day of the march, Oman. single for the crossing (Oman). "army only succeeded in crossing the river on 19th October" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 18922, scholarly) "But  on  Sunday  the  13th  October,  when" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 14439, scholarly)
- 010.25 Figure: the English at Agincourt. disputed: 900 lances and 5,000 archers fit to fight after Harfleur, the chaplain's Gesta, an eyewitness; some 900 lances and 3,000 archers, Ramsay's estimate from the Agincourt roll; not much more than 6,000 men, Oman; 9,000, Anne Curry (2005) from the pay records, against 6,000 in Barker, Rogers and Sumption (the infobox's strength), Wikipedia. "nongentas lanceas et quinque millia sagittariorum" (archive.org-download-henriciquintian00willgoog-henriciquintian00willgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Gesta, after Harfleur, l. 4689, primary) "estimated  that  Henry  left  Harfleur  with  some  900 … lances  and  3000  archers" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 14884, scholarly) "Henry had not much more than six thousand men left" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 18867, scholarly) "the French army was 12,000 strong, and the English army 9,000, proportions of four to three" (Battle_of_Agincourt.wiki, Numbers at Agincourt, wikipedia)
- 010.26 Figure: the French at Agincourt. disputed: 150,000 in camp the night before, Monstrelet; more than half as many again as the English, the Bourgeois; at least three times the English by the lowest contemporary count, and about four times with the regulars counted, Ramsay; three or four times the English at Bapaume on 20 October, Oman; 12,000 (Curry), about 14,000 (Sumption), up to 24,000 with the armed servants (Rogers), Wikipedia. "the  French  were  full  (me  hundred  and  fifty  thousand  strong" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 1, the battle of Azincourt, l. 25220, primary) "et estoient les Françoys plus la moictié que Angloys" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §129, 1415, primary) "calculation  rates  them  as  three  times  as  numerous  as the  English" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 14927, scholarly) "army three or four times as numerous as that of King Henry" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 18941, scholarly) "Sumption, thus, concludes that the French had 14,000 men" (Battle_of_Agincourt.wiki, Numbers at Agincourt, wikipedia)
- 010.27 Battle: Agincourt, Friday 25 October 1415, the feast of Sts Crispin and Crispinian: Henry V's English against the French host under the Constable d'Albret, on the ground between the woods and enclosures of Agincourt and Tramecourt across the road to Calais; an English victory. The positions and movements of both sides: Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 18976-19210; Ramsay 1 ch. 15, l. 14840-15330; Wylie 2 ch. 34, l. 9276-11872. agreed. "Crispini et Crispiniani, xxv°. die Octobris" (archive.org-download-henriciquintian00willgoog-henriciquintian00willgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Gesta, the battle, l. 5652, primary) "Friday,  Oct.  25,  the  feast  of  SS.  Crispin  and  Crispinian" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift02wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift02wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie 2, ch. 34, l. 9414, scholarly) "en ung lieu nommé Agincourt, près de Rousseauville; et en ladicte place, le jour Sainct Crespin et Crespinien" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §129, 1415, primary) "between  the  woods  of  Tramecourt  on  the  one  side, and  the  closes  and  hedgerows  of  Agincourt  on  the  other  side" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 14918, scholarly)
- 010.28 Battle: the ground at Agincourt: a gap no more than 1,100 yards wide between the orchards and enclosures of the two villages, the open space 1,200 yards long between the armies, the ploughed fields soaked by a week of rain, Oman; a French frontage of 800 or 900 yards at most, the French drawn back some 300 yards along the road to Ruisseauville onto higher ground, Ramsay. agreed for the narrow ground; single for each measure. "no more than eleven hundred yards across" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 18980, scholarly) "with  a  frontage  of  800  or  900  yards  at  the  most  to  defend" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 14920, scholarly)
- 010.29 Battle: the order at Agincourt: the English in one line, York on the right, the king in the centre, Camoys on the left, each a body of men-at-arms between wings of archers behind sharpened stakes, Oman; the French in three divisions, two mounted squadrons (Vendôme and Clignet de Brabant) to ride down the archers, a first line on foot under d'Albret, Orléans, Bourbon, Eu, Richemont and Boucicaut, a second under Alençon and Bar, a third on horseback under Marle, Dammartin and Fauquembergue, Oman and Ramsay. agreed. "Edward Duke of York led the right, the king himself the centre, Lord Camoys the left." (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 18996, scholarly) "The  second  or  main  division  of  the  French  army  was commanded  by  the  Dukes  of  Bar  and  Alenjon" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 14993, scholarly)
- 010.30 Battle: the course at Agincourt: after a parley Henry advances to bring the French within bowshot, some 400 yards (Oman) or 700 to 800 yards to the Agincourt-Tramecourt road (Ramsay), and replants the stakes; the French horse is shot down or breaks back through its own line; the dismounted men-at-arms struggle forward through the mud and are beaten in the mêlée; at an alarm Henry has the prisoners killed. disputed for the distance of the advance. "When he had advanced, slowly as was necessary in the deep mud, for some four hundred yards" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19044, scholarly) "700  or  800  yards  would  bring  them  up  to  the" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 15132, scholarly)
- 010.31 Figure: the French losses at Agincourt. disputed: more than 10,000 dead by the heralds' count, of whom only 1,600 of low degree, Monstrelet; some 3,000 knights ("gilded spurs") besides the princes, the Bourgeois; chroniclers' estimates of 4,000 to 11,000 dead and about 1,500 prisoners, Ramsay; 1,500 nobles, 3,000 men-at-arms and perhaps 1,000 common soldiers dead and over 1,000 prisoners, Oman. "that  day,  amounted  to  upwards  of  ten  thousand" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 1, the battle of Azincourt, l. 25749, primary) "et bien trois mil esperons dorez sur les autres" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §130, 1415, primary) "estimates,  ranging  from  4000  to  11,000.  The  prisoners  were estimated  at  1500" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 15319, scholarly) "fifteen hundred counts, barons, knights, and nobles, with three thousand men-at-arms, and perhaps a thousand common soldiers" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19194, scholarly)
- 010.32 Person: the princes lost at Agincourt: killed, the Constable Charles d'Albret, the Dukes of Brabant, Alençon and Bar, the Count of Nevers and others; taken to England, the Dukes of Orléans and Bourbon, the Counts of Eu, Vendôme and Richemont, and Marshal Boucicaut. agreed. "Ceulx cy nommez furent tous mors en la bataille … mais de ceulx qui furent prins et menez en Angleterre, le duc d'Orleans, le duc de Bourbon" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §130, 1415, primary) "three  Dukes  (Brabant,  Alengon, and  Bar)" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 15313, scholarly) "were the Dukes of Orleans and Bourbon, the Counts of Eu, Vendéme, and Richemont, and the Marshal Boucicault" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19202, scholarly) "The  greatest  and the  bravest  of  the  French  nobles  were  killed  or  carried to  England  as  prisoners" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 377, scholarly)
- 010.33 Figure: the English losses at Agincourt, thirteen men-at-arms and about 100 others, among them the Duke of York and the Earl of Suffolk, Ramsay; fewer than 100 in all, Oman; both from the English chroniclers. single. "of  men-at-arms  there fell  apparently  just  thirteen" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 15324, scholarly) "the victors had lost less than one hundred men in all" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19189, scholarly)
- 010.34 Event: the army reaches Calais on 29 October 1415. single (Ramsay). "On  the  29th October  the  army  reached  Calais" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 15, l. 15369, scholarly)
- 010.35 Event: in May and June 1418 the Burgundians take Paris and massacre the Armagnacs, the Count of Armagnac among them; the Dauphin Charles, the king's last surviving son, fifteen (Lowell) or sixteen (Lang), is carried off and flees to Bourges. agreed. "only  the  Dauphin  Charles,  the king's  last  surviving  son,  a  boy  of  fifteen,  was  snatched from  his  bed" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 398, scholarly) "Tn  May  to  June  1418,  the  Burgundians  and  the  mob  of  Paris overthrew  the  Armagnac  soldiery  in  the  place … The  Dauphin,  a  boy  of  sixteen,  fled  to  Bourges" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1338, scholarly)
- 010.36 Event: Henry V lands again at Touques in Normandy on 1 August 1417. agreed for Touques; single for the day (Ramsay). "On  the  1st  August  he  landed  at … Touques  in  Normandy" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 17, l. 16533, scholarly) "When  Henry  left  Touques  on  Aug.  13" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 51, l. 3622, scholarly)
- 010.37 Event: Caen, the first town of the conquest, is taken by assault on 4 September 1417, its castle on 20 September; Lisieux and Bayeux follow. agreed. "On  the  4th  September  the  town  was  carried  by  assault" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 17, l. 16633, scholarly) "an  assault  was planned  for  Sept.  4" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 51, l. 3859, scholarly) "In 1417 he took Caen, Lisieux, and Bayeux" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19277, scholarly)
- 010.38 Event: Henry conquers Normandy town by town, sparing the country, and by the end of 1419 only Mont-Saint-Michel is left to the French. agreed. "to conquer it town by town, till he should have taken the whole duchy" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19255, scholarly) "to  win  towns  piecemeal" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 17, l. 16585, scholarly)
- 010.39 Event: the siege of Rouen: Henry before the city on 29 July 1418, blockade by 1 August; famine; articles signed on 13 January 1419 and the city surrendered on the 19th, with a ransom of 300,000 écus. agreed. "Henry  appeared  before  Rouen  on  the  29th  July" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 18, l. 17320, scholarly) "agreed  to  surrender  the  place  with  all  arms  and munitions  of  war  on  the  19th  of  the  month" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 18, l. 17582, scholarly) "held out from August 1418 to January 1419, suffering all the horrors of famine" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19289, scholarly)
- 010.40 Event: John the Fearless is killed at a meeting with the Dauphin in a fenced enclosure on the bridge of Montereau, where the Yonne meets the Seine, ten attendants allowed each side; his head is cleft with an axe. agreed for the place and the deed; disputed for the day: Sunday 10 September 1419, Ramsay, Wylie and Waugh, Beaucourt (who notes one account that gives the 9th); 9 September, Oman. "On  Sunday,  10th  September,  the  day  finally  chosen  for" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 19, l. 18008, scholarly) "it  is  certain  that  the duke's  head  was  cleft  with  an  axe" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 62, l. 10613, scholarly) "at  the  junction of  the  Seine  and  the  Yonne" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 62, l. 10566, scholarly) "the  interview  should  take  place  on  the  bridge at  Montereau  on  Sept.  io" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 62, l. 10572, scholarly) "Le dimanche 10 septembre" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-1-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-201-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 1, ch. 5, l. 13879, scholarly) "on 9th September 1419, at the Bridge of Montereau" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19312, scholarly)
- 010.41 Event: how the duke was killed and what the Dauphin knew. disputed: the Burgundian account has him cut down from behind as he knelt to the Dauphin, armed men rushing in from behind the Dauphin, Tanguy du Châtel striking first (Ramsay, following Monstrelet and the secretary Seguinat); the Armagnac version has him attacked after offering violence; Lowell has an old servant of Louis of Orléans split his head after his arrogant words, in a plot laid for months; Wylie and Waugh say what followed will never be known; Lang calls the Dauphin's foreknowledge and presence matters of dispute. "a  gang  of  armed  men  rushed  in  upon  him from  behind  the  Dauphin" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 19, l. 18040, scholarly) "followed  will  never  be  known.  Many  stories  are  extant" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 62, l. 10608, scholarly) "An  old servant  of  Louis  of  Orleans,  taking  advantage  of  the duke's  arrogant  words  and  bearing,  split  open  his  head with  an  axe" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 463, scholarly) "Whether  or  not  the Dauphin  had  guilty  foreknowledge  of  the  design,  whether  or  not he  was  present  at  its  bloody  consummation,  are  matters  of  dispute" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1353, scholarly)
- 010.42 Event: the murdered duke's son, Philip the Good, and the Burgundian party go over to Henry and back his claim to the throne, to shut the Dauphin out of the succession. agreed. "at  once  offered  to  Henry  terms  so  favorable  that  the English  king  accepted  them" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 473, scholarly) "They promised to back his claim to the French throne, in order to exclude the treacherous Dauphin from the succession" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19316, scholarly) "The  new  Duke  of  Burgundy,  Philippe,  pursuing  the  blood-feud, strengthened  his  alliance  with  England" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1357, scholarly)
- 010.43 Event: the Treaty of Troyes is sealed in the cathedral of Troyes on Tuesday 21 May 1420, Henry having entered the city on the 20th: Henry is to marry Catherine and is made heir of Charles VI and regent of France, the two crowns to be united after Charles's death, and none is to treat with the Dauphin. agreed. "Next  day,  May  21,  the  councils  of  the  two  kings  and  the duke  deliberated  together,  and  the  treaty  was  finally  sealed  in the  cathedral" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 63, l. 11567, scholarly) "Le 20, Henri V entrait dans Troyes; le 21, le traité était" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-1-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-201-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 1, ch. 2, l. 7360, scholarly) "was,  on  paper,  actual  Regent  and  prospective  Heir  of the  realm  of  France" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 19, l. 18405, scholarly) "whereby  Henry,  married to  the  daughter  of  Charles  VL,  was  declared  the  heir  of the  crazy  king  and  regent  of  France" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 475, scholarly)
- 010.44 Event: by the treaty the Dauphin is disinherited and branded with the murder of Montereau. agreed. "disinherited  the  Dauphin  Charles,  and  publicly  branded  the  boy with  the  guilt  of  the  murder  of  Burgundy" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1360, scholarly) "horrible  and  enormous  crimes  perpetrated  by him" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 19, l. 18401, scholarly)
- 010.45 Event: Henry marries Catherine of France in the cathedral of Troyes on Trinity Sunday, 2 June 1420. agreed for the marriage; single for the day (Ramsay). "Trinity  Sunday  (2nd  June),  Henry  and  Catherine … were  married  in  the  cathedral  of  Troyes" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 19, l. 18460, scholarly) "Meanwhile  Henry  V  married  Catherine,  the  sister  of  the  Dauphin" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1364, scholarly)
- 010.46 Border: 1420, the obedience of Charles VI that Henry now governed is the land north of the Loire without Burgundy and Brittany, cut up by Dauphinist holds such as Melun, Meaux, Soissons and Compiègne (Ramsay); Henry is acknowledged over the Île-de-France, Champagne and Picardy as well as Normandy (Oman). Both give it by provinces and towns. agreed. "limited  to  the  territory  north  of  the  Loire … cut  up  by  a  network  of  Dauphinois  holds,  including  Melun" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 19, l. 18426, scholarly) "acknowledged as ruler all over the Isle de France, Champagne, and Picardy" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19324, scholarly)
- 010.47 Battle: Baugé, Anjou, 1421: the Dauphin's Scots and French under the Earl of Buchan beat Thomas, Duke of Clarence, who attacked with his mounted men-at-arms without waiting for his archers; Clarence is killed and the Earls of Somerset and Huntingdon taken. The ground: Wylie and Waugh 3 ch. 69 (the fight on the low hill by the church of Vieil-Baugé, beyond the Couasnon bridge), l. 16832-17430; Ramsay 1 ch. 20, l. 18961-19000; Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19343-19368. agreed. "refused to wait for his archers, and pursued with the cavalry alone" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19349, scholarly) "Clarence  had been  defeated  and  slain  ;  Somerset  and  Huntingdon  were prisoners" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 20, l. 18957, scholarly) "En  ceste  bataille  fut  le  duc  de  Clarance  occis" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chronique de la Pucelle ch. 182, l. 7478, primary) "et  ne  print  sinon  les  archiers  de  son corps" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, Baugé, l. 5992, primary) "main  conflict  occurred  on  the  crest  and  eastern  and  southern  slopes  of  the  low  hill" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 69, note, l. 17104, scholarly)
- 010.48 Event: the day of Baugé. disputed: Holy Saturday, 22 March 1421, in Beaucourt and in the Scots' own letter written at Baugé "the eve of Easter", and in Wylie and Waugh and Ramsay, who have the Scots reach Baugé on Good Friday and Clarence attack them on the Saturday (Ramsay's text reads "31st March" for that Good Friday, which Wylie and Waugh date 21 March); 21 March in Oman; the Saturday of Palm Sunday, in the year 1420 old style, in the Chronique de la Pucelle. "un éclatant succès remporté le 22 mars" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-1-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-201-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 1, ch. 7, l. 16846, scholarly) "Escript en ceste dicle ville de Baugé, la veille de Pasques" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-1-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-201-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 1, ch. 7, the letter of Douglas and Buchan, l. 16908, primary) "This was Baugé (March 21, 1421)" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19343, scholarly) "le  samedi  de  Pasqucs  flories  1420" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chronique de la Pucelle ch. 182, l. 7475, primary) "March  21,  they  were  at  Le  Lude  on  the  Loir" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 69, l. 16849, scholarly) "dinner  on  the  Saturday,  when  his  foragers  brought  in  some" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 69, l. 16902, scholarly) "On  Good  Friday  (31st  March)  the  allies  reached  Baug6" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 20, l. 18969, scholarly)
- 010.49 Figure: the numbers at Baugé. disputed: the English in Anjou 10,000 to 12,000 or more, Cagny; Clarence's own body about 1,500 men-at-arms with hardly any archers, Wikipedia; the Scots and French outnumbering his horse two to one, Oman; about 600 English taken, the Chronique de la Pucelle; Wylie and Waugh, weighing the chronicles, give the English 1,500 men-at-arms from a record of the time and find that the victors' counts of the English losses bear out some 1,500 men engaged, the French and Scots being put at 5,000 to 6,000 by Jean Juvenal and at twice the English by Le Fèvre. "nombre  de  x  à  xij  mille  ou  plus,  estoient  venuz  ou duché  d'Anjou" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, Baugé, l. 5945, primary) "with only about 1500 men-at-arms available, and virtually no archers" (Battle_of_Baugé.wiki, Battle, wikipedia) "they outnumbered his horse by two to one" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19352, scholarly) "au  nombre  d'environ 600  hommes" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chronique de la Pucelle ch. 182, l. 7483, primary) "states  that  the  English  had  1500  men-at-arms" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 69, note, l. 17126, scholarly) "confirm  the  view  that  they  had  some  1500  men  engaged" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 69, note, l. 17163, scholarly) "Scots  are  given  5000-6000  combatants  by  Jean  Juvenal" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 69, note, l. 17114, scholarly) "says  that  the  dauphinists  were  twice  as  many  as  the  English" (archive.org-download-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-reignofhenryfift03wyliuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wylie and Waugh 3, ch. 69, note, l. 17131, scholarly)
- 010.50 Event: Henry besieges Meaux on the Marne from October 1421 (about 6 October, Ramsay; 21 October, Oman) until May 1422, the Market to be handed over by 10 May; Oman says he took there the dysentery that killed him. agreed for the siege; disputed for its first day. "About  the  6th  October … the  army  settled  down  to  the  siege  of  Meaux" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 20, l. 19299, scholarly) "Meaux, which held out from 21st October 1421 till May 1422" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 1, l. 19337, scholarly)
- 010.51 Event: Henry V dies at the castle of the Bois de Vincennes on 31 August 1422, of dysentery. agreed. "carried  to  the  castle,  where  he  took  to  his  bed,  never  to rise  again" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 21, l. 19587, scholarly) "death (August 31, 1422)" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 2, l. 19381, scholarly) "Henry  V  and  Charles  VI  passed  away  within  two  months  of each  other  (August  31,  October  22,  1422)" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1412, scholarly)
- 010.52 Person: John, Duke of Bedford, Henry V's brother, regent of France for the infant Henry VI, nine months old, Gloucester holding England. agreed. "Apparently  he  appointed Bedford  to  be  Regent  of  France  and  Normandy" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 21, l. 19625, scholarly) "John,  Duke  of  Bedford,  was  Regent  of  France" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1420, scholarly) "a  baby  nine  months  old" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 510, scholarly)
- 010.53 Event: the day Charles VI died. disputed: 21 October 1422, the eve of the Eleven Thousand Virgins, in his hôtel of Saint-Pol, the Bourgeois and Ramsay; 22 October, Lang (010.51). "le XXIe jour du moys d'octobre, vigille de XIm Vierges, trespassa de ce siecle le bon roy Charles" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §361, 1422, primary) "He  had  died  on  the  21st  October" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 22, l. 21073, scholarly)
- 010.54 Event: at Charles VI's burial at Saint-Denis on 11 November 1422 a herald cries Henry VI King of France and England, and Bedford goes back to Paris with the king's sword borne before him as regent. agreed. "que Dieu voulsist sauver et garder le duc Henry de Lanclastre, roy de France et d'Angleterre" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §369, 1422, primary) "On  the  11th  November  he  was … laid  in  his  grave  at  Saint  Denis  ;  and  young  Henry  was" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 22, l. 21075, scholarly)
- 010.55 Event: the Dauphin takes the title of King, Charles VII, at Mehun-sur-Yèvre on 30 October 1422, the heralds crying "Vive le Roi". single (Beaucourt): Ramsay's note for Mehun cites Beaucourt, i. 241. "On  the  30th  October,  before  his  father  was  yet  buried,  he" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 22, l. 21174, scholarly) "le 30 qu'il prit le titre de" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 2, l. 2795, scholarly)
- 010.56 Event: "King of Bourges": the nickname his enemies gave Charles for his long stays at Bourges, Beaucourt; France writes of "the little King of Bourges". agreed for the name; single (Beaucourt) for its cause. "ce surnom de Roi de Bourges que ses ennemis" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-1-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-201-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 1, ch. 7, l. 17479, scholarly) "As for the little King of Bourges" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 3, l. 20861, scholarly)
- 010.57 Border: the divided kingdom of the 1420s, as Lowell gives it for 1428: Normandy, Paris and the land about it, Perche, Alençon, most of Maine and Champagne English; Picardy, Flanders and the two Burgundies Philip's; Brittany its own prince's; Charles VII acknowledged south of the Loire between the Garonne and the Rhône and in Dauphiné; Bordeaux and much of Guyenne English. Bedford governs Normandy, Paris and the north (Lang). By provinces, with no line. agreed for Bedford's north and Charles VII's south of the Loire (Lang here and at 010.6); single (Lowell) for the list of provinces. "Paris  and  the  country  about  it,  Perche, Alengon,  most  of  Maine  and  Champagne,  were in  the  hands  of  the  English" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 655, scholarly) "The  remainder  of  France,  the  country  south  of  the Loire  between  the  Garonne  and  the  Rhone,  together  with Dauphiny,  acknowledged  Charles  VII" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 1, l. 690, scholarly) "he  governed  Normandy,  Paris,  and  the north" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1421, scholarly)
- 010.58 Event: Bedford binds Burgundy closer by marrying Anne, the Duke of Burgundy's sister (Troyes, June 1423). agreed. "Bedford  took  the  fifth  sister,  Anne" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 22, l. 21291, scholarly) "He  married  Anne,  sister  of  the  Duke  of  Burgundy" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1424, scholarly)
- 010.59 Battle: Cravant on the Yonne, 1423: an English and Burgundian army under Salisbury, come from Auxerre to relieve the town, crosses to the left bank and faces the Dauphin's French, Scots and others drawn up between the walls and the river; Salisbury's men wade the Yonne on the left, Willoughby carries the bridge on the right, the garrison sallies on the French rear, and the Dauphin's army breaks; the Scots suffer most and their commander, Sir John Stewart of Darnley, loses an eye and is taken. Both banks and the movements: Ramsay 1 ch. 22, l. 21442-21490. agreed. "At  last  Salisbury  on  the  left  gave the  word" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 22, l. 21461, scholarly) "the English took the offensive, contrary to their wont, after waiting a long time for the French to attack" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 2, l. 19424, scholarly) "Les Français y furent battus" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 1, l. 527, scholarly)
- 010.60 Event: the day of Cravant. disputed: Saturday 31 July 1423, Beaucourt, Tuetey's note to the Bourgeois, Ramsay (the allies marched out on 30 July and found the enemy "next morning") and Monstrelet ("the following Saturday"); 30 July 1423, Lang; "Saturday, the last day of August" 1423, the Chronique de la Pucelle (Geste des nobles ch. 202). "bataille s'engagea le 31 juillet" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 1, l. 527, scholarly) "On  the  30th  July  they  marched  out" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 22, l. 21448, scholarly) "the  battle  of  Cravant (July  30,  1423)" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1429, scholarly) "la victoire de Cravant remportée le samedi 31" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois, Tuetey's note 757, 1423, l. 11545, scholarly) "On  the  following  Saturday,  they  decamped" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, the battle of Crevant, l. 35765, primary) "jour  d'aonst  l'an  mil  quatre  cent  vint" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chronique de la Pucelle (Geste des nobles) ch. 202, l. 7927, primary)
- 010.61 Figure: the armies and losses at Cravant: the Scots of the front ranks 3,000, the greater part of them killed or taken, some 400 knights and gentlemen taken and about 1,200 slain, Monstrelet; the constable of Scotland, Ventadour and forty-four other gentlemen of name taken, the Chronique de la Pucelle (Geste des nobles ch. 202), and 2,000 to 3,000 killed and taken (its ch. 4); about 4,000 Anglo-Burgundians (Smith and DeVries), about 4,000 Scots and a Dauphinist army two or three times the larger (Burne, Myers), 1,200 to 3,000 Scots dead and over 2,000 taken, Wikipedia; Ramsay has only the 1,000 or so men of the reinforcement brought in May, and some sixteen men of name taken or killed. single (Wikipedia) for the strengths of the two armies; disputed for the losses. "The Anglo-Burgundian army mustered about 4,000 men (2,000 Burgundians and 2,000 English)" (Battle_of_Cravant.wiki, Armies, wikipedia) "Perhaps 1,200–3,000 of them fell at the bridgehead or along the riverbanks, and over 2,000 prisoners were taken" (Battle_of_Cravant.wiki, Battle, wikipedia) "Some  sixteen  gentlemen  of  name  were  taken" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 22, l. 21488, scholarly) "the  greater  part  of  the  Scots,  amounting  to  three  thousand,  who  were  in  the  front" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, the battle of Crevant, l. 35778, primary) "with  several  gentlemen  of  note,  to  the  number  of  four  hundred," (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, the battle of Crevant, l. 35782, primary) "with  many  others,  to  the  amount  of  twelve  hundred" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, the battle of Crevant, l. 35797, primary) "et  quarante  quatre  autres  genfilz" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chronique de la Pucelle (Geste des nobles) ch. 202, l. 7932, primary) "prins  jusques  au  nombre  de  deux  à  (rois" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chronique de la Pucelle ch. 4, Cravant, l. 8899, primary)
- 010.62 Event: Bedford besieges Ivry in summer 1424; the French army, coming to its relief, turns aside and takes Verneuil by a ruse, Scots with bound hands passed off as English prisoners crying out in English as men beaten. agreed for Ivry and the taking of Verneuil; disputed for how it was taken: by that ruse, the Bourgeois, and by ruse, Beaucourt; opened to the Duke of Alençon, whose town it was, by its townsmen, Cagny. "Quant ilz furent pres de Verneil ou Perche, si firent une grant traïson" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §404, 1424, primary) "si  laissèrent  leur  entreprinse  d'aler  à Yvry  et  vindrent  audit  lieu  de  Vernueil" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, the battle of Verneuil, l. 6574, primary) "bourgeois  et  gens  de  la  ville  firent  ouverture  de  la grant  ville  au  duc  d'Alençon" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, the battle of Verneuil, l. 6576, primary) "on marcha vers Verneuil, dont on s'empara par ruse" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 1, l. 604, scholarly)
- 010.63 Battle: Verneuil, Thursday 17 August 1424: Bedford's English (with Salisbury and Suffolk) against the French and Scots under the Duke of Alençon, the Counts of Aumale, Ventadour and Tonnerre, the Viscount of Narbonne, the Constable Buchan and the Earl of Douglas, Duke of Touraine; an English victory that Lang calls "another Agincourt". Both lines and the movements: Oman book 14 ch. 2, l. 19426-19475; Ramsay 1 ch. 23, l. 22296-22455; the Chronique de la Pucelle, l. 9340-9420; the Bourgeois §405, l. 11963-12030. agreed. "le  jeudi  xvij  jour  du  mois d'aoust" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, the battle of Verneuil, l. 6520, primary) "le jeudi d'après la my-aoust qui fut au mardy" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §405, 1424, primary) "The  battle  of  Verneuil  (August  17,  1424) was  another  Agincourt" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1431, scholarly)
- 010.64 Battle: the lines at Verneuil: the English dismounted, men-at-arms in the centre, archers with stakes on the wings, and the horses and baggage laagered behind, tied head to tail three or four deep, under a guard of archers; the French dismounted in one great body, with horse on both wings to ride down the archers, the Lombards (about 500 lances, the Chronique de la Pucelle) on one, 200 to 300 French lances on the other. The Lombards ride round to the baggage, fail to break the hedge of horses and flee with plunder; after some forty minutes (Oman) or three-quarters of an hour (Ramsay) of mêlée the baggage-guard archers fall on the French flank and the line breaks; the slaughter is greatest at the moat of Verneuil. agreed. "The  archers,  with  the  exception of  those  told  off  for  the  baggage-guard,  were  posted  on the  wings,  with  their  stakes" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 23, l. 22320, scholarly) "et fist lier tous les chevaulx de son ost derriere l'ost, les testes devers le cul, III ou IIII d'espès" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §405, 1424, primary) "spécialement  les Lombards  sur  l'une  des  aisles,  qu'on  estimoit  à  environ cinq  cent  hommes" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chronique de la Pucelle, Verneuil, l. 9354, primary) "the two lines of men-at-arms had fought for forty minutes" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 2, l. 19443, scholarly)
- 010.65 Figure: the armies at Verneuil. disputed: the French 18,000 by their own count and the English under 10,000 by the heralds' count, the Bourgeois; the English over 10,000 and the French 7,000 to 8,000, Cagny; the French about 20,000 and the English 14,000, Beaucourt; the English perhaps 2,000 or 3,000 and the French at least half as many again, Ramsay. "qu'ilz estoient bien dix huit mil combatans … qu'ilz n'estoient pas dix mil au plus" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §405, 1424, primary) "le  tout  nombre  à X  mille  combatans  et  pluz … nombrez  de  vij  à  viij  mille" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, the battle of Verneuil, l. 6562, primary) "Une armée, composée d'environ vingt mille combattants … parut à la tête de quatorze mille hommes" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 1, l. 600, scholarly) "may  have  had  2000  or  3000  men" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 23, l. 22309, scholarly)
- 010.66 Figure: the losses at Verneuil. disputed: French dead 7,262 in Bedford's letter written two days after (Ramsay; Beaucourt has it with Wavrin's figures); about 9,000 by the heralds, the Bourgeois; six to seven thousand, the Chronique de la Pucelle; 6,000 to 8,000, Wikipedia; English dead 1,600, Wavrin (in Beaucourt and Ramsay); Ramsay gives the estimates without accepting them, and Oman calls Bedford's 7,000 slain or taken an incredible figure. "gives  the  French loss  as  7262  men" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 23, note, l. 22451, scholarly) "environ seize cents hommes ; les nôtres à plus de sept mille" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 1, l. 605, scholarly) "là furent mors par armes par le dit des heraux bien neuf milliers" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §405, 1424, primary) "jusques  au  nombre  de  six  à  sept  mille" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chronique de la Pucelle, Verneuil, l. 9405, primary) "which seems an incredible figure" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 2, l. 19470, scholarly) "Altogether some 6,000–8,000 men on the French-allied side were slain" (Battle_of_Verneuil.wiki, Casualties, wikipedia)
- 010.67 Person: the dead and the taken at Verneuil: killed, Douglas and his son James, Buchan, Aumale, Ventadour, Tonnerre and Narbonne, whose body Bedford had hanged for his part in the murder of Montereau; taken, the Duke of Alençon and Marshal La Fayette. agreed. "Aumile,  Ventadour,  Tonnerre,  Narbonne,  Douglas,  with James  his  younger  son,  and  Buchan  his  son-in-law,  were among  the  slain" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 23, l. 22413, scholarly) "Et si fut prins le duc d'Alençon" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois §405, 1424, primary) "pource  qu'il  avoit  este  à  la  mort  du  duc  de Bourgongnc" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chronique de la Pucelle, Verneuil, l. 9416, primary)
- 010.68 Event: after Verneuil the King of Bourges can no longer fight in the open and keeps to the defence of his places behind the Loire, Beaucourt; Bedford clears the last Dauphinist garrisons of the north and extends the same slow advance down the line of the Loire, Oman; the Dauphin lingers at Bourges, Chinon and Gien, Lang. agreed. "à défendre pied à pied ses positions" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 1, l. 661, scholarly) "and then to extend the same system down the line of the Loire" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman book 14 ch. 2, l. 19386, scholarly) "at  Bourges,  at  Chinon,  and Gien  on  the  Loire" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 1, l. 1434, scholarly)

# Research for Joan of Arc

## 020 the-road-to-chinon

Coverage: primary (the trial of 1431 in Barrett's and Murray's translations, which are one record and count as one source; the depositions of 1455-1456 in Murray: Laxart, the Le Royers, Jean de Metz, Poulengy, Dunois, Gaucourt, Pasquerel, Seguin; the Journal du siège and the French Continuator of Guillaume de Nangis in Quicherat 4; Monstrelet A; the Bourgeois; Cagny; the Chronique de la Pucelle; the letter of 22 March) and scholarly (Lowell, Lang, Ramsay 1, Oman, Beaucourt 2, Thurston, Champion's sketches in Barrett). The Journal du siège and the Chronique de la Pucelle share their text and count as one account; Monstrelet's Herrings is Wavrin's (Ramsay's note). France's Life was read for where to look and no line rests on it alone: 020.63 cites it beside Lowell and Lang, and its reading of the crossing at Gien is marked single. The Herrings: the Journal, Monstrelet, the Bourgeois, Ramsay and Oman give the laager and the course of the fight, but none read gives the laager's size or a plan of the field; Cagny's editor sets the fight between Janville and Saint-Péravy, and the Wikipedia article reports, without a footnote, S. Cooper's case for Rouvray-Sainte-Croix (Cooper unread). No record of the Poitiers examination survives (Thurston, Lowell); what was said there rests on the witnesses of 1456. How Baudricourt kept Vaucouleurs in July 1428, and why he let her go in February 1429, are not known (Lang, Lowell).

- 020.1 Event: at about thirteen Joan first heard a voice, towards noon in summer, in her father's garden at Domrémy, on her right towards the church, and seldom without a light, as she told her judges on 22 February 1431. single (the trial record; Barrett and Murray translate one record). "declared that at the age of thirteen she had a voice from God to help her and guide her. And the first time she was much afraid. And this voice came towards noon, in summer, in her father's garden … She heard the voice on her right, in the direction of the church; and she seldom heard it without a light." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1162-1166, primary) "The first time that I heard this Voice, I was very much frightened ; it was mid-day, in the summer, in my father's garden." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Part I, second public examination, lines 1416-1419, primary)
- 020.2 Event: the year of the first voices, 1424 or 1425. disputed: Thurston gives the summer of 1425, at thirteen and a half; Lowell finds 1424 in one of her answers and 1425 in another and holds neither exact; Lang says 1425 in one place and "more probably" the summer of 1424 in another. "It was at the age of thirteen and a half, in the summer of 1425, that Joan first became conscious of that manifestation" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 12, scholarly) "before her trial, i. e., 1424 ; at i 52, when she was thirteen years old, i. e., 1425. Neither statement was intended to be an exact one." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 3 note, lines 1389-1390, scholarly) "Voices may be even more probably dated in the summer of 1424" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4, line 2872, scholarly)
- 020.3 Event: what she said the voice bade her: to leave home and go into France, to raise the siege of Orléans, and to go to Robert de Baudricourt at Vaucouleurs, who would give her an escort; her father knew nothing of it. agreed (her answers of 1431; Seguin's account in 1456 of what she told the examiners at Poitiers in 1429; Jean de Metz and Laxart report the same of her in 1429). "this voice told her once or twice a week that she should leave and come to France, and that her father knew nothing of her leaving … the voice told her again that she should raise the siege of the city of Orleans … she, Jeanne, should go to Robert de Baudricourt, in the town of Vaucouleurs of which he was captain, and he would provide an escort for her." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1179-1184, primary) "it is four or five years since my brothers in Paradise and my Lord that is, God told me that I must go and fight in order to regain the kingdom of France … she told me she wished to go into France, to the Dauphin, to have him crowned … there had come to her, while she was minding the cattle, a Voice, which told her that God had great compassion for the people of France, and that she must go into France" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Jean de Metz, Durand Laxart and Seguin de Seguin, lines 11629-11632, 11707-11709, 15321-15324, primary)
- 020.4 Event: a first visit to Baudricourt at Vaucouleurs in May 1428, with her uncle Durand Laxart, after which she went home. disputed: Poulengy, in 1456, puts it about Ascension Day, 13 May 1428 by Murray's note, and has her go home with Laxart; Lang holds to that date; Thurston puts the journey a month after May 1428; Lowell holds a visit in 1428 improbable, notes that she, Laxart and Metz never mention it, and reads Poulengy's Ascension as 1 January 1429. "Jeanne came to Vaucouleurs, I think, about Ascension Day. … That time she went back to her father's house, accompanied by one of her uncles, named Durand Laxart." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Bertrand de Poulengy, lines 11869-11894, primary) "the week of the Ascension of Our Lord (May 1428), Poulengy was with Baudricourt when Jeanne came to him" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 3093-3094, scholarly) "This journey she eventually accomplished a month later, but Baudricourt, a rude and dissolute soldier, treated her and her mission with scant respect" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 14, scholarly) "and can hardly be reconciled with well-established facts. Laxart, Metz, and Joan herself make no mention of a visit in 1428" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4 note, lines 1900-1902, scholarly)
- 020.5 Event: Baudricourt told Laxart many times to take her back to her father and box her ears. single (Laxart, 1456); Lang says it is uncertain whether this was at her first or her second visit. "But many times Robert told me to take her back to her father and to box her ears." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Durand Laxart, lines 11714-11716, primary) "but it is uncertain whether this counsel was given during her first or her second visit to Vaucouleurs." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 3139-3140, scholarly)
- 020.6 Event: her own account: Baudricourt twice refused to hear her and repulsed her, and the third time listened and gave her an escort, as the voice had said he would. single (the trial record). "The said Robert twice refused to hear her and repulsed her; the third time he listened to her and gave her an escort." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1194-1195, primary)
- 020.7 Person: Robert de Baudricourt, squire, captain of Vaucouleurs for Charles VII, later knighted and bailli of Chaumont. agreed (Champion; her own answer; Murray's note). "He was captain of Vaucouleurs at the time of Jeanne d'Arc and later Bailly of Chaumont for King Charles VII" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketches, Robert de Baudricourt, lines 8913-8914, scholarly) "Robert de Baudricourt, Squire, Captain of Vaucouleurs in 1428" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Part I, Murray's note, line 1473, primary)
- 020.8 Border: by midsummer 1428 Vaucouleurs was the one town of the east that held for Charles VII; Champagne to the west, with Troyes and Reims, was Anglo-Burgundian. The source gives a town, not a line. agreed that Baudricourt held Vaucouleurs for Charles VII (Lowell; Lang); single for Vaucouleurs as the one town of the east that held (Lowell) and for Champagne (Lang). "before midsummer, 1428, in the whole east of France, only the town of Vaucouleurs held for Charles VII." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 3, lines 1549-1551, scholarly) "To the west, Champagne, with Troyes and Reims, was Anglo-Burgundian … None the less Robert de Baudricourt held high the flag of France in the castle of Vaucouleurs" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 2, lines 1672-1677, scholarly)
- 020.9 Border: Domrémy, inside the territory of Lorraine, belonged to France as a dependency of Champagne; Neufchâteau, seven miles south (six in Lang), was a walled town of the Duke of Lorraine, then on the English side. agreed (Murray's note; Lang; Lowell). "The village of Domremy, although in the territory of Lorraine, belonged to France, not to Lorraine ; for administrative purposes it was a dependance of Champagne." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions at Domrémy, Murray's note, lines 11873-11875, primary) "Jeanne and Charles VII agreed in regarding the sites of Domremy and Greux as French soil" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 2, lines 1668-1669, scholarly) "miles south to Neufchateau, a walled town belonging to the duke of Lorraine, who was an ally of the English." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 3, lines 1558-1559, scholarly)
- 020.10 Event: in July 1428 Antoine de Vergy, governor of Champagne for the English, marched against Vaucouleurs (his army reviewed at Saint-Urbain on 16-17 July, Lowell; marching about 17-18 July, Lang); the force left within days and Baudricourt kept the town. agreed (Lang; Lowell). How he kept it is open: Lang cites a compact of 22 July for a capitulation and says nothing more is known; Lowell guesses at a conditional surrender. "About July 17 or 18, 1428, the Governor of Champagne, Antoine de Vergy, marched a smaller force than he had expected to raise, for the purpose of reducing the region of Vaucouleurs to the English allegiance. … Vaucouleurs was not surrendered. The force which approached it departed within five days at most." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5 and ch. 7, lines 3162-3165, 3853-3855, scholarly) "At any rate, the Anglo-Burgundian force withdrew, and Baudricourt kept Vaucouleurs for Charles VII. … On July 16-17 Antoine de Vergy passed the attacking army in review at St. Urbain" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 3 and note, lines 1599-1601, 1620-1621, scholarly)
- 020.11 Event: for dread of the Burgundians the people of Domrémy fled with their cattle to Neufchâteau, where Joan's family lodged about a fortnight with an innkeeper called La Rousse. agreed for the flight (her answer of 1431; Lowell; Lang). disputed for the date: Lang and Lowell set it in July 1428, at Vergy's raid, Lowell calling the link "altogether probable" but not certain; Murray's note says no date is certain and gives 1428 or September 1426; later witnesses gave four or five days (Lang). "for dread of the Burgundians she left her father's house and went to the town of Neufchâteau, in Lorraine, to the house of a certain woman called La Rousse, where she stayed about a fortnight." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1151-1153, primary) "There is no certain date for this event. By some it is placed between the first and second visits to Vaucouleurs, in 1428;; by others, earlier, at the time of the Picard ravages of the neighbourhood in the September of 1426." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Part I, Murray's note, lines 1397-1399, primary) "retired a distance of six miles to Neufchateau, in Loraine. The family of Jeanne lodged there with a woman called La Rousse, who kept an inn ; there they dwelt for a fortnight, Jeanne said ; later witnesses said for four or five days." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 3170-3173, scholarly) "It is not certain that the attack on Vaucouleurs was the cause of the flight to Neufchateau, … but it is altogether probable." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 3 note, lines 1578-1609, scholarly)
- 020.12 Person: Thomas Montagu, Earl of Salisbury, Henry VI's lieutenant and head of the English army in France, brings fresh troops in summer 1428. agreed for the man and his command (the Journal; Lowell; Ramsay). disputed for the date: Ramsay has him pass his muster at Sandwich on 19 July and sail at once; Lowell has him land at Calais about 1 July. "le plus renommé eu faictz d'armes de tous les Angloys, et qui pour Henry roy d'Angleterre, dont il estoit parent, et comme son lieutenant et chef de son armée en ce royaulme" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, the opening, lines 4087-4090, primary) "On the 19th July he passed his muster at Sandwich, and immediately after sailed with 450 spears and 2250 bows" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24117-24119, scholarly) "He landed at Calais about July 1, and went to Paris" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3635-3637, scholarly)
- 020.13 Figure: Salisbury's army: 450 spears and 2,250 bows from England, 2,700 men, reinforced from Paris, Chartres and Normandy, Ramsay (from the indentures); four or five thousand at the start of August, about half brought from England, Lowell. single (each author).
