"The 2700 men he brought from England had been reinforced by native auxiliaries drawn from Paris, Chartres, and Normandy" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24229-24231, scholarly) "he marched toward Chartres with four or five thousand soldiers, about half of whom he had brought with him from England," (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3676-3677, scholarly)
- 020.14 Event: Salisbury takes the approaches to Orléans: Janville stormed (29 August, Ramsay; after a week, Lowell), Meung surrendered (entered about 8 September), Beaugency's bridge and castle (25 September), Jargeau taken by Sir John de la Pole (5 October). agreed for the towns (Ramsay; Monstrelet; Lowell); the dates single (Ramsay), Jargeau's agreed with Lowell. "August, after a considerable resistance. From Janville negotiations were opened with the people of Meun on the Loire, below Orleans. They agreed to surrender. … On the 25th September Salisbury made himself master of the bridge and castle of Beaugency … On the 5th October Sir John de la Pole captured Jargeau," (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24173-24183, scholarly) "the earl of Salisbury had subjected the towns of GFergeau, Grenville, Mdioo" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 52, line 38601, primary) "Janville held out but a week. … Meung, six miles farther was Beaugency, both with bridges strongly fortified. … All these places the English occupied in force. … On October 5, Jargeau surrendered to the English." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3708-3833, scholarly)
- 020.15 Figure: the towns Salisbury took before Orléans: thirty-eight, Ramsay, or forty in as many days, Lowell, both from Salisbury's own letter dated Janville, 5 September 1428. single (Salisbury's letter). "no less than eight-and-thirty towns of one sort or another" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, line 24179, scholarly) "Salisbury wrote out a list of forty towns which he had taken in as many days." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3714-3715, scholarly)
- 020.16 Border: by October 1428 the English held the Loire crossings of Meung and Beaugency below Orléans and Jargeau above it; Sully, higher up, surrendered (to the Burgundians, Ramsay; by La Trémoille, its lord, Lowell). Between Sully and Blois the Loire had four bridges: Jargeau, Orléans, Meung, Beaugency. Orléans lies on the north bank. The sources give the towns and bridges, not a line. agreed (Lowell; Ramsay). "Between Sully and Blois there were but four bridges crossing the Loire, viz., at Jargeau, Orleans, Meung, and Beaugency." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7 note, lines 3771-3773, scholarly) "higher still Sully fell into the hands of the Burgundians" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, line 24204, scholarly) "Above Jargeau was Sully, belonging to La Tremoille, wliicli he hastened to surrender" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3735-3736, scholarly)
- 020.17 Event: the siege of Orléans begins on Tuesday 12 October 1428, Salisbury camping on the Sologne (south) side near the suburb of the Portereau. agreed (the Journal; Ramsay; Thurston; Monstrelet has October). "la vint assiéger, le mardy douziesme jour d'octobre mil quatre cens vingt huict, à tout grant ost et armée, qu'il feit loger du coslé de la Sauloigne" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, the opening, lines 4095-4098, primary) "On the 1 2th October the Earl of Salisbury took up his position on the south side." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24228-24229, scholarly) "was invested (12 October, 1428)" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 15, scholarly)
- 020.18 Event: the English take the Tourelles, the fort at the southern end of the bridge, on Sunday 24 October 1428, after the French burned and left its mined boulevard on the 23rd; the bridge between the Tourelles and the town is broken. agreed for the taking (the Journal; Ramsay; Monstrelet; Lowell). disputed for who broke the bridge: the English broke two arches before the Tourelles after taking them, the Journal; the French broke down a span before they withdrew, Lowell. "Le dimenche ensuivant, vingt-quatriesme jour de ce meisme mois d'octobre, assaillirent les Angloys et prindrent les Tournelles du bout du pont" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, October 1428, lines 4227-4229, primary) "On Sunday, 24th October, the English carried … the Tourelles, where they proceeded to establish their chief" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24280-24281, scholarly) "bridge over the Loire to be stormed, which was won" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 52, line 38619, primary) "Et d'autre part rompirent les Anglois deux archesdupont" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, October 1428, lines 4260-4261, primary) "Before withdrawing, however, the French broke down a span of the bridge between the Tourelles and the town" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3871-3873, scholarly)
- 020.19 Event: Salisbury, looking at the city from a window of the Tourelles, is struck in the face by a cannon shot, is carried to Meung and dies there. agreed for the wound and the place of death (the Journal; Ramsay; Monstrelet; Lowell). disputed for the dates: the Journal has him struck on the evening of Sunday 24 October and dead on the night of Wednesday 27 October; Ramsay, after the Chronique de la Pucelle, has him struck on 27 October and dead on 3 November; Monstrelet has him struck on the third day after his arrival and living eight days; Lowell, after the Journal, has him die three days after the wound. "Celluy jour de dimenche au soir, voult le conte de Salebris … Le mercredy ensuyvant , vingt-septiesme jour … trespassa de nuict le conte de Salebris en la ville de Meung sur Loire" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, October 1428, lines 4241-4295, primary) "who was struck in the face by a splinter from a gun-stone while reconnoitring from the window of an upper chamber in the Tourelles … Salisbury was apparently struck 27th October and died 3rd November;" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26 and note, lines 24286-24338, scholarly) "The earl, on the third day after hia arrival before Orleans, entered the tower on the bridge … he was carried to Mehun, and there died" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 52, lines 38640-38650, primary) "he was struck in the head by a cannon ball, and was wounded so severely that in three days he died." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3878-3880, scholarly)
- 020.20 Person: William de la Pole, Earl of Suffolk, takes command before Orléans after Salisbury. agreed (Ramsay; Monstrelet); Lowell has the command divided in late November between Suffolk, Lord Scales and Lord Talbot. "the Regent having given the chief command to Suffolk, with orders to persevere" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24317-24318, scholarly) "The earl of Suffolk was now the commander of the English army before Orleans" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 52, line 38653, primary) "the question of the command of the English forces was settled by dividing it among three generals," (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3930-3932, scholarly)
- 020.21 Person: Jean, Bastard of Orléans (later Count of Dunois), comes into Orléans with reinforcements on 25 October 1428 and holds the chief command there as the king's lieutenant for the war. agreed for the command (his own deposition; Ramsay; Lowell). single for his coming on 25 October (the Journal); Ramsay has him in command inside the city at the English approach, and Lowell has him succeed Gaucourt, hurt by a fall, in November or December. "I was then entrusted with the care of the town of Orleans and was Lieutenant-General of the King in affairs of war." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Dunois, lines 11979-11981, primary) "arrivèrent dedens Orléans pour la conforter, secourir et ayder, plusieurs nobles seigneurs, … tard d'Orléans, le seigneur de Saincte-Sevère" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 25 October 1428, lines 4266-4279, primary) "Inside the city the Bastard of Orleans held the chief" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, line 24258, scholarly) "and Gaucourt, who had been injured by a fall, … in his command by John, Bastard of Orleans" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3898-3910, scholarly)
- 020.22 Event: through the winter the English ring Orléans with fortified posts (bastilles): the main army comes up from Beaugency on 29 December, headquarters in a fortified camp west of the city; seven works on the north bank, four on the south with the Tourelles, the north-east left open (Ramsay). agreed (Ramsay; Monstrelet; Lowell). "On the 29th December the main army came up from Beaugency … were constructed on the north bank, four having been constructed on the south side … works only covered the West and South, leaving one half of the town open on the North-East" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24322-24334, scholarly) "They also erected block-houses in various parts, in which large detachments were posted" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 52, lines 38656-38657, primary) "fortified camp, west of Orleans. It was about a gunshot from the walls" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3972-3973, scholarly)
- 020.23 Battle: the Herrings (Rouvray), Saturday 12 February 1429, the eve of the first Sunday in Lent, at Rouvray-Saint-Denis in the Beauce, two leagues from Janville: Sir John Fastolf, bringing a convoy of Lenten food and war stores from Paris to the army before Orléans, forms a park of his wagons and beats off the French and Scots of the Count of Clermont and John Stewart of Darnley; English victory. agreed (the Journal; Ramsay; Oman; Monstrelet; the Bourgeois). Monstrelet sets the fight in the night of the first Sunday in Lent; Cagny misdates it to March (his editor corrects to 12 February) and puts it between Janville and Saint-Péravy. Positions and movements of both sides: the Journal du siège (Quicherat 4, lines 5030-5252), Monstrelet A book 2 ch. 56 (38840-38927), the Bourgeois (13592-13640), Ramsay 1 ch. 26 (24366-24424), Oman book 14 ch. 2 (19476-19494); Lang ch. 6 (4312-4386) follows the Journal. "en Beausse, à un villaige nommé Rouvroy de Saint-Denis … ziesme jour de février, veille des brandons, messire Jehan Fascot" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, February 1429, lines 5051-5060, primary) "Darnley was left on the field with Guillaume D'Albret and other French gentlemen of rank (12th February)." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24418-24420, scholarly) "(February 12, 1429), the last English victory" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, book 14 ch. 2, lines 19476-19477, scholarly) "This battle of Bonvroy was fonght on the night of the first Sonday in Iai" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 56, line 38918, primary)
- 020.24 Battle: the English order at the Herrings: a park of carts and pointed stakes; one long narrow entry (the Journal) or two openings guarded by archers with the men-at-arms behind (Monstrelet); archers and crossbowmen outside on the two flanks, the Parisians on one and the English on the other, the main body in the middle (the Bourgeois; Ramsay); archers on the carts and spearmen in the gaps (Oman). disputed in detail; agreed for the wagon park and the stakes. "feirent ung parc de leur charroy et de paulx aguz … longue et estroicte issue ou entrée" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, February 1429, lines 5072-5074, primary) "forming a square veith their carts and carriages, leaving but two openings," (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 56, lines 38863-38864, primary) "furent mis les archiers et arbalestiers de Paris à ung costé, ausquelx fut ordonné une elle de noz gens et l'autre elle fut des archiers angloys, et ou millieu fut ce qu'ilz povoient avoir de grosse bataille" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, 1429, lines 13608-13610, primary) "the cross-bowmen and archers were posted outside, the French on one flank, the English on the other" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24385-24387, scholarly) "The archers got upon the carts, the French spearmen held the intervals between them." (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, book 14 ch. 2, lines 19488-19489, scholarly)
- 020.25 Battle: the course of the Herrings: the French agreed to fight mounted, only the archers on foot (and to let their guns play, Ramsay); Clermont forbade the van from Orléans to attack before he came up; the Constable of Scotland dismounted against the order and attacked with some four hundred; the English sallied from the park and routed the men on foot while Clermont's main body held back and then rode off to Orléans; the Bastard of Orléans was wounded in the foot and carried off. agreed (the Journal; Ramsay; Monstrelet; Lowell). "qu'il despeça à toute force l'ordonnance qui avoit esté faicte de tous, que nul ne descendis! … ilz saillirent hastivement de leur parc, et frappèrent dedans les François estans à piet" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, February 1429, lines 5148-5170, primary) "Dismounting, they pressed on in front of their line … then the English men-at-arms sallied … cavalry retired, carrying off the Bastard severely wounded." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24396-24416, scholarly) "the constable of Scotland, his son and all their men, dismounted and advanced to attack their adversaries" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 56, lines 38877-38878, primary)
- 020.26 Figure: strengths at the Herrings. disputed. French: three to four thousand, the Journal and Monstrelet; four thousand and more, Cagny; seven thousand, the Bourgeois; four times the English, Oman. English: 1,500, the Journal and the Bourgeois; 1,600 combatants and a thousand common men, of whom not more than six hundred real Englishmen, Monstrelet; two thousand and more, Cagny; a thousand archers and 1,200 Paris militia, Oman. Carts: about three hundred, the Journal; about five hundred, Monstrelet. "vèrent de trois à quatre mille combatans … accompaignez de quinze cens combatans, tant Angloys, Picards, Normans , que autres gens de divers pays, amenoient environ trois cens que chariotz et charrettes" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, February 1429, lines 5054-5066, primary) "collected about five hundred carts and … accompanied by sixteen hundred combatants and a thousand common men … from three to four … not more than six hundred real Englishmen" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 56, lines 38844-38872, primary) "car ilz n'estoient en tout pas plus de XVc contre VIIm, qui estoient XIII Arminalx contre deux de noz gens" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, 1429, lines 13611-13612, primary) "acompaigniez de ij mille coinbatans Enj^lois … acompaigniés de iiij mille combatans et mieulx" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1429, lines 6787-6791, primary) "(ten hundred archers and twelve hundred Paris militia, fighting on the English side) … Beset by four times their own number of men-at-arms" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, book 14 ch. 2, lines 19479-19486, scholarly)
- 020.27 Figure: losses at the Herrings. disputed. French dead: three to four hundred, the Journal; six score gentlemen and five hundred common men, most of them Scots, Monstrelet. Among the dead John Stewart of Darnley, Constable of the Scots, with his kin (his brother William, the Journal; his son, Monstrelet), and Guillaume d'Albret, lord of Orval (both). English: one man of note, Monstrelet. "car il y mourut de trois à quatre cens combatans françois." (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, February 1429, lines 5171-5172, primary) "Among the killed were, sir John Stuart, his son, sir William d'Albreth lord d'Orval … to the amount of six score gentlemen and five hundred common men, the greater part of whom were Scotsmen. … The English lost only one man of note" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 56, lines 38884-38919, primary)
- 020.28 Person: Sir John Fastolf, steward (grand master) of the Regent Bedford's household, commands the convoy. agreed (Ramsay; Monstrelet; the Journal names him chief). "under the charge of Sir John Fastolf, Steward of the Regent's Household" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24367-24368, scholarly) "the command of this convoy was given to sir John Fascot* grand^master of the duke's household" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 56, lines 38847-38848, primary)
- 020.29 Person: Charles de Bourbon, Count of Clermont, son of the captive Duke of Bourbon, leads the relief force from Blois and is knighted on the day of the battle; Sir John Stewart of Darnley, Constable of the Scots in France after the Earl of Buchan, leads the Scots. agreed (Ramsay; the Journal; Champion). "bon), having a force at Blois for the relief of Orleans … A Scottish contingent, under Sir John Stewart of Darnley, formed part of the force. Sir John had succeeded the Earl of Buchan as Constable of the Scots in France." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24370-24377, scholarly) "Le conte de Clermont , qui ce jour avoit esté faict chevallier" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, February 1429, lines 5216-5217, primary) "Charles de Bourbon, comte de Clermont, and later duc de Bourbonnais, fought the Battle of the Herrings." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketches, Charles de Bourbon, lines 9030-9031, scholarly)
- 020.30 Event: after the Herrings Clermont withdraws to his estates, and the king's council weighs a retreat to the Dauphiné if Orléans falls. agreed (the Journal; Ramsay; Lowell). "le meilleur estoit qu'il se retirast au Daulphiné" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, February 1429, lines 5318-5319, primary) "of the Herrings, Clermont retired in disgust to his estates. … gloomy eventualities were discussed at Court ; retirement to Dauphin^, to Spain, possibly even to Scotland" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, lines 24441-24446, scholarly) "discussing whether Charles had better take refuge in Dauphiny or in Languedoc" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 2532-2533, scholarly)
- 020.31 Event: the story that Joan told Baudricourt of the defeat on the day it happened. disputed: the Journal (and the Chronique de la Pucelle, which shares its text) tells it; Lowell finds the evidence insufficient, though Baudricourt came to believe it; Lang notes that it stands only in chronicles of about 1467; Thurston dates her announcement 17 February. "Cestuy propre jour aussi , sceut Jehanne la Pucelle , par grâce divine, ceste desconfilure" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, February 1429, lines 5254-5255, primary) "dence that Joan really announced the defeat to Baudricourt is quite insufficient, but there can be little doubt that he afterwards believed she had done so." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4 note, lines 2279-2281, scholarly) "Of course the story of the vision about Rouvray is given in chronicles very late, about 1467, and is far from being matter of certain history." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, notes, lines 14751-14753, scholarly) "On 17 February she announced a great defeat which had befallen the French arms outside" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 16, scholarly)
- 020.32 Event: she comes back to Vaucouleurs in the winter of 1428-1429, brought by Laxart, and lodges with Henri Le Royer, a cartwright, and his wife Catherine, about three weeks in all. agreed (Catherine and Henri Le Royer; Lowell; Lang; Thurston). For the dates Lang has her at Laxart's house at Burey from the first fortnight of January and at the Le Royers' from the first week of February; Poulengy has her come back towards the beginning of Lent; Thurston has her leave Domrémy in January 1429. "Jeanne, when she had left her parents, was brought to our house at Vaucouleurs by Durand Laxart, her uncle … She lived with us at Vaucouleurs, at different times about three weeks. … Jeanne, when she came to Vaucouleurs, lodged in our house." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Catherine and Henri Le Royer, lines 11756-11797, primary) "Arriving at Little Burey in the first fortnight of January 1429, Jeanne seems to have stayed there for three weeks (Lassois, in 1456, said for six weeks), and gone to the house of the Royers in Vaucouleurs in the first week of February." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3571-3574, scholarly) "This time, also, Laxart went with her, and found her lodgings with Catherine le Royer" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4, lines 1920-1921, scholarly) "Yielding at last, she left Domremy in January, 1429, and again visited Vaucouleurs." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 15, scholarly)
- 020.33 Event: Baudricourt comes to the Le Royers' house with the curé Jean Fournier, who exorcises her. single (Catherine Le Royer, who had it from Joan); Lowell tells it from her. "If you are an evil spirit, avaunt ! If you are a good spirit, approach !" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Catherine Le Royer, lines 11772-11773, primary) "he walked into the house, accompanied by the curate, John Fournier." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4, lines 1948-1949, scholarly)
- 020.34 Event: Duke Charles II of Lorraine sends her a safe-conduct and has her brought to Nancy; he asks her about his health; she asks him for his son (his son-in-law René, Lowell) and men to take her to France; he gives her four francs; she returns to Vaucouleurs. agreed (her answers of 1431; Laxart; Poulengy; Lowell; Champion). "the duke of Lorraine ordered that she should be taken to him; and she went to him and told him she wished to go to France. And the duke questioned her about the recovery of his health" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1196-1198, primary) "went with a safe-conduct to the Sieur Chafles de Lorraine. The Duke saw her, spoke to her, and gave her four francs, … she went to seek the Lord Duke de Lorraine, who had sent her a safe-conduct and asked to see her. She then returned to Vaucouleurs and lodged in the house of Henry Leroyer." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Durand Laxart and Bertrand de Poulengy, lines 11720-11722, 11899-11902, primary) "she set out from Vaucouleurs for Nancy, the capital of Lorraine, to ask the duke's help." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4, lines 2028-2030, scholarly) "Charles II had Jeanne brought to Nancy." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketches, Charles II, line 8921, scholarly)
- 020.35 Event: on that journey she made a pilgrimage to Saint-Nicolas-de-Port, near Nancy. agreed (Catherine Le Royer and Poulengy, with Murray's note identifying the shrine; Lowell). "Jacques Alain and Durand Laxart took her to Saint … After a pilgrimage to Saint" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Catherine Le Royer and Bertrand de Poulengy, lines 11782-11898, primary) "the pilgrimage was an incident of the journey." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4 note, line 2043, scholarly)
- 020.36 Event: Jean de Metz finds her at the Le Royers' and pledges to take her to the king; he and Bertrand de Poulengy offer to escort her and pay for the journey, for which the king's treasury repaid them. agreed (Metz; Poulengy; Champion; Lowell). "The journey was made at the expense of Bertrand de Poulengey and myself. … Then Jean de Metz and I offered to conduct her to the King" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Jean de Metz and Bertrand de Poulengy, lines 11620-11898, primary) "It was he who discovered Jeanne, when she was dressed in a poor red dress, and when she was lodged in the house of Henri le Royer." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketches, Jean de Nouvilonpont, lines 8960-8962, scholarly) "he grasped her hand and swore on his honor that, with God as their leader, he would take her to the king." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4, lines 1986-1987, scholarly) "The expense of the journey was borne by Metz and Poulengy, who were afterwards repaid out of the royal treasury." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 2373-2375, scholarly)
- 020.37 Person: Jean de Nouillonpont, called Jean de Metz, squire, born 1398, and Bertrand de Poulengy, squire, born 1392, the two men of Vaucouleurs who escort her to Chinon. agreed (Champion; Lowell). "was also called Jean de Metz, squire. … Bertrand de Poulengy was the squire who accompanied Jeanne to Chinon." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketches, Jean de Nouvilonpont and Bertrand de Poulengy, lines 8960-8985, scholarly) "He was born in 1398, … He was born in 1392." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4 notes, lines 1993-2035, scholarly)
- 020.38 Event: the people of Vaucouleurs buy her men's clothes and equipment, and she is given a horse. agreed for the clothes (Laxart; Metz; Henri Le Royer; Poulengy; Lowell; Lang). disputed for the horse: twelve francs paid by Laxart and Jacques Alain and repaid by Baudricourt, Laxart; about sixteen francs, Jean de Metz; a black horse from the Duke of Lorraine, Morel by hearsay (Champion), which Lowell doubts. "I also procured for her a horse at the price of about sixteen francs … the inhabitants bought for her a man's garments and a complete warlike equipment. Alain de Vaucouleurs and I bought her a horse for the price of twelve francs" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Jean de Metz and Durand Laxart, lines 11599-11726, primary) "a horse bought for Jeanne at Vaucouleurs cost, by one account twelve, by another, sixteen livres" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, lines 4936-4937, scholarly) "the people of Vaucouleurs, joining together, had bought for Joan men's clothes suited to her journey" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4, lines 2209-2211, scholarly) "and that he gave her a black horse (Jean Morel's deposition)" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketches, Charles II, lines 8932-8933, scholarly)
- 020.39 Event: Baudricourt at last gives way, though with doubt: he gives her a sword, makes her escort swear to take her well and safely, and sends her off with "Go, come what may". agreed (her answers of 1431; Lowell; Lang). "carried a sword which Robert de Baudricourt had given her … Robert de Baudricourt had sworn those who accompanied her to conduct her well and safely" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1203-1213, primary) "but he decided to try the experiment, and yielded, though with doubt and reluctance." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4, lines 2206-2207, scholarly) "Baudricourt gave the Maid a sword, and said, Allez, et vienne que pourrai" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3889-3890, scholarly)
- 020.40 Event: why Baudricourt yielded. open: Lowell says she then told him something of her visions and that what she said is not known; Lang weighs the news of Rouvray and the exorcism and leaves the motives open. "she told him at this time something of her visions and of her voices; just what she said we do not know." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4, lines 2203-2205, scholarly) "Whatever motives may have overcome Baudricourt's sense of the ridiculous" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, line 3871, scholarly)
- 020.41 Event: she rides out of Vaucouleurs by the Gate of France in men's clothes with six men: Jean de Metz and Bertrand de Poulengy, their servants Julien and Jean de Honnecourt, Colet de Vienne, a king's messenger, and Richard the Archer. agreed (her answers: a knight, a squire and four servants; Henri Le Royer and Poulengy; Lang; Beaucourt). Thurston gives three men-at-arms and Monstrelet four to six men. "on her departure from Vaucouleurs, she wore the habit of a man … accompanied by a knight, a squire, and four servants" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1202-1204, primary) "I saw them depart, all six, and Jeanne with them … Then we started with her to seek the Dauphin, together with Julian, my servant, Jean de Honecourt, servant of Jean de Metz, Colet de Vienne, and Richard the Archer." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Henri Le Royer and Bertrand de Poulengy, lines 11808-11909, primary) "the king's messenger, Colet de Vienne, rode out of the Gate of France" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3888-3889, scholarly) "escortée par six jeunes compagnons" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 5, line 10942, scholarly) "with a slender escort of three men-at-arms" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 16, scholarly) "supplied her with horses and from four to six men as an escort" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 57, line 38940, primary)
- 020.42 Event: the day she left Vaucouleurs. disputed: Lowell sets it at or about 12 February 1429, the day of the Herrings, after the clerk of La Rochelle and Boismarmin; Jean de Metz has them back from Nancy about the first Sunday in Lent (13 February) and then setting out; Lang has 23 February and says the critical historians make it 13 February; the Journal tells her setting out among the events of the day of the Herrings. "fixes Joan's departure from Vaucouleurs at or about February 12." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 4 note, lines 2220-2221, scholarly) "On the return to Vaucouleurs, the first Sunday in Lent," (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Jean de Metz, lines 11613-11614, primary) "On February 23 he was one of the little band that started with Jeanne from the Gate of France … while critical historians make Jeanne leave Vaucouleurs on February 13, and arrive at Chinon on March 6" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7 and notes, lines 3824-14757, scholarly)
- 020.43 Event: the day she reached Chinon. disputed: 23 February 1429, Lowell, after the Livre Noir of La Rochelle and Boismarmin; 6 March, the French Continuator of Guillaume de Nangis, the one chronicle that gives the day outright, followed by Quicherat, Lang and Thurston; Beaucourt has the news of her arrival reach the castle about noon on 8 March. "gives the date as February 23 ; and I agree with M. de Boismarmin … that the earlier date is the more probable." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5 note, lines 2856-2859, scholarly) "la date du jour où la Pucelle arriva à Chinon … le 6e jour de mars , la Pucelle vint au roy" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Continuateur français de Guillaume de Nangis, p. 313, primary) "Jeanne and her company occupied eleven days, (February 23-March 6) on the march from Vaucouleurs to Chinon." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 7, lines 3828-3829, scholarly) "She reached Chinon on 6 March, and two days later was admitted into the presence of Charles VII." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 17, scholarly) "Le 8 mars 1429, vers midi, on apprenait au château de Chinon, où résidait Charles VII, qu'une jeune fille des marches de Lorraine venait d'arriver" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 5, lines 10930-10932, scholarly)
- 020.44 Figure: the journey from Vaucouleurs to Chinon took eleven days, by her two escorts; Lang notes that Metz's date of leaving with the La Rochelle date of arrival would give ten. agreed (Jean de Metz; Poulengy). "We journeyed eleven days, always riding towards the said town of Chinon. … We were eleven days on the road" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Jean de Metz and Bertrand de Poulengy, lines 11624-11927, primary) "The duration of the journey is thus ten, not eleven days" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, notes, lines 14761-14762, scholarly)
- 020.45 Event: they rode mostly by night, and all the first night, for fear of the Burgundians and the English who held the roads, avoided inns, and heard mass only twice on the way. agreed (Jean de Metz and Poulengy, two witnesses; Lowell). "We travelled for the most part at night, for fear of the Burgundians and the English, who were masters of the roads. … we were only able to hear it twice. … On starting, the first day, fearing to be taken by the Burgundians and the English, we travelled all night" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Jean de Metz and Bertrand de Poulengy, lines 11622-11911, primary) "Often they avoided the inns and slept in the fields" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 2365-2366, scholarly)
- 020.46 Border: the first half of the road, to the Loire, ran through country that acknowledged Henry VI: Auxerre was a Burgundian city; Gien, on the Loire about forty miles above Orléans, held for Charles VII. The sources give towns, not a line. agreed (Lowell; Lang). "the first half of the road lay through a country which acknowledged Henry as king. … On Friday or thereabouts they reached Gien, a town on the Loire about forty miles above Orleans, holding for Charles" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 2319-2404, scholarly) "they had to ford four or five rivers before they reached Auxerre, in Anglo-Burgundian territory, where they heard Mass. Soon they were at Gien, in the Dauphin's country" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, lines 4004-4007, scholarly)
- 020.47 Event: the halts: the first night at the abbey of Saint-Urbain on the Marne; mass in the principal church of Auxerre; Gien, from where word of her spread to Orléans. agreed (her answers of 1431; Dunois; Lowell; Lang). "she reached the town of Saint Urbain, where she slept in an abbey. She said that on her journey she passed through Auxerre, and she heard Mass in the principal church there" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1204-1207, primary) "a young girl, commonly called the Maid, had just passed through Gien, going to the noble Dauphin" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Dunois, lines 11975-11977, primary) "abbey of St. Urbain, on the river Marne." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, line 2339, scholarly) "Their first night march brought them to the town of St. Urbain." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, line 3997, scholarly)
- 020.48 Event: at Sainte-Catherine-de-Fierbois she heard mass three times in one day and sent a letter to the king asking leave to come to him, saying she had ridden a hundred and fifty leagues to help him and would know him among all others. agreed (her answers of 1431; Lowell; Beaucourt; Lang). "Asked if she had been to Ste. Catherine de Fierbois, she answered yes; and there she heard Masses three times on the same day; and then went to Chinon. She said she sent letters to her king" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 27 February 1431, lines 1592-1594, primary) "There they halted, not daring to bring Joan to court until leave should be had of the king." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 2453-2455, scholarly) "Catherine-de-Fierbois, cette fille avait écrit au Roi pour lui demander la permission de se rendre auprès de lui" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 5, lines 10934-10935, scholarly)
- 020.49 Event: she reached Chinon towards noon and lodged at an inn (her account); the king kept her waiting two days before he received her (Dunois's envoys; Thurston; two or three days, Lowell). agreed for the wait (Dunois, and Simon Charles as Lowell cites him; Thurston; Lowell), though her own account at her trial goes on from the inn to the king "after dinner" and names no wait. "She reached Chinon towards noon and lodged at an inn; and after dinner she went to him whom she calls king, who was at the castle." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1227-1228, primary) "They said that the King at first had no wish to listen to her: she even remained two" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Dunois, lines 11989-11990, primary) "a time for the audience, some two or three days after her arrival." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 2631-2632, scholarly) "It was two days before she saw the king [see P. iii. 4, Bastard ; 115, Simon Charles]" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5 note, lines 2865-2867, scholarly)
- 020.50 Event: the audience in the castle of Chinon: Louis de Bourbon, Count of Vendôme, brought her in; she knew the king among many, by the counsel of her voice, she said, though some of his men played the king; she told him she came from God to help him and the kingdom. agreed (her answers of 1431; Gaucourt, who was present; Pasquerel at second hand; the Journal; Lang). "when she entered her king's room she recognized him among many others by the counsel of her voice, which revealed him to her. She told him she wanted to make war on the English." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1229-1231, primary) "herself before the King's Majesty with great lowliness and simplicity … It was the Sieur Count de Venddme.who brought her into the King's apartment." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Gaucourt and Pasquerel, lines 12418-14287, primary) "et le congneut entre ses gens, combien que plusieurs d'eulx faignoient, la cuidant abuser, estre le roy" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, the coming of the Maid, lines 5328-5329, primary) "Louis de Bourbon, Comte de Vendome, led Jeanne into the Royal presence." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, lines 4091-4092, scholarly)
- 020.51 Event: the sign she gave the king. open: she said at her trial that her king had a sign before he believed her and would not say more; Pasquerel had from her that she told him he was the true heir of France; Thurston says she never revealed it and gives the common belief that it touched his doubt of his own birth. "her king had a sign touching of her mission before he believed in her." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 27 February 1431, lines 1590-1591, primary) "On the part of My Lord, I tell thee thou art true heir of France and son of the King … All this I heard from Jeanne, but without having been witness of it." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Pasquerel, lines 14305-14314, primary) "What this sign was, Joan never revealed" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 17, scholarly)
- 020.52 Event: churchmen and doctors examine her at Poitiers, where she lodges with Jean Rabateau, before the king's council with the Archbishop of Reims, the chancellor; they find nothing in her contrary to the faith and advise that the king, given his need, may use her and send her to Orléans. agreed (Pasquerel; Seguin, an examiner; Gaucourt; Beaucourt; Thurston; Lowell). "came to the conclusion that, in view of the necessity which weighed upon the Kingdom, the King might make use of her aid, and that they had found nothing in her contrary to the Catholic Faith. … We were sent to question her at the house of Mattre Jean Rabateau, where she was lodging." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions of Pasquerel and Seguin, lines 14269-15315, primary) "résulla cette déclaration : qu'on n'avait rien trouvé de mal en la Pucelle" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 5, lines 11358-11359, scholarly) "The theologians found nothing heretical in her claims to supernatural guidance" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 18, scholarly)
- 020.53 Event: when and how long. disputed: sent to Poitiers about 10 March (Lowell); examined three weeks at Chinon and Poitiers (Gaucourt; more than three weeks, Beaucourt; three weeks or a month, Dunois); the doctors' written opinion about 6 April (Lowell) or about 17-20 April (Lang). No minutes of the examination survive (Thurston). "Thither Joan was sent, accordingly, about March 10 … was written six weeks after Joan's arrival at Chinon, and therefore about April 6." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5 and ch. 6 note, lines 2847-3516, scholarly) "Her deeds and words were examined during three weeks, not only at Chinon, but at Poitiers." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Gaucourt, lines 12430-12432, primary) "it was issued, apparently, about April 17-20." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, line 4850, scholarly) "It is regrettable in the extreme that the minutes of the proceedings, to which Joan frequently appealed later on at her trial, have altogether perished." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 18, scholarly)
- 020.54 Event: her letter summoning the King of England, Bedford and the captains before Orléans to leave France, dated the Tuesday of Holy Week, 22 March 1429, at Poitiers; at her trial she owned it, save two or three words. agreed (the letter; Lowell; Lang; her answers). "Done on the Tuesday of Holy Week (March 22, 1429)." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-halsall-source-joanofarc.asp.txt, the letter, line 57, primary) "The letter to the English was written from Poitiers on March 22." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5 note, line 2861, scholarly) "A letter of this kind is dated March 22," (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, line 4724, scholarly) "she sent to the English at Orleans letters telling them to depart, as shown in the copy of the letters which had been read to her in this town of Rouen, except two or three words in the copy" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1220-1222, primary)
- 020.55 Event: at Tours, from about the middle of April, she lodges with Jean Dupuy and is given armour and a household: Jean d'Aulon her squire, the pages Louis de Coutes and Raymond, the Austin friar Jean Pasquerel her confessor. agreed (Pasquerel; Lowell; Lang). Lang gives her armour as white, at a hundred livres tournois; Gaucourt says she armed herself first at Blois. "then to Tours, in which town I was at that time Reader in a Convent ; and there we found her lodging in the house of a citizen named Jean Dupuy," (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Pasquerel, lines 14222-14224, primary) "About the middle of April, Joan left the abbey and went to Tours … here provided with a military household befitting the position she was about to take. … John of Anion, a discreet young man, became her squire." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6, lines 3271-3279, scholarly) "Jeanne was now accepted, and was sent to Tours, while arms were prepared for her, and a Household was appointed to attend on her. … of Jeanne, as smaller, cost less, a hundred livres tournois" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9 and ch. 10, lines 4852-4934, scholarly)
- 020.56 Event: her banner is painted at Tours (the war treasurer's account pays a painter of Tours 25 livres tournois for a great standard and a small one, Murray's note). agreed for the banner made at Tours (Pasquerel; Murray's note from the account). disputed for its design: a field sown with lilies, the world and two angels, with Jhesus Maria at the side, by her own account; Christ in judgment on the clouds and an angel with a fleur-de-lys, by Pasquerel; God the Father with kneeling angels presenting a fleur-de-lys, Thurston; Lowell thinks she had more than one. "It was for this she had her banner made, on which was painted the image of Our Saviour seated in judgment on the clouds of Heaven, with an Angel holding in his hand a fleur-de-lys which Christ was blessing. I was at Tours with her when this banner was painted," (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Pasquerel, lines 14322-14327, primary) "she answered she had a banner, with a field sown with lilies; the world was depicted on it, and two angels, one at each" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 27 February 1431, lines 1636-1637, primary) "The accounts of her banner vary considerably; probably she had more than one." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6 note, lines 3444-3445, scholarly) "There was made for her at the same time a standard bearing the words Jesus, Maria, with a picture of God the Father, and kneeling angels presenting a fleur-de-lis." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 19, scholarly)
- 020.57 Event: while at Tours or Chinon she sent for a sword in the church of Sainte-Catherine-de-Fierbois, behind the altar; it was found rusty, with five crosses on it, and an armourer of Tours fetched it. agreed for the finding (her answers of 1431; Lowell; Lang; Thurston). disputed for how she knew: through her voices, she said, as Thurston holds; Lowell thinks she probably saw or heard of the chest of votive arms when she heard mass there; Lang says no more is known than what she told her judges. "was at Tours or Chinon she sent for a sword which was in the church of Ste. Catherine de Fierbois, behind the altar; and immediately it was found there all rusted over. … a merchant, an armorer of Tours, fetched it." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 27 February 1431, lines 1600-1608, primary) "bly saw or at least heard of the old chest with its rusty contents" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6, lines 3371-3372, scholarly) "As to the famous mystic sword of the Maid, we really know no more than she told her judges in 1431." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, lines 4975-4976, scholarly) "It was found in the very spot her voices indicated." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, paragraph 19, scholarly)
- 020.58 Event: after about ten days at Tours she goes up the Loire to Blois with the Archbishop of Reims and Gaucourt, where the convoy for Orléans gathers under the marshals de Rais and de Boussac, the admiral de Culant and La Hire; the force leaves Blois and comes opposite Orléans by the south bank. agreed (Dunois; Lowell; Lang). disputed for the day: opposite Orléans on 28 April, Lang; out of Blois on the morning of Thursday 28 April, a night in the fields, and on the heights of Olivet about noon on Friday 29 April, Lowell. "the King sent her with the Lord Archbishop of Rheims … and the Sieur de Gaucourt, then Grand Steward, to Blois, where those were who had the charge of escorting the convoy" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Dunois, lines 12025-12029, primary) "After staying about ten days at Tours, Joan went up the Loire to Blois, where the troops had their rendezvous," (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6, lines 3451-3452, scholarly) "When they left Blois, the clergy went in advance, singing Veni creator spiritus. On April 28 this strange force, with a convoy of cattle, arrived opposite Orleans by the south bank of the Loire" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, lines 5227-5230, scholarly) "On the morning of Thursday, April 28, … about Friday noon Joan came upon the heights of Olivet," (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, lines 4266-4278, scholarly)
- 020.59 Person: Regnault de Chartres, Archbishop of Reims and Chancellor of France, of La Trémoille's party: witness of her first interview with the king, one of her examiners, sent to Blois to direct the relief. agreed (Champion; Lowell; Seguin names him at Poitiers). "He was a witness of the decisive interview of the King and Jeanne, and was one of her examiners, was sent to Blois to direct the relief of Orléans." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketches, Regnault de Chartres, lines 9138-9139, scholarly) "Regnault of Chartres, archbishop of Rheims and chan- cellor of France" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 2501-2502, scholarly)
- 020.60 Person: Yolande of Aragon, Queen of Sicily, the Dauphin's mother-in-law, who then held Tours (Lang), and who advanced money for Orléans and furnished provisions for the army (Lowell). Her examination of Joan: single (each author, from the witnesses of 1456), and the place differs: at Tours, later, in a jury of ladies who vouched for Joan's maidenhood, Lang; at Poitiers, where she examined Joan herself and reported to the council, Lowell. "by a jury of illustrious ladies, including the Queen of Sicily, mother-in-law of the Dauphin." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, lines 4769-4770, scholarly) "To the city of Tours, then held by the Queen of Sicily," (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, line 4923, scholarly) "was come to Poitiers. She examined Joan herself, and made her report to the council, … She had already advanced money to help the defense of Orleans. … She furnished provisions for the army." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6 and notes, lines 3066-3081, scholarly)
- 020.61 Person: Durand Laxart of Burey-le-Petit, who had married the daughter of a sister of her mother (Lang), called her uncle by her and by the witnesses, who took her to Vaucouleurs. agreed (his deposition; her answers; Lang). "Jeanne was of the family of Jeanne, my wife." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Durand Laxart, line 11689, primary) "She said she went to an uncle of hers, and told him she wanted to stay with him for some time" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 22 February 1431, lines 1186-1187, primary) "Lassois had married the daughter of a sister of Jeanne's mother" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 3040-3041, scholarly)
- 020.62 Border: Vaucouleurs, the walled town held for the Dauphin by Robert de Baudricourt, stands on the Meuse downstream (north) of Domrémy, in the valley of the upper Meuse, where Lang sets Domrémy. agreed for the place on the Meuse (Lowell; Lang). The distance from Domrémy differs: thirteen miles, Lowell; some twelve miles, Lang. "Domremy was only thirteen miles south of Vaucouleurs … At Vaucouleurs (the valley of color), the valley of the Meuse is a little narrower than at Domremy, and the town, beginning on the meadows," (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 3 and ch. 4, lines 1554-1555 and 1828-1830, scholarly) "one of many villages that nestle by the banks of the Upper Meuse. … The Meuse flowing north past the legendary oak forest, … before it reaches the walled town of the region, Vaucouleurs, then held for the Dauphin by a stout, rough, humorous captain, Robert de Baudricourt." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 2, lines 1625-1659, scholarly) "Robert de Baudricourt, commanding in Vaucouleurs, some twelve miles distant from Domremy" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 2982-2983, scholarly)
- 020.63 Event: from Gien, a town on the Loire about forty miles above Orléans that held for Charles VII, the party rode on south of the river towards Touraine. agreed for the road on from Gien south of the Loire (Lowell, across the Sologne; France, along the northern border of Berry into the Blésois; Lang sets Fierbois south of the Loire). That they crossed the Loire at Gien itself is France's reading, who puts Gien on the right (north) bank and the passage of the Loire after it; Lowell's road, from Gien across the Sologne, fits it, but no source read names the crossing in words, and France alone carries no claim (rules.md): single (France) for the crossing at Gien. "On Friday or thereabouts they reached Gien, a town on the Loire about forty miles above Orleans, holding for Charles; … Joan impatiently rode forward across the sandy Sologne" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 5, lines 2402-2425, scholarly) "then they reached the town of Gien, on the right bank of the Loire. … it was after their passage of the Loire that Bertrand de Poulengy and his companions found themselves exposed to the greatest danger. … From Gien, the little company followed the northern boundary of the duchy of Berry, crossed into Blesois," (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, vol. 1 ch. 4, lines 5885-5948, scholarly) "the little town of Fierbois, near Chinon, south of the Loire." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, lines 4015-4016, scholarly)

# Research for Joan of Arc

## 030 orleans

Coverage: primary, scholarly and wikipedia. The ten days are told day by day in the Journal du siège (Quicherat 4, 6236-6830, which dates 7 May as the 6th and 8 May as the 7th, as Quicherat's notes correct) and the Chronique de la Pucelle (Cousinot's edition, 11880-12530, where the paragraphs marked as the Geste share their text with the Journal: the standard touching the boulevard is one account in both). Three other primary accounts written in 1429 or soon after stand apart from them: Guillaume Girault's notarial note (Quicherat 4, 11277-11329), Charles VII's letter to Narbonne of 10 May 1429 (Quicherat 5, 5466-5600) and the Orléans chronicle of the feast of 8 May (Quicherat 5, 13880-14150). The witnesses of 1456 (Dunois, Luillier, de Contes, Beaucroix, Pasquerel, d'Aulon, in Murray), Monstrelet from the Burgundian side, the Bourgeois of Paris and Joan's own answers at her trial give the rest. Of the scholarly works, Ramsay, Lowell, Lang and France follow these with notes, Beaucourt and Oman in a page each. No source read gives a plan of the bastilles, or the places of the garrisons within the forts: their names, their side of the river and the order of the attacks are in the chronicles, and only some distances are given (Saint-Loup from the city, 030.5; the western bastilles from each other, France alone, 030.79), so a drawing of them is a reconstruction. Lowell's map of Orléans and its vicinity is not in the text read. The name of the island used for the crossing of 6 May differs from source to source (030.51), and only Anatole France names the gate by which Joan entered on 29 April (030.25).

- 030.1 Border: the English held Orléans with a chain of detached bastilles on both banks that covered the west and the south and left the north-east open, so that armed companies could come and go. agreed. "were constructed on the north bank, four having been constructed on the south side" "works only covered the West and South, leaving one half of the town open on the North-East" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, scholarly) "small armed companies to come and go without opposition" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "Exit and entrance from Orleans by French troops had never been entirely prevented" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly)
- 030.2 Figure: the number of English works: seven bastides on the north bank and four on the south, with the Tourelles and the Isle Charlemagne, Ramsay; ten or eleven bastilles, the chronicle of the feast of 8 May; thirteen fortified places, boulevards and bastilles, in December 1428, the Chronique de la Pucelle; not less than sixty towers and batteries, Monstrelet. disputed: Ramsay and the chronicle of the feast give about eleven works, the Chronique thirteen, Monstrelet sixty. "were constructed on the north bank, four having been constructed on the south side" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, scholarly) "Et avoient de dix à unze bastilles) dont la première esloient les Torelles" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, 4 May 1429, primary) "la ville fut enclose tant de la partie de Beaussc que de Soulongnc , de treize places fortifiées, tant boulevarts, comme bastides" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 39, 29 December 1428, primary) "batteries and towers, of which they had erected net kss thas sixty" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary)
- 030.3 Border: the English bastilles by name: on the north bank Saint-Laurent, the Croix-Boissée, London, Rouen at the Pressoir-Ars, Paris at Saint-Pouair, and Saint-Loup to the east; on the south bank Saint-Jean-le-Blanc, the Augustins, Saint-Privé and the Tourelles; one on the Isle Charlemagne between. agreed. "la bastide Sainct-Laurcns, la bastide du Colombier, la bastide de la Croix-Boisséc, la bastide qu'ils nommèrent Londres … la bastide Haro, nommée Rouan" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 39, 29 December 1428, primary) "de Sainct-Poire où ilz avoient faict une forte bastille qu'ilz appeloient Paris … du Pressoir -Ars qu'ilz nommoient Rouen" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Guillaume Girault, primary) "Blanc, celle du champ Saint-Privé, et celle de l'Isle Charlemaigne, Saint-Loretit, et Londres" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, 4 May 1429, primary)
- 030.4 Border: Saint-Laurent was the largest English work, outside the west gate, the Porte Regnart, where the chief English captains and most of their strength lay. agreed. "at St. Laurent des Orgerils, outside the west gate, Porte Regnart, of the city" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6, scholarly) "la bastide Sainct-Laurens , où furent tous les plus grands chefs de guerre et le plus de la puissance" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, 5 May 1429, primary) "Saint-Laurens, the strongest of the English works" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.5 Border: the bastille of Saint-Loup stood on the north bank east of the city, about a mile and a half above it (Lowell), a league (Lang) or two and a half miles (France), over the landing-place by the Chécy road. agreed for the place; the distance differs by author. "situated on the north bank of the Loire, about a mile and a half above the town" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "the English fort which commanded her landing-place, about a league above Orleans" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "two and a half miles east of the city, they occupied without opposition the steep slope of Saint-Loup" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly)
- 030.6 Border: on the south bank William Glasdale held the Tourelles, the boulevard before them and the bastides of the Augustins, Saint-Privé and Saint-Jean-le-Blanc; Saint-Privé guarded the crossing to the Isle Charlemagne. agreed. "tides des Augustins , de Sainct-Privé , et de Sainct-Jcan le Blanc , qui furent bien grandement fortifiez … sous le gouvernement de Glacidas capitaine des Tournclles" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 38, December 1428, primary) "William Glasdale having maintained his position on the south side throughout" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, scholarly) "St. Prive, built on the opposite bank of the river to secure the ferry from the isle Charlemagne" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6, scholarly)
- 030.7 Border: the Tourelles were a bridge-house on a pier at the south end of the bridge, joined by a drawbridge to the boulevard, an earthwork on the shore before them; on the city side three arches of the bridge were broken. agreed. "the boulevard of the Tourelles, an earthwork connected by a drawbridge with the pier upon which the Tourelles itself was built" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "southern end of the bridge, but separated from the shore by a drawbridge" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, scholarly) "il y avoit trois arches rompues avant que on pust joindre ausdictes Torelles" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, 7 May 1429, primary)
- 030.8 Border: the French held a boulevard on the bridge at the Belle-Croix, facing the Tourelles across the broken arches, built when the English took the Tourelles in October 1428. agreed. "feirent ung boulevert au droit de la Belle Croix, qui est sur le pont" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, October 1428, primary) "breaking down an arch of the bridge, and erecting a barricade on the arch of the Belle Croix" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6, scholarly)
- 030.9 Person: William Glasdale (Glacidas, Classidas, Clasdas in the sources), English, captain of the Tourelles. agreed. "Glacidas, qui estoit cappitaine et moult renommé" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "William Glasdale, Bailly of Alengon. He was Captain of the Fort of the Tourelles" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, Murray's note, primary) "the Bridge Fort, in which was the Englishman, Clasdas" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary)
- 030.10 Person: William de la Pole, Earl of Suffolk, John Talbot and Thomas, Lord Scales, the English commanders before Orléans as lieutenants of Bedford, regent; Suffolk had the chief command after Salisbury's death. agreed. "William de la Pole, Count of Suffolk, Sir John Talbot, and Thomas, Lord Scales, lieutenants of the Duke of Bedford" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-halsall-source-joanofarc.asp.txt, the letter of 22 March 1429, primary) "Pole replaced him as commander of the English troops in France" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of William Pole, scholarly) "the Regent having given the chief command to Suffolk" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 26, scholarly) "the English captains, namely, the earl of Suffolk, lord Talbot, lord Scales and others" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary)
- 030.11 Person: Jean, the Bastard of Orléans (Dunois), French, brother of the captive Duke of Orléans and commander in the city. agreed. "Dunois, commanding in Orleans, bore the brunt of her" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "I ordered the boats to the town … The Duke was then a prisoner in England" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition and Murray's note, primary) "my lord of Dunois [the Bastard], brother of my lord the duke of Orleans" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, quoting the chronicle of the feast, scholarly)
- 030.12 Person: the leaders of the convoy from Blois, French: the marshals Gilles de Rais and Jean de la Brosse, lord of Boussac (also Sainte-Sévère), with La Hire and Ambroise de Loré. agreed. "the Sieurs de Rais 2 and de Boussac, Marshals of France ; de Coulent, Admiral of France ; La Hire ; and Ambroisede Lore" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "the Marshals de Rais and Boussac, the leaders of the expedition" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "le mareschal de Rays et messire Ambroys de Loré, qui par le conmandement du roy" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 29 April 1429, primary) "Jean de la Brosse, Marshal of France, is sometimes called Marshal de Boussac and Marshal de Saint Sévère" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of Jean de la Brosse, scholarly)
- 030.13 Person: Étienne de Vignolles, called La Hire, French, a Gascon captain, who crossed and entered Orléans with her on 29 April. agreed. "Étienne de Vignolles, called La Hire, was a Gascon captain" "he encountered the Maid at Blois and reëntered Orléans with her on April 29, 1429" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of Étienne de Vignolles, scholarly) "La Hire crossed the Loire at the same time as she" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary)
- 030.14 Person: Raoul de Gaucourt, French, governor of Orléans; Dunois calls him then Grand Steward. agreed. "covered by Gaucourt, the old governor of the city" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "wife of the Sire de Gaucourt, Governor of Orleans" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "the Sieur de Gaucourt, then Grand Steward" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary)
- 030.15 Person: Jacques Boucher, treasurer of the Duke of Orléans, her host, whose house stood by the Porte Regnart. agreed. "auprez de la porte Regnart , en l'ostel de Jacquet Boucher, pour lors thrésorier" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 29 April 1429, primary) "His house was situated at the Renard Gate" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of Jacques Boucher, scholarly) "fut logée près de la porte Regnart" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary)
- 030.16 Event: the convoy and its escort left Blois for Orléans on 27 or 28 April 1429. disputed: France gives Wednesday 27 April, noting that Windecke gives the 28th; Lowell gives Thursday 28 April and holds one night on the march; Ramsay leaves it at the 27th or 28th; Pasquerel says they slept two nights in the fields. "Wednesday, the 27th of April, they started." (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, ch. 11, scholarly) "On the morning of Thursday, April 28," (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "On the 27th or 38th April the convoy left Blois." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "On that and the two following days, we slept in the fields." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary)
- 030.17 Event: the captains took the convoy up the south bank through the Sologne, because the English were strongest in the Beauce, and did not tell Joan, who wanted to go by the Beauce. agreed. "passeroient par la Sauloigne, obstant que la plus … puissance des Angloys estoit du couslé de la Beausse; combien que de ce ne dirent riens" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 28 April 1429, primary) "Is it you who said I was to come on this side [of the river], and that I should not go direct to the side where Talbot and the English are" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "Jeanne wanted to enter Orleans from the North, through the district of Beauce." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "qu'on iroit par la Solongne, pour ce que toute la plus grand puissance estoit du cosié de la Bcausse" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 44, primary)
- 030.18 Event: the day the convoy came opposite Orléans by the heights of Olivet. disputed: 28 April in Lang, France and Ramsay; about noon on Friday 29 April in Lowell. "On April 28 this strange force, with a convoy of cattle, arrived opposite Orleans by the south bank" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "On the evening of Thursday, the 28th of April, Jeanne was able to discern from the heights of Olivet" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, ch. 12, scholarly) "passing by Olivet on Thursday, 28th April," (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "about Friday noon Joan came upon the heights of Olivet" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.19 Event: where Joan passed the night before her entry. disputed: the Journal has the force lie at Chécy; Lang and France have her cross to Chécy on 28 April and sleep at Reuilly, the house of Guy de Cailly; Ramsay has them lodge at Rully near Chécy; Lowell holds the Journal's Chécy a slip of the pen for the night of 28 April and reads Cléry, on the march. "vindrent jusques à ung villaige nomme Checy, là où ilz geurent la nuict" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 28 April 1429, primary) "had passed the night at Reuilly, the house of Guy de Cailly" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "they lodged at Rully, near Ch&y, some four miles above" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "plainly by a slip of the pen" "The night referred to is that of the 28th, and Cl^ry is a reasonable emendation of the text" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.20 Event: on 29 April Dunois crossed the Loire to meet the convoy, its supplies were loaded into boats sent up from the city, the contrary wind turned, and the supplies went into Orléans while the garrison skirmished before Saint-Loup. agreed. "entrèrent dedans la ville les vivres et artillerie que la Pucelle avoit conduicts jusques à Checy" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 29 April 1429, primary) "It was necessary to load the convoy on boats, which were procured with difficulty." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "Et soudainement le vent se changea" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 44, primary) "le vent, qui estoit contraire, se tourna d'aval" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "On the morning of the 29th the Bastard of Orleans crossed the river to meet them" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.21 Event: where the convoy met the boats. disputed: facing the church of Saint-Loup, Dunois, and apparently at Saint-Loup, Ramsay; at the Île-aux-Bourdons before Chécy, the chronicle of the feast, with the Journal's "jusques à Checy"; some five miles above the city, Lowell. "to the Loire, facing the Church of Saint Loup" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "The convoy was brought down to the river bank, apparently at the Fort Saint- Loup" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "arrivèrent jusques à ITsle-aux-Bourdons qui est devant Checi" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "were brought five miles farther up the river to a place where the supplies were embarked" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.22 Figure: Joan crossed into Orléans with about 200 lances, Beaucroix; Ramsay gives the same number. single. "she crossed the river with about 200 lances in boats" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Simon Beaucroix's deposition, primary) "Jeanne, the provision train, and some 200 lances were taken on board" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.23 Event: the rest of the escort went back to Blois, with Pasquerel, the priests and her banner, to cross there and return. agreed.
