"s'en retourneroyent à Bloys" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 29 April 1429, primary) "As for myself I returned to Blois, by Jeanne's command, with the Priests and the banner." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary) "go up the river they and the army to Blois" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary)
- 030.24 Event: Joan entered Orléans on the evening of Friday 29 April 1429, about eight o'clock, armed and on a white horse, with Dunois beside her and the townspeople with torches, and the English did not stop her. agreed. "ainsi comme à huyct heures au soir, malgré tous les Angloys qui oncques n'y mirent empeschement aucun, elle y entra armée de toutes pièces , montée sur ung cheval blanc" "avoit à son cousté senestre le bastart d'Orléans" "portans grant nombre de torches" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 29 April 1429, primary) "ville d'Orléans, le pénultiesme jour d'avril, l'an mille quatre cent vingt-neuf" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 44, primary) "elle entra en la cité à tout grant foison d'Arminalx et grant quantité de vivres, que oncques ceulx de l'ost ne s'en meurent" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, §503, primary)
- 030.25 Event: she entered by the Porte de Bourgogne, the Burgundian Gate on the east side, France. single. Lang has the plan bring the convoy to that gate; the Journal, which France cites, names no gate. "of the Burgundian Gate, also called the Gate of Saint-Aignan … she entered Orleans, by the Burgundian Gate" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, ch. 5 and 12, scholarly) "the convoy to the east or Burgundian gate of Orleans" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly)
- 030.26 Figure: the army that brought the convoy from Blois: about 3,000, Lowell, and Lang from Windecke and the Chronique de Tournai; 7,000, Monstrelet; 10,000 or 12,000, Joan at her trial; Lang calls the large figures impossible. disputed. "started on its march, three thousand strong" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "were followed by seven thousand more, who escorted" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary) "he gave her 10 or 12,000 men" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 27 February 1431, primary) "These are impossible figures : the army in a very few days would have eaten up the supplies" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, notes to ch. 10, scholarly)
- 030.27 Event: on Saturday 30 April La Hire and Florent d'Illiers made a sortie towards Saint-Pouair, in which Joan took no part. single (the Journal, which Lowell follows). "saillirent La Hire, messire Florent d'IUiers" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 30 April 1429, primary) "on Saturday there was an unimportant skirmish in which she took no part" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.28 Event: the English had kept the herald who carried her letter, and answered that they would burn her, calling her a whore and telling her to go back to keeping cows. agreed. "qu'ilz la brusleroient et feroient ardoir, et que elle nestoit qu'une ribaulde, et comme telle s'en retournast garder les vaches" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 30 April 1429, primary) "en l'injuriant, et que si ils la tenoicnt, ils la feroicat ardoir" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 44, primary) "lequel la diffama moult de langaige, comme clamer ribaulde et putain" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, §504, primary)
- 030.29 Event: from the boulevard of the Belle-Croix on the bridge she called on Glasdale and the English in the Tourelles to yield, and they insulted her. agreed. "elle -s'en ala au boulevert de la Belle Croix, sur le pont, et de là parla à Glacidas … l'injuriant et appelant vachère" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 30 April 1429, primary) "oîi estoit Glacidas, car on pouvoit parler à eux de dessus le pont" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, primary) "à laquelle ilz dirent plusieurs injures et entre les autres Clacidas" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary)
- 030.30 Event: the day she called to Glasdale from the bridge. disputed: the evening of Saturday 30 April, the Journal and Lowell; the day of the Ascension, 5 May, the Chronique de la Pucelle and Ramsay after it. "Le lendemain qui fut samedy , derrenier jour d'i" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 30 April 1429, primary) "so she went out to the barricade on the bridge and called across the narrow opening to Glasdale" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "si délibéra le lendemain de les aller visiter" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, primary) "took her on to the bridge to hail Glasdale to surrender" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, note, scholarly)
- 030.31 Event: on Sunday 1 May Dunois left Orléans for Blois to bring the army back. single (the Journal, which Lowell follows); the Chronique has news come to Blois from Dunois. "se partist de la ville le bastart d'Orléans, pour aller à Bloys" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 1 May 1429, primary) "on May 1 the Bastard also went to Blois" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "vint nouvelles du bastard" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 44, primary)
- 030.32 Event: on Monday 2 May she rode out of the city to look at the English bastilles. single (the Journal, which Lowell follows). "eust veu et regardé à son plaisir les fortificacions des Angloys" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 2 May 1429, primary) "she also spent much time in reconnoitring the English" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.33 Event: the army left Blois on 3 May along the north bank through the Beauce, and on the morning of 4 May, met by Joan and part of the garrison, it entered Orléans past the English bastilles, whose garrisons did not come out. agreed. "et se partirent le troisiesmc jour de niay … passèrent par devant les Anglois qui n'osèrent y.ssir de leurs bastides, et puis entrèrent dedans la ville environ prime" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 44-45, primary) "ilz entrèrent pardevant la bastille des Angloys, qui … n'osèrent oncques saillir, mais se tenoient fort en leurs gardes" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 4 May 1429, primary) "I came to Orleans by way of the Beauce always with this same banner surrounded by Priests meeting no obstacle" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary) "a fresh relief was despatched from Blois, along the north bank of the Loire" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.34 Figure: Joan rode out to meet the army with 500 combatants, the Journal; Lang gives the same. single. "estans en tout cinq cens combatans" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 4 May 1429, primary) "Jeanne rode out at dawn with some five hundred combatants" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 11, scholarly)
- 030.35 Border: the English work the army passed on 4 May. disputed: the bastille called Paris, on the road skirting the forest north of the city, Lang and the chronicle of the feast; Saint-Laurent, Wikipedia alone (Siege_of_Orléans_(1428–1429).wiki, "Lifting the siege": in full view of the English garrison at St. Laurent, a sentence whose footnote cites the Chronique de la Pucelle, p. 285, which names no bastille); the Journal and the Chronique name none. "the only strong fort which the army passed was that called Paris" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 11, scholarly) "et failloit passer au plus près de la bastille des dits Anglois, nommée Paris" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, 4 May 1429, primary)
- 030.36 Figure: about 5,000 regular troops in Orléans after 4 May, besides several thousand armed citizens, Lowell. single. "About five thousand regular troops were now gathered in Orleans, beside several thousand armed citizens." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.37 Figure: the English strength before Orléans: 10,000 under the captains Bedford sent after Salisbury's death, who closed the Beauce side in December 1428, the Chronique de la Pucelle; an army of 5,000, Oman; fewer than 3,000 round the town in March and April, France; hardly equal to the French regulars in May, Lowell. disputed. "bien la puissance du siège nonibréede dixiuilic hommes" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 38, December 1428, primary) "An army of five thousand men, which has strung itself out to the thinnest of lines" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly) "for they had less than three thousand round the town" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "The besieging force, it is probable, hardly equaled that of the French regulars" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.38 Event: on Wednesday 4 May, the eve of the Ascension, the French attacked the bastille of Saint-Loup from about noon; Joan came to the assault after it had begun, and the bastille was taken, burned and pulled down. agreed; the Journal has her go out with the Bastard of Orléans at the start, where the Chronique, Pasquerel and de Coutes have her come after it had begun. "Et durant iceluy y vint très hastivemeut la Pucelle armée, à estendart desployé" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, primary) "s'en allèrent assaillir la bastille Sainct-Loup … abatirenl , bruslèrent et desmolirent du tout celle bastille" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 4 May 1429, primary) "Le mercredi , veille d'Ascension , ive jour de may" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Guillaume Girault, primary) "celle de Saint-Loup; laquelle, Dieux aydant, iizont prinse et gaignée par puissance" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Charles VII to Narbonne, 10 May 1429, primary) "this took place on the Eve of the Ascension of Our Saviour" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary)
- 030.39 Event: during the assault English came out to help Saint-Loup, from Saint-Pouair in the Journal, from the other bastilles under Talbot in the Chronique and the chronicle of the feast, and went back when the French drew up against them. agreed. "Partie desquelz estans à la bastille de Sainct-Pouair, saillirent à grant puissance" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 4 May 1429, primary) "Mais le sire de Tallebot, ce voyant, tist retirer Anjjlois au dedans de leurs bas" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, primary) "issirent de leurs bastilles cinq à six estandars pour cuider leyer le siège dudit Saint-Loup" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "combien que les Anglois des autres bastides fussent alors yssus en bataille" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Charles VII to Narbonne, 10 May 1429, primary)
- 030.40 Person: where John Talbot was on 4 May. disputed: Champion and Ramsay put him at Saint-Loup, and the Chronique has Talbot garrison it; the Chronique and the chronicle of the feast have him bring out men from the other bastilles; Lang puts his main force at Saint-Laurent, and France has him sally from Saint-Laurent to help Saint-Loup. "At the siege of Orléans. he occupied the bastille of Saint Loup." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of John Talbot, scholarly) "attacked the Bastille Saint- Loup, where Lord Talbot was posted" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "esté grandement garnie par le sire de Tallebot" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, primary) "the main force of Talbot at St. Laurent" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "Talbot sallied forth from the camp of Saint-Laurent-des-Orgerils" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, ch. 12, scholarly)
- 030.41 Figure: about 1,500 French combatants attacked Saint-Loup, the Journal. single. "et environ quinze cens combatans" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 4 May 1429, primary)
- 030.42 Figure: the English at Saint-Loup and their losses. disputed: 114 killed and 40 prisoners, the Journal; 160 killed, the Chronique; more than 120 taken and dead, Girault; 120 to 140 combatants within, the chronicle of the feast; a garrison of 300 to 400, Monstrelet; all killed, with two French dead, Charles VII's letter. "rent cens et quatorze Angloys, et en retindrent et amenèrent quarante prisonniers" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 4 May 1429, primary) "Don! fut l'occision nombrée à huit vingts hommes" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, primary) "Et y furent prins et mors plus de six vingtz Anglois." (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Guillaume Girault, primary) "taient de six à sept vings Anglois combatens" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "garrisoned with firom three to fbnr hundred English" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary) "mors et tués tous les Anglois qui dedens estoienl , sans ce qu'il y soit mort des nostres que deux seules personnes" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Charles VII to Narbonne, 10 May 1429, primary)
- 030.43 Figure: the length of the assault on Saint-Loup. disputed: three hours, the Journal; more than four or five hours, Charles VII's letter; several hours, Ramsay. "l'espace de trois heures que l'assault dura très aspre" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 4 May 1429, primary) "qui dura plus de quatre ou cinq heures" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Charles VII to Narbonne, 10 May 1429, primary) "After several hours of severe fighting" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.44 Event: English who had taken refuge in the church tower of Saint-Loup in priests' vestments were spared at Joan's word and brought to Orléans. agreed. "qui prindrent haiiillemens de preslres ou de gens d'église" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, primary) "I heard it said that the English ecclesiastics had taken their ornaments" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Louis de Contes's deposition, primary)
- 030.45 Event: Saint-Loup was the first English fort taken in May, and its fall cleared the east side of the city. agreed that it was the first; that its fall cleared the east side is Lowell's. "she went first to Orleans, to the fortress of Saint-Loup, and then to the fortress of the Bridge" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 27 February 1431, primary) "cessivement de la bastille Saint-Loup, de la bastille Saint Augustin et du fort des Tourelles" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, book 2, scholarly) "The fall of St. Loup cleared the approaches on the east side of the town." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.46 Event: no one fought on the feast of the Ascension, Thursday 5 May. disputed: the Chronique and Ramsay have the captains refuse to fight for the feast while Joan wanted to attack Saint-Laurent; Pasquerel and the chronicle of the feast have Joan herself refuse to fight; Lowell notes that the accounts disagree. "Mais les chefs de guerre ne furent point d'accord d'issir ny bcsongncr ccste journée, pour la réicrcncc du jour" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, primary) "she told me that on the following day, the Ascension of Our Saviour, she would not fight" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary) "homme ne fist guerre, car la dicte Fucelle ne le vouloit pas" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "the captains, who probably knew that their men needed rest" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "do not agree in all respects" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, note, scholarly)
- 030.47 Event: a council decided to attack the Tourelles and the works on the south bank, so that the city could again be supplied from Berry. agreed. "fut conclud qu'on assauldrait les Tournelles et bouleverts du bout du pont" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 5 May 1429, primary) "afin tpie la ville peust recouvrer vivres de Berry et autres pays" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 46, primary) "it was agreed to cross the river on Friday, and to attack the Tourelles and the other English works in the neighborhood" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.48 Event: on the Ascension Joan had a last summons to the English shot into their lines tied to an arrow, Pasquerel. single. "Jeanne took an arrow, on the point of which she fastened this letter with a thread" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary)
- 030.49 Event: on Friday 6 May the French crossed the Loire in boats from the city to an island, and from it to the south bank over a bridge of two boats. agreed. "passa la rivière de Loire, entre Sainct-Loup et la Tour" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 6 May 1429, primary) "two boats were brought, of which a bridge was made, for the attack of the fort" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Jean d'Aulon's deposition, primary) "vendredy dernier, nos dites gens passèrent la rivière par bateaux à Orléans" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Charles VII to Narbonne, 10 May 1429, primary) "sixiésme jour de may, François passèrent oullre la Loire" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 47, primary) "crossing the narrow southern arm of the Loire with two boats moored as pontoons" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.50 Figure: about 4,000 French combatants crossed on 6 May, the Journal. single. "et environ quatre mil eombalans" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 6 May 1429, primary)
- 030.51 Border: the island of the crossing. disputed in its name: a small island facing Saint-Aignan, the Journal; an island before Saint-Jean-le-Blanc, the chronicle of the feast; the Île Saint-Aignan, Ramsay; the Île-aux-Toiles, France. "en une petite ysle, qui est au droict de Sainct Aignan" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 6 May 1429, primary) "une isle qui est devant Saint- Jehau-le-Blaiic" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "landing in the He Saint Aignan" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "l'Ile-aux-Toiles was reached, and thence on a bridge formed by two" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, ch. 13, scholarly)
- 030.52 Event: at the crossing the English left the bastille of Saint-Jean-le-Blanc without a fight, Glasdale burning it, and fell back on the Augustins, the boulevard and the Tourelles; the Journal has the French take it at the first onset. agreed. "et fist retirer ses Anglois avec ses habillenicns en la bastide des Auguslins, au boulevart et aux Tournelles" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 47, primary) "found quite deserted ; for the English who were therein, so soon as they perceived the coming of the French, went away" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Jean d'Aulon's deposition, primary) "they quitted the Fort of Saint-Jeanle-Blanc, and retreated to another fort near the Augustins" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Simon Beaucroix's deposition, primary) "et de prime face prindrent Sainct-Jehan le Blanc" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 6 May 1429, primary)
- 030.53 Event: the French fell back towards the island, on a cry that the English of Saint-Privé were coming (the Chronique) or on the captains' decision (d'Aulon); the English came out after them, Joan and La Hire turned on them and drove them back, and the French took the Augustins by assault before night. agreed. "survint un cry (pie les Anjjlois venoient à puissance du costé de Sainct" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 47, primary) "immediately the Maid and La Hire, who were always in the front to protect them, couched their lances and were the first to attack the enemy" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Jean d'Aulon's deposition, primary) "Mais la Pucelle et La Hire, à tout partie de leurs gens se joignirent ensemble" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 6 May 1429, primary) "rent les . Augustins de bel assault" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "Et le mesmes jour gangnèrent le logis des Âugustins" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Charles VII to Narbonne, 10 May 1429, primary)
- 030.54 Event: Joan was hurt in the foot by a caltrop at the Augustins, the Chronique; Ramsay follows it. single. "chausse-trapes en l'un des pieds" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 47, primary) "had been slightly wounded in the foot" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.55 Event: the Augustins were burned when taken, by Joan's order so that the soldiers would not stop to plunder, the Chronique; Monstrelet has the second fort destroyed by fire and its men put to the sword. agreed for the burning; the order is the Chronique's. "tentifs au pillage, la Pucelle list bouter le feu en la bas" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 47, primary) "which was in like manner destroyed by fire, and those within put to the sword" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary)
- 030.56 Event: on the night of 6 May the French force lay before the Tourelles, fed by the townspeople. disputed whether Joan stayed with it: the Chronique, de Contes and Lowell have her taken back into the city; d'Aulon has her stay before the fort all night. "le soir ensuivant fut par les Françoys mis le siège devant les Tournelles" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 6 May 1429, primary) "(pi'il ennuictoit, lui rammenée àOrh'ans" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 47, primary) "then she re-entered Orleans, and went back to sleep at her lodging" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Louis de Contes's deposition, primary) "the Lords and their people with the Maid remained before the same [fort] all that night" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Jean d'Aulon's deposition, primary) "spite of the testimony of Aulon" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, note, scholarly)
- 030.57 Event: that night English crossed the Loire at Saint-Privé. disputed: the Chronique and Ramsay have the garrison of Saint-Privé fire their work and cross to Saint-Laurent; the chronicle of the feast has English try to cross at Saint-Privé in two or three boats, many drowning, which Lowell reads as Talbot sending men to the south bank. "et se retirèrent en la bastide Sainct-Laurcns" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 47, primary) "During the night the English at Saint Prive' abandoned their position" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "cuidèrent passer la rivière les dits Ânglois au droit de la bastille du champ Saint" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "On Friday night Talbot tried to send a small body of men across the river" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.58 Event: on Saturday 7 May the French attacked the boulevard and the Tourelles from the south bank from morning to evening. agreed: to sunset in the Journal and Pasquerel, to six in the evening in the Chronique, to eight in Dunois. "celluy assault qui dura depuis le malin jusques au soleil couchant" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "The attack lasted throughout, -from the morning until 8 o'clock in the evening" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "The attack lasted from morning to sunset without interruption." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary) "qui dura despuJs prime jusques à six heures après midy" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary)
- 030.59 Event: whether the captains opposed the attack of 7 May. disputed: the Chronique has Joan cross with the burgesses' consent and against all the royal captains, and Lowell has the council of war give way to her and the burgesses; Lang differs from Lowell on that resistance. "par l'accord et consentement des bourgeois d'Orléans, mais contre l'opinion et volonté de tous les chefs et capitaines" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary) "Assailed on every side, the council of war at last recalled its decision." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "resistance of the French leaders to her attack on the Tourelles, on May 7, I differ from Mr. Lowell" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, preface, scholarly)
- 030.60 Event: from the city side the townsmen bridged the broken arches with a gutter lengthened by a carpenter, so that men crossed and attacked the Tourelles from the bridge as well. agreed. "ilz menèrent ung charpentier, et portèrent goutières et eschelles, dont ilz feirent planches" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "on trouva une vieille et large gouttière" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary) "Et devers ceulx de la ville aussi firent pons pour assaillir" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "having succeeded in bridging the broken arches with beams" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.61 Border: the townsmen's guns fired on the Tourelles from the Île Saint-Antoine in the river, Ramsay; the Chronique has the guns of Orléans fire on the English without naming the place. single. "while the townspeople bombarded the Tourelles from the He Saint- Antoine" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "car ceux d'Orléans jectèreut à merveilles contre Anglois de canons" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary)
- 030.62 Event: Joan was struck by an arrow or crossbolt between the neck and the shoulder, by her own account as she raised a ladder against the boulevard. agreed for the neck and the shoulder; Pasquerel alone puts the wound above the breast. "as she was raising the ladder she was wounded in the neck with the crossbolt" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 27 February 1431, primary) "frappée d'un traict entre l'espaule et la gorge, si avant qu'il passoit" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "wounded by an arrow which penetrated half-a-foot between the neck and the shoulder" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "was struck by an arrow above the breast" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary) "d'un coup de traict de gros garriau, par l'espaule tout oullre" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary)
- 030.63 Event: the hour of her wound. disputed: in the morning, the Chronique; after dinner, Pasquerel; early in the afternoon, Lowell; after the boulevard was won, Ramsay. "fut ladictc Jeanne blessée dès le matin" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary) "At this assault, after dinner, Jeanne, as she had predicted" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary) "Early in the afternoon Joan, who had been in the thick of the fight" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "won ; but Jeanne was struck down by a quarrel" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.64 Event: she was carried off and her wound dressed, and came back; towards evening, when the captains meant to draw off, she prayed apart, set her standard at the edge of the ditch, and the French went up the boulevard and took it. agreed. "Et ce dit, laissa son estandart, et s'en ala sur son cheval à ung lieu destourné faire oraison à Nostre Seigneur" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "all alone by herself, remained in prayer about half an hour" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "Jeanne appeared, her standard in her hand, and placed it on the edge of the trench" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Jean Luillier's deposition, primary) "aucuns chevaliers veirent ung colon blanc voler par sus l'estandart de la dicte PucelJe" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "Tout est vostre — et y entrez" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.65 Event: as the English of the boulevard made for the Tourelles the bridge between gave way under them, Glasdale and many others fell into the Loire and drowned, and the Tourelles were taken. agreed. "parce qu'en eulx cuidans sauver, le pont fondit soubz eulz" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "mais le pont levis rompit soubs eux, par le jugement de Dieu, et noyèrent en la rivière de Loire" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary) "who, thinking to gain the Bridge Tower, fell into the river, where they were drowned" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "At this moment Clasdas, fully armed from head to foot, fell into the Loire, where he was drowned." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary) "car il se noia le jour devant que l'occision fut faicte" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, §504, primary) "the drawbridge broke down under him, and he perished in the Loire" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.66 Event: why the bridge failed. disputed: it gave way under the English, the Journal, and by God's judgement, the Chronique; Quicherat cites the town's accounts that it was burned on purpose with a fire-boat, and Lowell has the fire spread from the fire-boat to the drawbridge; the chronicle of the feast tells of the fire-boat and says the English had cut the bridge themselves to trap the French. "parce qu'en eulx cuidans sauver, le pont fondit soubz eulz" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "levis rompit soubs eux, par le jugement de Dieu" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary) "comptes de la ville pour 1429 prouvent que ce pont fut brûlé à dessein au moyen d'un bateau mcendiaire" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat's note to the Journal du siège, scholarly) "The fire, however, had spread from the fire-boat to the drawbridge, and this broke under the great weight" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly) "mené entre les Torelles et le boloart, et là fut boté le feu … car ils avoient copé le dit pont pour cuider tromper noz gens" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary)
- 030.67 Figure: the English in the boulevard and the Tourelles and their losses. disputed: 600 combatants under two banners and the standard of Chandos, all killed or taken, Charles VII's letter; 500 to 600 picked men, the chronicle of the feast; 400 to 500, all killed or drowned but a few prisoners, the Journal; of 500, about 200 taken alive, the Chronique; 300 prisoners saved, Ramsay; Wikipedia gives a garrison of 700 to 800 (Siege_of_Orléans_(1428–1429).wiki, "Assault on the Augustines", with no footnote). "bien vi*' combatans anglois, sous deux bannières et l'estendart de Ghandos" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Charles VII to Narbonne, 10 May 1429, primary) "et estoient léans de cinq à six cens combatans et gens d'élitte" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "quatre ou cinq cens combatans qu'ilz estoient fu- rent tous tuez ou noyez" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "car du nombre de cinq cent chevaliers et escuyers … ne furent retenus prisonniers en vie, fors environ deux cent" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary) "but 300 prisoners were saved alive" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.68 Figure: the losses of the three days of fighting: 6,000 to 8,000 English killed or taken and fewer than 100 French killed, Monstrelet; two French dead at Saint-Loup, Charles VII's letter (030.42); a hundred wounded or more, Joan at her trial. disputed. "so that firom ax to eight thousand combatants were killed or taken, whfle the French did not lose more than one hundred men of all ranks" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary) "there were a hundred wounded, or more" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, public examination of 27 February 1431, primary)
- 030.69 Person: English lords killed with Glasdale at the Tourelles: Sir William Moleyns and Sir Hugh Poynings. agreed; the chronicle of the feast names the lords of Poynings and Moleyns among those taken. "le seigneur de Moulins, le seigneur de Pommiers" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "Lisez Poiniiig et Mokijns" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, editor's note, scholarly) "were slain, a valiant English captain named Classendaci the lo^ Molina" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary) "Sir William Moleyns 3 and Sir Hugh Poynings" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "comme le sire de Poains, le sii-e de Molins" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary)
- 030.70 Event: where Joan spent the night of 7 May. disputed: in the field with the captains, guarding the Tourelles, the Journal; back in the city across the repaired bridge, the Chronique, Dunois and Lowell. "laquelle demoura celle nuyt, et les seigneurs, cap- pitaines et gens d'armes avecques elle, sur les champs" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 7 May 1429, primary) "qui letourna ceste nuictéc par-dessus le pont" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 48, primary) "we re-entered the town of Orleans the Maid and all the army" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "Joan rode back into the city through the fort and across the bridge, as she had foretold that very morning" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.71 Event: with the Tourelles taken and Saint-Privé abandoned (030.57) the English held nothing south of the Loire, all their men there killed or taken. agreed. "On this day, all the English who were on the other side of the bridge were taken and killed." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary) "gangnèrent toute lu dite bastide. Et ont esté tous les dits Anglois que y estoient, mors ou pris" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Charles VII to Narbonne, 10 May 1429, primary) "south side, where they were weakest If the blockade were" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 030.72 Event: on Sunday 8 May the English left their bastilles and drew up in order of battle before the city; the French came out and drew up facing them; Joan forbade them to begin the fight on a Sunday, and the English marched away. agreed. "rent les Angloys de Sainct-Pouair et d'ailleurs, et levans leur siège se mirent en bataille … pour l'amour et honneur du sainct dimenche, ne commanchassent point la ba" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 8 May 1429, primary) "she forbade the English to be attacked or in any way molested" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "but she would not allow any one to attack the English" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Jean Luillier's deposition, primary) "qui desconseilla la poursuite et voulut qu'on les laissast libres de partir" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 49, primary) "but they had no such intentions, having b^n exhorted to the contrary by Joan the Maid" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary)
- 030.73 Figure: the two armies stood facing each other for a whole hour, the Journal; Monstrelet says the English waited a considerable time. single. "l'espace d'une heure entière sans eulx toucher" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 8 May 1429, primary)
- 030.74 Event: the English marched to Meung; their captains went to Meung, Beaugency and Jargeau, Talbot to Meung and then Beaugency, Suffolk to Jargeau. agreed. "le comte de Suffort à Jargeau , et les seigneurs de Scales, de Tallebot, et autres chefs de leur parly, se retrairent tant à Meun , à Baugency" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 49, primary) "s'en alèrent bien rengez et ordonnez de- dans Meung sur Loire" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 8 May 1429, primary) "Ledit Tallebot demoura à Meung, à Boisgency et à Jargueau et à Yenville" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary) "to Meun, some to Beaugency, and some to Jargeau" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "After the siege was raised, Talbot drew back to Meting; then to Beaugency." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of John Talbot, scholarly)
- 030.75 Event: that Sunday masses were said before the army in the field, the French went back into the city giving thanks, and Orléans went in procession with a sermon. agreed. "et dire deux messes" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 49, primary) "there was in Orleans a solemn procession and a sermon" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Pasquerel's deposition, primary) "D'autre part rentrèrent à grant joye dedans Orléans la Pucelle" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 8 May 1429, primary) "dist ladicte Pucelle que chacun allast oyr messe" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, chronicle of the feast of 8 May, primary)
- 030.76 Event: the siege had lasted about seven months, from 12 October 1428 to 8 May 1429. agreed for the length; the first day, 12 October, is the Journal's. "qu'ilz avoient tenu devant Orléans deppuis le douziesme jour d'octobre mil quatre cens vingt huyt" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, 8 May 1429, primary) "The English captains had oontinned their siege of Orleans about seven mentfas" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary) "For seven weary months the English had lain at their gates" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.77 Event: the townspeople plundered the abandoned bastilles and pulled them down. agreed. "puis toutes les bastides furent jectécs par terre" (archive.org-download-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-chroniquedelapuc00cousuoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 49, primary) "The whole of these castles were soon burnt, and razed to the ground" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 59, primary)
- 030.78 Battle: the relief of Orléans, 29 April to 8 May 1429, the French under Dunois, with Joan, the marshals de Boussac and de Rais and La Hire, against the English besiegers under Suffolk, Talbot, Scales and Glasdale: the convoy and the army brought in, Saint-Loup, Saint-Jean-le-Blanc, the Augustins and the Tourelles taken, and the siege raised; a French victory. The day-by-day French movements, who crossed the Loire where and which bastille fell, are in the Journal du siège (Quicherat 4, 6236-6830), the Chronique de la Pucelle (11880-12530), the chronicle of the feast (Quicherat 5, 13880-14150) and d'Aulon's deposition (Murray, 15600-15830); the English side is told only from outside, and no source gives a plan. agreed. "taient en retraite : Orléans était délivré" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, book 2, scholarly) "Saturday, 7th May, brought the struggle to a close." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "Without haste and in good order Talbot prepared to retreat." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 8, scholarly)
- 030.79 Border: on the north-west the English built the bastille of London on the road to Le Mans by 20 March 1429, and between 9 and 15 April Rouen, nine hundred feet east of London, and Paris, nine hundred feet from Rouen, France. single. "On the 20th of March they completed the bastion named London, on the road to Mans" "two new bastions were erected towards the west, Rouen nine hundred feet east of London, Paris nine hundred feet from Rouen" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly)

## 040 the-loire-and-reims

Coverage: primary and scholarly; no line rests on wikipedia. The Loire week rests on four French chronicles read in Quicherat 4 (Cagny, who rode in Alençon's household; Jean Chartier; the Journal du siège; Gruel, Richemont's man), on Alençon's, Dunois's and Louis de Contes's depositions of 1456 (Murray), and for Patay on Wavrin, who rode under Fastolf. Monstrelet's chapter 61 is the same text as Wavrin's chapter 13 (Quicherat 4 heads it so), so the two are one account of Patay, and the Chronique de la Pucelle copies the Journal and Chartier: neither is counted as a second source to its original. The march to Reims has Cagny, the Journal, Monstrelet, Dunois, the letter of the three Angevin gentlemen written at Reims on 17 July (Quicherat 5) and Joan's own answers at her trial; the towns after the coronation have Cagny, the Journal, Monstrelet, Ramsay, Beaucourt 2 and the three lives. The scholarly works read for the section are Ramsay 1 ch. 27, Oman book 14 ch. 2, Lowell ch. 9-12, Lang ch. 14-16, France ch. 15-18 and vol. 2 ch. 1, and Beaucourt 2 ch. 5; Lowell, Lang and France build on Quicherat, so they count as one reading where they cite the same chronicle. Oman's paragraph on the Loire has Talbot come from Paris with the relief and dates Patay 17 June, against every chronicle read; Beaucourt 2 at p. 33 (book 2 ch. 1) prints Vailly and Soissons as 22 and 23 'août', a slip for July that his p. 234-235 (ch. 5) and every other source contradict. No author is used here with two works but Quicherat as editor of volumes 4 and 5, and volume 5 does not withdraw volume 4; it corrects the printed Monstrelet's date for the coronation (040.63). The assault on Jargeau is told in Cagny, Chartier, the Journal and Alençon from the French side only, and none of them places the English within the town, beyond Alençon's word that at the end they fell back to the bridges, where the French pursued them. The numbers on both sides at Jargeau, Beaugency and Patay differ from writer to writer and are given each with its author. Cagny's figures are read from Roman numerals that the OCR garbles: his 'de vu à vmc' at Jargeau is read as 'vii to viii c', 700 to 800, and his 'n à m mille' as 'ii to iii thousand'.

- 040.1 Event: early in June 1429 the king's council gave John, Duke of Alençon, command of the army, with orders to take the English places on the Loire, and sent Joan, Dunois and other captains with him; Alençon calls himself the king's lieutenant in command of the army. agreed. "early in June the duke of Alen^on was given command of the army" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, scholarly) "sent, for this purpose, the Duke d'Alengon, myself and other captains, as well as Jeanne, to reduce these towns and camps" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, 1456, primary) "I was then Lieutenant to the King, and thus in command of the army" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Alençon's deposition, primary)
- 040.2 Event: the army gathered at Selles-en-Berry; Joan was there by Monday 6 June, rode out that evening for Romorantin, and Alençon reached Selles the same day, as Guy de Laval wrote from Selles on Wednesday 8 June. agreed. "s'est partie ce luudy aux vespres de Selles, pour aller à Romorantin" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter of Guy de Laval, 8 June 1429, primary) "ce mercredy huictiesme de juin" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter of Guy de Laval, primary) "The rendezvous was at Selles, about fifteen miles from Loches and about fifty miles south of Orleans" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, scholarly) "From Loches the Maid went to Selles-en-Berry, a considerable town on the Cher" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, vol. 1 ch. 14, scholarly)
- 040.3 Figure: the French force that marched on Jargeau, by author: Alençon about 1,200 lances once Dunois's division had joined; Cagny 2,000 to 3,000 fighting men and as many common people or more; Monstrelet five or six thousand; the Journal du siège about 8,000, horse and foot. disputed. "we found ourselves to number about 1,200 lances" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Alençon's deposition, primary) "en leur compaignie de n à m mille com- batans et autant de gens de commun ou plus" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 12, primary) "with about five or six tibonsand combatants" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 60, primary) "faisans tous environ huict mil combatans, tant à cheval comme à piet" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 170, primary) "The Journ, Siege says there were about 8,000 fighting men altogether" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9 note, scholarly)
- 040.4 Event: the French came before Jargeau on the afternoon of Saturday 11 June; the common people rushed the ditches and were thrown back; the guns were set up in the night, and on Sunday 12 June the town was stormed after an assault of three or four hours. agreed. "le samedi xie jour du mois de juing environ deux heures après disner" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 12, primary) "L'assault très dur, lequel dura de trois à quatre heures. Et en la pariin la place fut prinse" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 13, primary) "On the 1 ath of the month Jargeau was captured by Jeanne and the Duke of Alen^on" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "It was still Sunday morning when the assault began" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, scholarly)
- 040.5 Person: William de la Pole, Earl of Suffolk, commanded Jargeau for the English and was taken there; of his brothers John was taken and Alexander killed. Chartier names Suffolk's captor Guillaume Regnault, whom the earl knighted before yielding. agreed. "The Earl of Suffolk and his brother John were taken ; a third brother, Alexander de la Pole, was killed" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "Le conte de Sutïbrd fut prins à prinsonnier, ung de ses frères" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 13, primary) "fut prisonnier le conte de Sufï'ord par ung nomméGuiIlaumeRegnault" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Jean Chartier, p. 65, primary) "y fut occis messire Alexandre de La Poulie, son aultre frère" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Jean Chartier, p. 65, primary)
- 040.6 Figure: the English garrison of Jargeau, by author: Lowell about 600; Chartier 600 to 700; Cagny 700 to 800; Monstrelet 300 to 400 with the townsmen. disputed. "held the place with about six hundred men" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, scholarly) "en sa compaignie de six à sept cens anglois" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Jean Chartier, p. 65, primary) "conte de Sufford, deux de ses frères et de vu à vmc" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 12, primary) "from three to four hundred of his men" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 61, primary)
- 040.7 Figure: the losses at Jargeau, by author: Alençon has more than a thousand English killed in the pursuit to the bridges; Chartier 300 to 400 killed; Monstrelet about 300 killed and sixty or more taken with Suffolk; Cagny 40 or 50 taken with Suffolk, the rest put to death, and only 16 or 20 French dead. disputed. "the English retired to the bridges, where the French pursued them" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Alençon's deposition, primary) "over a thousand men were slain in the pursuit" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, quoting Alençon, scholarly) "bien de trois à quatre cens Anglois, et les anltres prisonniers" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Jean Chartier, p. 65, primary) "about three hundred of the English" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 61, primary) "with rixty or more of their mem" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 61, primary) "Et n'y mourut de nostre costé que xvi ou XX personnes" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 13, primary)
- 040.8 Event: in the assault Joan, with her standard, was struck on the head by a stone thrown from the wall, fell, rose at once and urged the men on. agreed. "Jeanne was on a ladder, her standard in her hand, when her standard was struck and she herself was hit on the head by a stone" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Alençon's deposition, primary) "ung Angloys luy gecta une grosse pierre de fez sur la teste" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 172, primary)
- 040.9 Event: before the assault Suffolk asked for a fortnight's delay, offering to yield if not relieved; the French captains refused, and Joan told the garrison it might leave in its tunics with its lives. agreed. "It was the Lords of my party who answered the English that they should not have the fortnight's delay which they asked" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, trial, public examination, Joan's answer, primary) "offered to surrender the place in fifteen days un- less sooner relieved" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, scholarly) "we were told that La Hire was in confer- ence with the English Lord Suffolk" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Alençon's deposition, primary)
- 040.10 Event: Jargeau was sacked, the church with it, and prisoners were killed on the road to Orléans in a quarrel: most of them in Chartier, some in Lowell and Lang, who lay it to a quarrel among their captors, over the ransoms in Lang. agreed that prisoners were killed on the road; by author for how many. "Lesquelz prisonniers furent la pluspart tuez pour aucuns debatz qu'ilz firent sur au- cuns Franchois entre Gergeau et Orléans" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Jean Chartier, p. 65, primary) "the town was sacked, even to the church" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, scholarly) "Even some of the prisoners were butchered on the road to Or- leans, owing to a quarrel among their captors" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 9, scholarly) "whose French captors had quarrelled over the right to their ransoms" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, scholarly)
- 040.11 Event: on Wednesday 15 June the army left Orléans and took the fortified bridge of Meung by assault, leaving the town and castle of Meung to the English; the French held the bridge. agreed. "le mercredy, quinziesme jour d'icelluy mois de juin" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 174, primary) "fut prins de plain assault" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 174, primary) "partirent après disner et alèrent gésir auprès de Meun" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 13, primary) "The bridge-head of Meun, strongly fortified, as at Orleans, was taken by assault (June 15)" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, scholarly) "the Duke and the Maid marched from Orleans to near Meun" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 040.12 Event: on Thursday 16 June the French came before Beaugency and entered the town; the English had left it for the castle and the bridge, and men they left hidden in the houses fell on the French as they took their quarters; the guns were set up that night. agreed. "Le jeudy ensuivant xvie jour de juing" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 14, primary) "parce que les Anglois l'avoient desemparée , et s'es- toient retirez ou chasteau et sur le pont" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 174, primary) "the English abandoned the town and retired into a camp" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Alençon's deposition, primary) "next day attacked Beaugency, where Matthew Gough, a Welshman, was in command, as Talbot's lieutenant" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 040.13 Person: Matthew Gough, called Matago in the French chronicles, a Welsh captain, held Beaugency for the English with Sir Richard Gethin, bailli of Evreux, as Talbot's lieutenant; Cagny gives the two of them 400 English (his 'iinc', read as iiii c), Lowell fewer than a thousand men. agreed for the captains; by author for the numbers. "Matago accom- paigniez de iinc Englois avoient la garde de la place" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 14, primary) "Matthew Gough, a Welshman, was in command, as Talbot's lieutenant" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "As Gough had less than a thousand men" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly)
- 040.14 Event: Arthur de Richemont, Constable of France, came to the army before Beaugency though the king, at La Trémoille's urging, had forbidden it and Alençon had orders not to receive him. agreed. "combien que le roy ne vouloit point qu'il se meslast de sa guerre par l'enortement du sire de la Trimoille" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 14, primary) "because we had orders from the King not to receive the said Constable into our company" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Alençon's deposition, primary) "Charles had forbidden the consta- ble's approach" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly)
- 040.15 Event: the day Richemont reached Beaugency: Friday 17 June in Cagny and Ramsay; 16 June in Lang, who follows Gruel. disputed. "Le vendredy, le conte de Richemont , conestable de France, vint à la compaignie" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 14, primary) "On the 17th the French were reinforced by the Constable de Richemont" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "appeared (June 16) the formidable Constable" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, scholarly)
- 040.16 Figure: Richemont's force, by author: Cagny 500 or 600 fighting men; Guy de Laval reports the talk at Selles on 8 June that he was coming with 600 men-at-arms and 400 archers. disputed. "Le conestable ar- rivé, v ou vic combatans en sa compaignie" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 14, primary) "monseigneur le connestable vient avec six cens hommes d'armes, et quatre cens hommes de traict" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter of Guy de Laval, 8 June 1429, primary)
- 040.17 Event: how Joan and Richemont met is told two ways: in Gruel she knelt to him and he answered that he did not know whether she came from God or the devil; in the Chronique de la Pucelle he begged her pardon. Lowell holds both fantastical. disputed. "The story of the in- terview is told quite differently by his biographer and by the so-caUed Chronicler of the Maid" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "Both these accounts are fantastical" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "I know not whence you may be ; but if yon are of God I fear yon nought" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27 note, quoting Gruel, scholarly) "The Maid dismounted and embraced his knees" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, quoting Gruel, scholarly)
- 040.18 Event: the garrison of Beaugency yielded the castle and bridge on terms: to go with their lives, horses and harness, and, in the Journal, not to bear arms for ten days; they marched off through the Beauce, and the French entered. agreed. "se rendirent à la Pucelle et au duc d'Alençon sauf leurs corps, chevaux et hernois" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 15, primary) "qu'ilz jurèrent qu'ilz ne se armeroient que dix jours ne feussent passez" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 175, primary) "par condition que saulvement s'en yroient et emmenroient tous leurs biens" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 419, primary)
- 040.19 Event: when Beaugency yielded: the articles in the night of 17 to 18 June and the garrison gone on the morning of Saturday 18 June in Cagny, the Journal, Wavrin, Ramsay and Lowell; Lang, reading Gruel, puts the surrender at midnight on 16 to 17 June, or else on 17 to 18 June. disputed. "Gough that night signed articles, under which the French took possession next morning at sunrise (Saturday, 18th June)" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "qui fut dix-huictiesme jour" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 175, primary) "le samedy au matin se dé- partirent les Anglois" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 419, primary) "At about midnight on Fri- day Gough capitulated" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "the midnight of June 16-17 apparently" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, scholarly)
- 040.20 Event: the English relief army under Sir John Fastolf left Paris early in June, left its supplies at Étampes, and halted at Janville on hearing that Jargeau had fallen; Lord Talbot rode in from Beaugency to join it. agreed. "entra en la ville le seigneur de Thalboth, à tout environ quarante lanches et deux cens archiers" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 414, primary) "avoient laissé h Estampes les vivres et artil- lerie" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 176, primary) "fall of Jargeau they halted at Janville" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly) "had left Paris early in June under the command of Sir John Fastolf, and on the day that Jargeau was taken it had reached Janville" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly)
- 040.21 Event: at Janville Fastolf advised against going on, the English being disheartened, and Talbot swore he would fight with only his own men if need be; Fastolf gave way. single. Wavrin was present; Monstrelet's text is the same account, and Ramsay, Lowell and Lang follow it. "lequel dist que s'il n'avoit que sa genl et ceulx qui le volroient ensievir, si les yroit il combatre" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 415, primary) "Fastolf strongly urged a retreat ; but Talbot would not listen to him" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 040.22 Figure: the English army of Talbot and Fastolf, by author: Wavrin, who marched with it, about 5,000 leaving Paris; the Journal 4,000; Chartier 4,000 to 5,000; Monstrelet four or five thousand sent by Bedford; Alençon a thousand men-at-arms, which Lang reads as about 5,000 men in all. disputed. "environ v mil combatans, aussi bien prins que j'eusse oncques veu" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 413, primary) "combien qu'ilz feussent quatre mil combatans" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 176, primary) "jusques au nombre de quatre à chincq mille" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Jean Chartier, p. 67, primary) "sent in haste for four or five thousand men" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 60, primary) "to the number of a thousand men-at-arms" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Alençon's deposition, primary) "Fastolf s com- mand would be of about 5000 men, the number at which it is usually reckoned" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, scholarly)
- 040.23 Event: on 17 June the English came within a league of Beaugency and found the French drawn up on a small hill; they did not attack, turned back to Meung, took the town, and battered the bridge, still held by the French, through the night. agreed. "se rengèrent et misrent en battaille sur une petite montaignette" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 417, primary) "Et s'en retournèrent au pont de Meung, et l'assaillirent moult asprement" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 176, primary) "Posted on a small hill in front of Beaugency was the body of the French army" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly)
- 040.24 Event: before the hill the English dismounted, the archers set their stakes, and two heralds offered three knights to fight any who came down; the French told them to lodge for the night and they would see them closer in the morning. single. Wavrin, an eyewitness; Lowell and Lang follow him. "disans qu'ilz estoient trois chevalliers quy les combalroient" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 417, primary) "the heralds were answered 'that it was too late, and that the English had better encamp for the night" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly)
- 040.25 Event: on the morning of Saturday 18 June, about eight by Wavrin and nine by Lowell, as they made ready to storm the bridge of Meung, the English learned that Beaugency had fallen; they gave up Meung and marched off for Janville, van, artillery and supplies, main body under Fastolf and Talbot, and a rearguard of English. agreed. "environ huict heures du matin que les capittaines eurent oy messe" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 420, primary) "The news of the surrender of Beaugency reached Tal- bot about nine o'clock on Saturday morning" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly) "L'advant-garde se mist premiers à chemin" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 421, primary) "habandonnèrent la place et s'en alèrent tous ensemble droit à Yanville" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 15, primary) "voulans aler à Yenville" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 176, primary)
- 040.26 Event: asked by Alençon what to do, Joan told the captains to have good spurs, for the English would not stand and they would need spurs to follow them. single. Dunois's deposition of 1456; Ramsay and Lowell set it on 18 June, Lang thinks it was said on 17 June. "Have all of you good spurs ?" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Dunois's deposition, primary) "Though the most learned of the historians of the Maid places this dialogue on the morning of June 18, from the context of the evidence it rather appears to be a saying of June 17" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, scholarly)
- 040.27 Event: the French vanguard in the pursuit was led by La Hire and Poton de Xaintrailles with other mounted captains, the main body following under Alençon and Dunois; Joan was not in the van, to her anger. Wavrin and Monstrelet put the Constable and Boussac in the van too. agreed. "esleurent La Ilire, Poton, Jamet de Tilloy" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 177, primary) "On this day La Hire commanded the vanguard, at which Jeanne was much vexed" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, deposition of Louis de Contes, primary) "Si furent adont ordonnez le connestable de France , le marissal de Bousac, La Hyre, Pothon" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 419, primary) "the French van under La Hire and Pothon de Xaintrailles surrounded Talbot and overwhelmed him" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 040.28 Event: the French scouts found the English when a stag broke from the woods and ran into their ranks, and the English raised a shout. single. Wavrin; Monstrelet's text is the same, and Ramsay, Lowell and France follow it. "veyrent ung cherf partir hors des bois" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 422, primary) "put up a stag, which ran straight for the army" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 61, primary)
- 040.29 Battle: Patay, 18 June 1429, French against English: the French vanguard caught the English on the march before they were formed, overran Talbot, who held a passage between two hedges with 500 picked archers, and the English van, seeing Fastolf ride towards it, fled; the French won with small loss. Wavrin, Quicherat 4 pp. 419-424, gives the English order and movements from inside the English army; the Journal pp. 176-178 and Cagny p. 15 give the French vanguard and main body. agreed. "dist qu'il descenderoit à pie à tout v cens archiers d'eslite" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 422, primary) "les François s'estoient feruz à l'estroit passage où estoit le seigneur de Thalbot" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 423, primary) "que lesdits Anglois n'eurent pas le loisir de se mettre en bataille" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Gruel, p. 319, primary) "L'avant-garde de noz gens férit dedens" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Cagny, p. 15, primary) "Talbot ordered his advance guard, with the wagons and artillery, to take position near Patay, behind some stout hedges" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 10, scholarly)
- 040.30 Event: the English archers were overrun before they could plant their stakes. single. Monstrelet; Wavrin's matching passage does not name the stakes, and Oman and France tell it after Monstrelet. "before they had time to fortify themselves, as usual, with sharp-pointed stakes in their front" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 61, primary) "the archers had not even fixed their stakes when the hostile cavalry hurtled in among them" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly)
- 040.31 Event: Talbot was taken at Patay, with Lord Scales, Sir Thomas Rempston and a Hungerford, a lord in Monstrelet and Sir Walter Hungerford the younger in Ramsay. agreed. "avoient les François rué jus le seigneur de Thalbot, lui prins prisonnier" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Wavrin, p. 423, primary) "le seigneur de Talebot, le seigneur d'Escalles, messire Thomas Rameston" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Journal du siège, p. 177, primary) "Without great difficulty the English were beaten and slain, and Talbot made prisoner" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Alençon's deposition, primary) "Among the prisoners taken were the Lords Talbot and Scales, Sir Walter Hun- gerford the younger, and Sir Thomas Remston" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 27, scholarly)
- 040.32 Person: Talbot's captors: the archers of Poton in Gruel; La Hire and Poton in France. disputed.
