"In 1423 he made his fortune by the capture from the French of La Charite on the Loire" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, p. 183, scholarly) "for Burgundy by Gressart, commanding in La Charite" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 18, p. 214, scholarly) "arrested at La Charité sur Loire by Perrinet Gressart, the captain who vainly fought" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of Georges de La Trémoille, scholarly) "ron la ville de la Charité qui estoit tenue par les Bour-" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1429, p. 172, primary) "had been made bailiff of St. Pierre le Moustier" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, p. 183, scholarly)
- 050.34 Border: autumn 1429, the towns of the upper Loire and the Allier held against Charles between Berry and the duchy of Burgundy: La Charité, Cosne and Saint-Pierre-le-Moûtier among them. Given as a list of towns. agreed. "mesmementde La Charité, Gosne et autres" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter of Albret to Riom, 9 November 1429, primary) "most necessary to recover the town of La Charit6, which was held by the enemy" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, testimony of d'Aulon, p. 317, primary) "At the western frontier of the duchy of Burgundy, on the Loire and the Allier, were several fortified towns held by soldiers of fortune." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, p. 182, scholarly)
- 050.35 Event: the council at Mehun-sur-Yèvre resolves to take La Charité, and Saint-Pierre-le-Moûtier first; Joan gathers her force at Bourges and goes with Albret to besiege Saint-Pierre at the end of October 1429 (on or soon after 25 October, Lang). agreed. "the Maid went to the town of Bourges, in which she assembled her forces" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, testimony of d'Aulon, p. 318, primary) "Toward the end of October she left Bourges, and marched with Albret against St. Pierre le Moustier." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, p. 185, scholarly) "The siege of St. Pierre le Moustier seems to have begun on or shortly after October 25." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 18, p. 216, scholarly) "Et, le roy estant en sa ville de Bourges, elle print aucuns des capitaines" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1429, p. 172, primary)
- 050.36 Event: Saint-Pierre-le-Moûtier is taken by storm: the first assault is beaten back; d'Aulon, wounded in the heel, finds Joan left with four or five men and is told she has fifty thousand of her people with her; she has faggots and hurdles brought to bridge the ditch and the town is taken without great resistance; she stops the soldiers pillaging the church. agreed for the taking by assault (her letter to Riom, Albret's letter, d'Aulon, Thierry); single (d'Aulon) for the course and her words. "la ville de Saint-Pierre le Monstier a esté prinse d'assault" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter to Riom, 9 November 1429, primary) "she had yet in her company fifty thousand of her people, and that she would not leave until she had taken the town" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, testimony of d'Aulon, p. 318, primary) "she had not with her more than four or five men" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, testimony of d'Aulon, p. 318, primary) "faggots and hurdles brought to make a bridge over the trenches of the town" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, testimony of d'Aulon, p. 318, primary) "the soldiers wanted to pillage the Church and to seize the sacred vessels" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, testimony of Reginald Thierry, p. 246, primary) "We know about the Maid's brilliant success at St. Pierre le Moustier only from the evidence of d'Aulon." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 18, p. 216, scholarly)
- 050.37 Event: the date of the taking of Saint-Pierre-le-Moûtier: before 9 November 1429, when Joan and Albret write from Moulins that it has been taken; about 1 November (Lowell); in November (Beaucourt). agreed for "taken by 9 November". The Wikipedia article's 4 November rests on a site that does not count. "Escript à Molins, le neufviesme jour de novembre." (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter to Riom, primary) "About November 1 St. Pierre le Moustier was thus taken" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, p. 187, scholarly) "Après la prise deSaint-Pierre-le-Moutier (novembre), on alla atiaquer La Charité" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6, p. 239, scholarly) "the town was eventually taken by assault on November 4, 1429" (Siege_of_Saint-Pierre-le-Moûtier.wiki, lead, wikipedia)
- 050.38 Event: the powder spent at Saint-Pierre leaves them short for La Charité: on 7 November Joan and Albret ask Clermont for powder, arrows and artillery, and Clermont sends two quintals of saltpetre, one of sulphur and two cases of arrows, with a sword, two daggers and a battle-axe for her; on 9 November she writes from Moulins to Riom for powder, saltpetre, sulphur, arrows and crossbows. agreed. "deux quintaux de saupestre, un quintal seuphre, deux quaysses de traict" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, note of the town of Clermont, 7 November 1429, primary) "vueillez incontinant envoyer et aider pour ledit siège" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter to Riom, 9 November 1429, primary) "she and d'Albret then sent to the town of Clermont, asking for ammunition to attack La Charite" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 18, p. 216, scholarly)
- 050.39 Event: the siege of La Charité, from about 10 November 1429: Joan, Albret and Montpensier, joined by Marshal Boussac, sit before the town about a month in the cold, short of money and supplies; an assault is made, in which a baron of Dauphiné, Raymon de Montremur, is killed (the Berry herald); the king sends neither food nor money, Bourges is asked for 1,300 crowns on 24 November, and the siege is raised with the loss of artillery (bombards and artillery, the Berry herald; the greater part of it, as was said, Chartier; some, Lang). agreed. "et là lurent environ ung mois et se levèrent honteusement" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Berry herald, p. 49, primary) "y morut à ung assault ung baron du pais du Daulphiné" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Berry herald, p. 49, primary) "Devant laquelle se tindrent par aulcun temps et en la fin se levèrent" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Jean Chartier, p. 91, primary) "la somme de treize cens escuz" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, levy of the town of Bourges for the siege of La Charité, 24 November 1429, p. 356, primary) "où Jehanne la Pucelle, Mgr. de Montpensier et nous, alons présentement mettre le siégé" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter of Albret to Riom, 9 November 1429, primary) "parceque le roy ne fist finance de lui envoyer vivres ne argent pour entretenir sa compaignie, luy convint lever son siège" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1429, p. 172, primary) "she had an assault made, but she neither threw nor sprinkled holy water" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination of 3 March, primary) "the said Catherine did not advise her to go, and it was too cold, and she would not go" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination of 3 March, primary) "It lasted about a month" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, p. 190, scholarly) "On November 24, as we have seen, the people of Bourges were asked for 1300 gold crowns" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 18, p. 218, scholarly) "they lost some of their artillery, for the King sent no money and supplies" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 18, p. 218, scholarly)
- 050.40 Event: the day the siege of La Charité was raised. open: no source read gives it; Moranvillé, Cagny's editor, puts it at the end of November or the start of December; Lowell has the siege last about a month from about 10 November; Beaucourt has Joan at Jargeau on Christmas Day. The Wikipedia infobox's 24 November to 25 December has no source that counts. "Fin de novembre ou commencement de décembre." (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Moranvillé's note, p. 172, primary) "About November 10 the army marched and sat down before the place. The details of the siege are almost alto- gether unknown." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, p. 190, scholarly) "Nous la trouvons le jour de Noël à Jargeau" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6, p. 240, scholarly)
- 050.41 Event: La Charité was not taken: Gressart held it for Burgundy after the siege and in 1430; the story that he sold it in January 1430 for the money of Bourges is called impossible by Lowell and a fable by Lang. The Wikipedia infobox's "delivered to Charles VII against a huge ransom" cites a flag website, which does not count. agreed. "luy convint lever son siège et s'en départir à grant desplaisance" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1429, p. 172, primary) "the story is impossible" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 14, p. 191 n., scholarly) "the story is a manifest fable" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 18, p. 218, scholarly) "the city delivered to Charles VII against a huge ransom" (Siege_of_La_Charité.wiki, infobox, wikipedia)
- 050.42 Event: in December 1429 (on 29 December, Thurston) at Mehun-sur-Yèvre Charles VII ennobles Joan and her family. agreed for December; single (Thurston) for the day. "Charles, on 29 December, 1429, ennobled the Maid and all her family" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "C'est là qu'au mois de décembre, Charles VII, qui n'avait cessé de lui yrodiguer ses dons" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 6, p. 239, scholarly) "but her family were ennobled by Patent" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 28, p. 416 n., scholarly)
- 050.43 Event: the truce with Burgundy, prolonged, runs out at Easter (16 April) 1430, and Philip takes the field on the Oise as soon as Easter is past. agreed. "for the truce between the Burgundians and French did not expire until Easter" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 72, primary) "The truce was over at Easter" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 15, p. 201 n., scholarly) "as soon as Easter (16th April) was passed, and was advancing in force along the line of the Oise" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 28, p. 416, scholarly)
- 050.44 Event: at the end of March 1430 (between 28 March and 1 April, Moranvillé; at the very end of March or the first days of April, Lowell) Joan leaves the court at Sully-sur-Loire without the king's leave, as if going for an outing, with her small household. agreed. "le roy estant en la ville de Sulli sur Laire" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 173, primary) "sans le sceu du roy, ne prendre congé de lui" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 173, primary) "Entre le 28 mars et le" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Moranvillé's note, p. 173, primary) "she left Sully quietly without leave" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 15, p. 200, scholarly) "she had slipped away from Court at Sully" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 28, p. 416, scholarly)
- 050.45 Event: Melun, held by the English and then the Burgundians since Henry V took it, expels its garrison about Easter 1430 and comes over to Charles; whether Joan was there before it rose is not known (Lowell). agreed. "This happened about Easter, April 16, and Joan was thus able to cross the Seine without trouble." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 15, p. 201, scholarly) "for Melun had come over to King Charles in April" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 19, p. 226, scholarly) "had added Melun to the list of their conquests" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 28, p. 416, scholarly)
- 050.46 Event: in Easter week 1430 (16 to 23 April), in the trenches at Melun, her voices, Saint Catherine and Saint Margaret, tell her she will be taken before Saint John's Day (24 June), and tell her so nearly every day after, but not when. single (her answer of 10 March 1431, the court's record; Lowell and Ramsay follow it). "in Easter week last, when she was in the trenches at Melun, she was told by her voices, namely by St. Catherine and St. Margaret, that she would be captured before St. John's Day" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination in prison of 10 March, primary) "Asked if since Melun she had been told by her voices that she would be taken, she answered yes, several times, nearly every day." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination in prison of 10 March, primary)
- 050.47 Event: from Melun she goes to Lagny-sur-Marne, whose garrison makes war on the English of Paris. agreed. "pour ce que ceulx de la place fesoient bonne guerre aux Englois de Paris" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 173, primary) "Joan rode on from Melun to cross the Marne at Lagny" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 15, p. 202, scholarly)
- 050.48 Event: near Lagny, Joan and the French of the garrisons defeat and take Franquet d'Arras, a Burgundian captain, returning from a raid; the date is disputed: early May 1430 (Monstrelet; May, Champion), the end of April (Lowell, who takes the Bourgeois's raid of 21 April on Chelles and La Chasse for the same affair). disputed for the date. "At the beginning of the month of May, a valiant man-at-arms named Franquet of Arras" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 84, primary) "Le XXIe jour d'avril, allerent bien IIIc Angloys ou environ pour cuider prendre ung chastel nommé la Chasse" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, § 530, primary) "and Cochon put it at the end of April; Monstrelet early in May" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 15, p. 203 n., scholarly) "They were defeated by the Maid and her company in May, 1430, and put to the sword." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of Franquet d'Arras, scholarly)
- 050.49 Figure: Franquet's company about 300 (Monstrelet, Chastellain, Champion; the Bourgeois's raiders about 300 English), Joan's 400 (Monstrelet, Chastellain after him); Cagny has 300 to 400 English put to death. agreed for Franquet's 300; single for Joan's 400 and Cagny's dead. "with about three hundred combatants toward Lagny sur Mame" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 84, primary) "by Joan the Maid and four hundred French" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 84, primary) "en nombre de iiic ou environ" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 442, primary) "là furent mis à mort de iij à iiijc Englois" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 173, primary)
- 050.50 Event: the fight: Franquet's dismounted archers hold off Joan's first attacks; she sends for the garrison of Lagny and the places round, who come with culverins and crossbows; Franquet's men are killed or taken and he is captured. agreed. "sent hastily for succours from the garrisons of Lagny and other castles" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 84, primary) "les Arminalx eulx assemblerent des garnisons d'entour et les encloyrent entre le chastel et eulx" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, § 530, primary) "manda astivemenl à Laigny loute la garnison" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 443, primary)
- 050.51 Event: Franquet is tried for about a fortnight before the bailiff of Senlis and a jury of Lagny, confesses himself a murderer, thief and traitor, and is beheaded; Joan had asked to exchange him for the landlord of the Bear Inn in Paris, and gave him up to justice when she heard the landlord was dead. agreed for the beheading; single (her answer of 14 March 1431) for the trial and the exchange. "The Maid even caused Franquet to be beheaded" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 84, primary) "fut décapité après par la erudélité de ceste femme qui desiroit sa mort" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 443, primary) "his trial lasted a fortnight; and he had for judges the Bailly de Senlis and a jury of the people of Lagny" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination of 14 March, primary) "she had asked to have Franquet exchanged for a man from Paris, the" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination of 14 March, primary)
- 050.52 Person: Franquet d'Arras, a captain of partisans of the Burgundian party. agreed. "a valiant man-at-arms named Franquet of Arras, attached to the duke of Burgundy" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 84, primary) "Franquet d'Arras, was a captain of guerrillas" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of Franquet d'Arras, scholarly)
- 050.53 Event: in May 1430 Philip of Burgundy takes the castle of Choisy-sur-Oise, held by Louis de Flavy for his brother, razes it and bridges the Oise to come at Compiègne from the side of Montdidier. agreed. "to lay siege to the castle of Choisy sur Oise, in which was Louis de Flavy" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 83, primary) "The diU« built a bridge over the Oise" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 83, primary) "Philip immediately demolished the castle even to its foundation" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 16, p. 214, scholarly)
- 050.54 Event: Soissons, held for Charles by a captain bought by Burgundy, shuts its gates on the army of Joan, the archbishop and Vendôme (18 May, Lang), and is sold to Burgundy weeks later; the captains go to Senlis and leave Compiègne to itself. agreed for the refusal; single (Lang, after Champion's document) for the captain's name, Guichard Bournel, and the price of 4,000 saluts. "The officer in command of the place, being bribed by the duke, who was negotiating for its surrender, refused to admit the army" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 16, p. 213, scholarly) "Some weeks after their departure from Soissons its treacherous captain sold his charge to the duke of Burgundy" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 16, p. 214, scholarly) "Bournel, claiming the privilege of the good town, refused to allow the army to enter, and then sold the town to Burgundy for 4000 salus" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 20, p. 236, scholarly)
- 050.55 Event: the besiegers' posts, 21 or 22 May 1430, all on the right bank of the Oise opposite Compiègne: the Duke at Coudun, a league from the town; John of Luxembourg, Count of Ligny, at Clairoix; Baudot de Noyelle at Margny at the end of the causeway from the bridge; the English under Montgomery in the meadows at Venette. agreed. "he quartered himself in the fortress of Coudun, within a league of Compiegne, and sir John de Luxembourg was lodged in Claroi" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 83, primary) "on the causeway of Marigny, and the lord Montgomery and his men were quartered along the meadows of La Venette" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 83, primary) "le duc venu logier à Coudun, le conte de Liney à Claroy" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 441, primary) "the English were at Venette, a mile or two" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 16, p. 215, scholarly) "Clairoy, Margny-le-Petit and Venette" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 28, p. 417, scholarly)
- 050.56 Event: Joan, at Crépy-en-Valois, hears that Burgundy and the Earl of Arundel besiege Compiègne, rides out about midnight with her company, saying they are enough, and enters Compiègne at sunrise on 23 May. disputed: Cagny, Lowell and Lang have her come in at dawn on the day of the sortie; Chastellain has her enter by night and rest two nights before it. "Environ mienuit, elle partit dudit lieu de Crespy" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 174, primary) "Par « mon martin, nous suymes assez; je iray voir mes « bons amis de Compiegne" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 174, primary) "Elle arriva audit lieu environ soleil levant" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 174, primary) "She left Crepy at midnight, and reached Compiegne in safety at the dawn of Tuesday, May 23." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 16, p. 216, scholarly) "toit entrée par nuit dedens Compiègne. Laquelle, après y avoir reposé deux nuis" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 443, primary)
- 050.57 Figure: the company she brought from Crépy. disputed: 300 to 400 combatants, Cagny; Bartolomeo Baretta's band of about 200, Lowell and Lang. "with her company of 300 or 400 combatants (really with about half that number at most" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 20, p. 237, scholarly) "had about two hundred men in his command" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 16, p. 215, scholarly)
- 050.58 Event: the day of the sortie and the capture. disputed: 23 May 1430, in the Duke of Burgundy's letter to Saint-Quentin written that day at Coudun (about six in the afternoon), in Cagny and in the Bourgeois, followed by Lowell, Lang and Beaucourt; the eve of the Ascension, 24 May, in Monstrelet and Chastellain, followed by Ramsay, who notes the letter's date, and by Tuetey's note to the Bourgeois. "XXI II* de may, enrirpn six heures après midi" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter of the Duke of Burgundy to Saint-Quentin, primary) "Escript à Codun emprès Compiengne, le xxiii* jour de may" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter of the Duke of Burgundy to Saint-Quentin, primary) "En l'an m CGCC xxx, le xxiij jour dudit mois de" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 174, primary) "le XXIIIe jour de may, fut prinse devant Compigne dame Jehanne" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, § 536, primary) "in the afternoon, on Ajcension-eTe" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 86, primary) "par une vigille de l'Ascension" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 445, primary) "About five o'clock on the afternoon of the same 24th May" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 28, p. 417, scholarly) "Yet the Duke of Burgundy's letters announcing Jeanne's capture are dated 23rd May" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 28, p. 416 n., scholarly) "C'est le mercredi 24 mai 1430, veille de l'Ascension" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Tuetey's note 954, primary)
- 050.59 Event: the sortie: late in the afternoon (about four, Chastellain and Lowell; about five, Monstrelet) Joan rides out over the bridge and through the boulevard at its far end against Baudot de Noyelle's post at Margny; John of Luxembourg, riding over from Clairoix with Créqui and a few gentlemen, calls up his men; she drives them back twice to the Burgundian camp and a third time to the middle of the road; the English from Venette, near 500, cut her off from the bridge, and the French fall back. Cagny tells it otherwise: a skirmish in the meadow, and a Burgundian ambush laid behind the hill of Clairoix (Mont de Claire) that rode between her and the bridge. agreed for the course; Cagny's ambush is single. "she went over the bridge and through the boulevard" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination in prison of 10 March, primary) "twice drove them as far as the camp of the Burgundians, and the third time to the middle of the highway. Then the English who were there cut off the road from her and her company" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination in prison of 10 March, primary) "with the intent to sttick tbe post of sir Baudo de Noielle, at the end of the causeway of Marigny" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 86, primary) "formed themselves into battle-array against the French, and were near five hundred mei" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 86, primary) "entour quatre heures après midy, saillit dehors la ville" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 445, primary) "midrent les Bourguignons une grosse embusche de leurs gens en la couverture d'une grant montaigne près d'illec" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 174, primary) "At four o'clock or thereabouts in the long May after- noon" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 16, p. 217, scholarly)
- 050.60 Figure: the strength of the sortie, 500 to 600 horse and foot, Monstrelet; 500 armed men, Chastellain; about 500, Lowell. single (the Burgundian chroniclers). "from five to six hundred combatants horse and foot" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 86, primary) "en nombre de vc armez" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 445, primary)
- 050.61 Event: as the enemy press on to the end of the bridge, the captain of Compiègne raises the bridge and shuts the gate for fear of losing the town, and Joan is left outside with few of her men, in the fields on the Picardy side by the boulevard, with only the river and the boulevard and its ditch between her and the town. agreed. "fist lever le pont de la ville et fermer la porte" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 175, primary) "et ainssi demoura la Pucellc enfermée dehors et poy de ses gens avecques elle" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 176, primary) "retreating to the fields on the Picardy side near the boulevard, was taken; and between the place where she was captured and Compiègne there was nothing but the river and the boulevard with its ditch" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination in prison of 10 March, primary) "Seeing the barriers shut, and that she could not retreat directly into Compiegne" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 16, p. 219, scholarly)
- 050.62 Person: Guillaume de Flavy, captain of Compiègne for Charles VII, half-brother of Regnault of Chartres; the sources read find no treason in his shutting the gate (Cagny, Champion, Ramsay), and the Wikipedia article reports the question as one scholars still debate (DeVries). agreed. "Le témoignage de Perceval de Cagny écarte de sa mémoire toute idée de trahison." (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Moranvillé's note, p. 175, primary) "It would be more unwise still to regard Guillaume de Flavy, Regnault's half-brother, as a traitor abandoning the Maid before his besieged city." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of Regnault de Chartres, scholarly) "The facts raise no prima facie case of treachery against Guillaume de Flavy" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 28, p. 417 n., scholarly) "The next moments remain a source of scholarly debate." (Siege_of_Compiègne.wiki, Capture of Joan of Arc, wikipedia)
- 050.63 Event: the capture: an archer pulls her from her horse by her cloth-of-gold huque, and she gives her faith to the Bastard of Wandonne, a man-at-arms of John of Luxembourg's company, who takes her to Margny; Poton le Bourguignon, her brother and her steward are taken with her (Chastellain). Cagny has five or six lay hands on her and her horse and she answers that she has given her faith to another, and she is led to John of Luxembourg's lodging. agreed. "la tira du cheval toute platte à terre" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 447, primary) "ung homme d'armes, nommé le bastard de Wandonne" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 447, primary) "the Maid was dragged frxim her hone bja archer, near to whom was the bastard de Vendome, and to him she surrendered and pledged her faith" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 86, primary) "We know that the Bastard of Wandomme, who took Jeanne prisoner, served in the company of this captain" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of Jean de Luxembourg, scholarly) "Je ay juré et baillé ma foy à autre que à vous" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 176, primary) "An archer seized her by the leg, unhorsed her, and she was taken" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 28, p. 417, scholarly)
- 050.64 Person: Lionel, the Bastard of Wandonne, in whose troop the archer served. single (Lowell gives the first name; the Wikipedia article gives "Lionel, Bastard of Vendôme" after Pernoud and Clin). "Lionel of Wandonne, in whose troop the archer served" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 17, p. 224, scholarly)
- 050.65 Person: John of Luxembourg, Count of Ligny, lord of Beaurevoir, a Burgundian captain, younger brother of Louis of Luxembourg, the chancellor of Henry VI; he commands at Clairoix and Joan is given into his keeping that evening. agreed. "Jean de Luxembourg, Lord of Beaurevoir, comte de Ligny, was the younger brother of Cardinal de Luxembourg, Chancellor of England." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion's sketch of Jean de Luxembourg, scholarly) "leaving the Maid iw the guard of sir John de Luxembourg" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 86, primary) "Messire Jehan de Lucembourc la fist tenir en son logis iij ou iiij jours" (archive.org-download-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-chroniquesdeperc00cagnuoft-djvu.txt.txt, 1430, p. 176, primary)
- 050.66 Event: the Duke of Burgundy comes from Coudun too late for the fight, goes to see her and speaks with her; Monstrelet, who was there, does not remember what was said. agreed. "but what they were I do not now recollect" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 86, primary) "eut aveuques elle aulcuns langages qui ne sont pas venus jusques à moy" (archive.org-download-procsdecondam04joanuoft-procsdecondam04joanuoft-djvu.txt.txt, Chastellain, p. 447, primary)
- 050.67 Figure: the losses of the sortie. disputed: about 400 of Joan's men killed or drowned, the Bourgeois; many taken, drowned and killed, with fewer than twenty of the besiegers wounded and none killed or taken, the Duke's letter; those taken with her few in number, Monstrelet. "furent bien IIIIc des hommes à la Pucelle que tuez que noyez" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, § 536, primary) "eu de noz gens bleciez vint personnes" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter of the Duke of Burgundy to Saint-Quentin, primary) "With her was token Poton the Burgundian, and some others" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 86, primary)
- 050.68 Event: the story that on the morning of 23 May, in the church of Saint-Jacques at Compiègne, she told the townsfolk and children that she had been sold and betrayed: it was taken down in 1498 from two old men, and Lowell and Lang doubt it. single (Bouchart's story of 1498). "The story was taken down in 1498." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 16, p. 216 n., scholarly) "It is hardly worth while to criticise a story of May 23, gleaned in 1498 from the lips of two men over ninety" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, ch. 20, p. 237, scholarly)
- 050.69 Battle: Compiègne, 23 May 1430 (card): the sortie of Joan and part of the garrison of Compiègne against the Burgundian army of Philip the Good, with its English contingent, besieging the town; the sortie is driven back and Joan taken. The positions of both sides and the course of the sortie are in Monstrelet (book 2 ch. 83 and 86), Chastellain (Quicherat 4, p. 441-447), her own account (Barrett, the examination of 10 March), Cagny (1430, p. 174-176), Lowell ch. 16 (p. 215-220) and Lang ch. 20; Monstrelet and Chastellain stood in the Burgundian camp. agreed. "with those of her company she attacked the followers of lord Jean de Luxembourg" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the examination in prison of 10 March, primary) "icelle appellée la Pucelle a esté prise" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, letter of the Duke of Burgundy to Saint-Quentin, primary) "The French re-entered Compiigne doleful and vexed at their losses" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, book 2 ch. 86, primary)

## 060 rouen

Coverage: primary and scholarly. The trial of condemnation is read in Barrett's translation of the record (with Champion's sketches of Cauchon, Le Maistre and John of Luxembourg in the same file) and in Murray's, with Murray's depositions of 1450-1456 for the witnesses of the prison, Saint-Ouen and the Old Market (Toutmouille, Isambart de La Pierre, Ladvenu, Massieu, Taquel, Leparmentier, Guesdon). Quicherat 5 gives the documents of the price and the pay: the Norman levy of September 1430, a receipt of 6 December 1430 for the king's ten thousand livres, and the king's taxation of twenty sous a day to the Paris masters. Monstrelet A copies Henry VI's letter to the Duke of Burgundy on the sentence, and the Bourgeois tells the burning as it was reported in Paris. Ramsay 1 ch. 29, Lowell ch. 17-24, Lang ch. 21-26, France vol. 2 ch. 8-14 and Thurston follow the record and the documents with notes. The Wikipedia articles of the section were opened for the order of events and no line rests on them. The coast road from Le Crotoy by Saint-Valery, Eu and Dieppe is told only by Père Ignace's history of 1657, which Quicherat 5 prints with a warning and France follows (060.24). No modern study of the trial was read (the Caen and Rennes volumes are behind a bot wall, see sources.md "Unread").

- 060.1 Person: John of Luxembourg, lord of Beaurevoir and count of Ligny, the Burgundian captain before Compiègne, holds her: she was taken by men of the Bastard of Wandomme, his follower, who turned her over to him. agreed. "by men serving under the Bastard of Wandomme" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "We know that the Bastard of Wandomme, who took Jeanne prisoner, served in the company of this captain: he turned Jeanne over to Jean de Luxembourg" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion, Partisans of England, Jean de Luxembourg, scholarly) "Jeanne remained in the custody of Messire Jean de Luxembourg, to whom she belonged henceforward." (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 7, scholarly)
- 060.2 Event: after three or four days at Clairoix, May 1430, John of Luxembourg sends her under escort to his castle of Beaulieu in the Vermandois, near Noyon, some twenty miles north-east of Compiègne (Lowell). agreed for Beaulieu near Noyon; single for the days at Clairoix (Lang) and for the distance (Lowell). "After keeping Jeanne for three or four days at Clairoix, Jean de Luxembourg sent her to the castle of Beaulieu in the Vermandois" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "who sent her first to Beaulieu near Noyon, and thence to Beaurevoir, near Saint-Quentin, in the Vermandois" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "Joan was sent under strong escort some twenty miles northeast of Compiegne to Beaulieu, a stronghold" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 17, scholarly)
- 060.3 Event: at Beaulieu she nearly escapes, slipping out "between two pieces of wood" of her tower and about to shut her keepers in, until the porter stops her. single (her own answer at the trial, 15 March 1431; Lowell and Lang tell it from her). "how she expected to escape from the castle of Beaulieu between two pieces of wood: she answered she was never a prisoner in any place but she would gladly escape; and being in the castle she would have shut up her keepers in the tower, had not the porter seen and encountered her" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Thursday, March 15th, in prison, primary) "While she was at Beaulieu, the negotiations for her purchase nearly had an unexpected end. Joan almost escaped." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly) "I would have locked up my guardian in a certain tower, but the porter saw me and stopped me." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly)
- 060.4 Figure: how long she was at Beaulieu and at Beaurevoir. disputed: she said at the trial that she was about four months in the tower of Beaurevoir; Lang has Beaulieu not more than a fortnight and Beaurevoir from the beginning of June to the end of September; Ramsay, from a document of Quicherat 5, has Beaurevoir August to November and so Beaulieu all June and July; France has the move early in August; Champion has her at Beaurevoir in August; Lowell says some weeks at Beaulieu. "Asked if she was long in the tower of Beaurevoir, she answered that she was there about four months." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the public examinations, 3 March, primary) "Jeanne d'Arc was kept in his castle of Beaurevoir during the month of August." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion, Jean de Luxembourg, scholarly) "She was now removed to Beaurevoir Castle, forty miles north of Beaulieu, where she lay between the beginning of June and the end of September." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "Jeanne was at Beaurevoir August-November ; Proces, v. 38a. If so she must have been at Beaulieu all June and July." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29 note 1, scholarly) "Early in August, the Sire de Luxembourg had the Maid taken from Beaulieu, which was not safe enough, to Beaurevoir, near Cambrai." (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 9, scholarly) "Little is known of Joan's life at Beaulieu, where she was kept for some weeks." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly)
- 060.5 Event: Beaurevoir, John of Luxembourg's principal castle, near Saint-Quentin in the Vermandois (Ramsay), in Picardy (Lowell), near Cambrai (France): high walls, great towers and a moat; she is held in one of the towers. agreed for the place; single (Lowell) for "his principal castle" and for the walls, towers and moat. "removed her to Beaurevoir, his principal castle. This was in Picardy, a fortress recently built or enlarged, with high walls, great towers, and a deep moat." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly) "who sent her first to Beaulieu near Noyon, and thence to Beaurevoir, near Saint-Quentin, in the Vermandois" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly)
- 060.6 Person: the ladies of Beaurevoir, John of Luxembourg's aunt Jeanne, the Demoiselle of Luxembourg, his wife Jeanne de Béthune, and (in Lang) her daughter Jeanne de Bar, offer her a woman's dress or cloth for one; she answers that she has not God's leave and it is not yet time. agreed. "She said the Demoiselle of Luxembourg and the Lady of Beaurevoir offered her a woman's dress, or the cloth to make one, and told her to wear it; and she replied she had not God's permission, and it was not yet time." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the public examinations, 3 March, primary) "the aunt of Jean de Luxembourg, Jeanne, who was old and near her death ; Jeanne de Bethune, Vicomtesse de Meaux, his wife ; and her daughter by a former marriage, Jeanne de Bar." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "At this time Luxemburg's wife and aunt were living in Beaurevoir. Some twelve years earlier he had married Joanna of Bethune, the rich widow of Robert of Bar." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly)
- 060.7 Event: the Demoiselle of Luxembourg begs her nephew not to hand Joan to the English. single (Joan's words in a recapitulation of the evidence, as Lang gives them; Lowell and Champion hedge it). "begged Jean de Luxembourg not to hand me over to the English." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "It seems that the demoiselle of Luxemburg protested against Joan's sale to the English" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly) "Required to give her up to the English, Luxembourg at first refused, restrained from this villainy, perhaps, by his aunt." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion, Jean de Luxembourg, scholarly)
- 060.8 Event: she leaps from the tower of Beaurevoir, against her voices, commending herself to God and Our Lady; she is found senseless and for two or three days cannot eat or drink. single (her answers at the trial, which Ramsay and Lowell follow, Ramsay citing Proces i. 150-152, 169; the Bourgeois has the leap among the charges read at Rouen, and says she was unhurt). No source read gives the day of the leap. "though her voices forbade her to jump from the tower, at last, from fear of the English, she leaped and commended herself to God and Our Lady, and in leaping was wounded." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the public examinations, 3 March, primary) "She was picked up in an insensible condition, and for two or three days could neither eat nor drink." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "she commended herself to God and our Lady and jumped." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly) "mais elle sailly d'une haulte tour en bas sans soy blecier aucunement" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois, 1431, item 576, primary)
- 060.9 Event: her reasons, in her own answers: she had learned the English were coming to take her, she had been sold to them and would rather die than be in their hands, and she feared for the people of Compiègne, who she was told would all be killed when the town fell. single (her answers at the trial; Lang and Lowell tell it from them). "She said that when she learned the English were to come and take her" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the public examinations, 3 March, primary) "The other reason was, that I was sold to the English; and I would rather die than be in the hands of my enemies of England." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "The city would soon be taken, so they told her, and then all the dwellers in it, from seven years old and upwards, would be put to the sword" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly)
- 060.10 Figure: the height of the tower of Beaurevoir. disputed: about sixty feet, Lang; at least seventy feet, France; neither gives a measurement it rests on. "The height of the tower of Beaurevoir must have been about sixty feet." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "The keep was at least seventy feet high" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 9, scholarly)
- 060.11 Event: on 14 July 1430, in the Duke of Burgundy's camp before Compiègne, Pierre Cauchon, Bishop of Beauvais, presents a summons to the Duke, John of Luxembourg and the Bastard of Wandomme, in the king's name and his own, to deliver her to him for trial. single (the instrument of the summons in the record; Ramsay follows it, and Lang has Cauchon at the camp "soon after July 14"). "In the year of our Lord 1430, the 14th day of July, indiction 8, the 13th year of the pontificate of our most Holy Father the Pope Martin V, in the castle of the very illustrious prince my lord Duke of Burgundy, in his camp before Compiègne" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, preliminary documents, the instrument of the summons, primary) "On the 14th July Pierre Cauchon, Bishop of Beauvais, a creature of the Anglo- Burgundian party" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "soon after July 14, Pierre Cauchon, Bishop of Beauvais, expelled from his see by the arms of France, presented himself at the camp of Jean de Luxembourg before Compiegne." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly)
- 060.12 Figure: the offer of the summons, up to 6,000 francs to her captors and a pension of 200 or 300 to the Bastard of Wandomme, or failing that 10,000 as the king could pay for a captured king or prince. single (the summons; Lowell gives the sums as pounds). "the King is bountifully pleased to grant them up to the sum of 6,000 francs" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, preliminary documents, the summons of the Bishop of Beauvais, primary) "the bishop was willing to give them proper security for the ten thousand pounds." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 17, scholarly)
- 060.13 Figure: her price, ten thousand. agreed for the sum; disputed for the unit: ten thousand livres tournois in the Norman levy that raised it and in the king's receipt (Quicherat 5), ten thousand pounds in Lowell and 10,000 livres in Champion; 10,000 francs in Ramsay, as in the summons of 14 July; Lang gives francs only for the summons' rule on a captured prince and livres for the levy, and France has both ("ten thousand golden livres", "King Henry's ten thousand golden francs"). "Her price was fixed at ten thousand pounds, the larger sum mentioned by Cauchon in his letter to Luxemburg" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly) "an authority from Henry VI to offer 10,000" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "Later on he yielded her on the demands of Pierre Cauchon, and sold her to the English for 10,000 livres" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion, Jean de Luxembourg, scholarly) "c'est assavoir dix mil livres tournois , au paiement de l'achapt de Jehanne la Pucelle" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, letters and acts, the levy of September 1430, primary)
- 060.14 Event: who sold her. disputed: John of Luxembourg, whose prisoner she was (Lowell, Lang, Champion, Thurston); Ramsay has the bargain made with the Duke of Burgundy, Philip the Good, who wanted the ransom for his followers; the record has the Duke and John of Luxembourg together surrender her to the king. "John of Luxemburg may have been willing to sell Joan for ten thousand pounds, but he wished to see the ready money." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 17, scholarly) "Joan was sold by John of Luxembourg to the English" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "Philip the Good did not intend to spare the Maid, but he wished to secure the benefit of her ransom for his followers." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "the most illustrious lord Duke of Burgundy and the lord Jean de Luxembourg graciously consenting to these demands" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the opening of the record, primary)
- 060.15 Event: the money is raised in Normandy: the Estates of Normandy, met at Rouen in August 1430, grant the king 120,000 livres tournois, of which 10,000 are for the purchase of Joan; the levy is assessed in September. single (the levy letter of Quicherat 5, which Ramsay, Lowell and Lang follow; France has an aid of eighty thousand livres tournois, ten thousand of them for her purchase). "en l'assemblée faicte à Rouen ou moys d'aoust derrenier passé; pour tourner et convertir, c'est assavoir dix mil livres tournois , au paiement de l'achapt de Jehanne la Pucelle" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, letters and acts, the levy of September 1430, primary) "The money was paid out of a subsidy of 120,000 francs obtained by Bedford from the Norman Estates, in August, 1430" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29 note 1, scholarly) "of which sum ten thousand pounds were especially appropriated" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly)
- 060.16 Event: she is handed over and paid for in November 1430: a receipt of 6 December 1430 speaks of the ten thousand livres tournois paid by the king to have her, a prisoner of war; Lowell places the handing over at Arras, Le Crotoy or between, Lang and France in November. agreed. "livres tournois paiées par ledit seigneur pour avoir Jehanne qui se dit la Pucelle, prisonnterre de guerre" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, letters and acts, receipt of Johan Bruyse, 6 December 1430, primary) "Either at Arras or Le Crotoy, or at some place between the two, the Eng- lish took their prisoner and paid their money." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly) "November, Jean de Luxembourg, resisting the prayers of the lady, his aunt, had sold the girl" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "At length, about the middle of November, the Sire de Luxembourg consented to the bargain; Jeanne was delivered up to the English." (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 10, scholarly)
- 060.17 Figure: Cauchon was paid 765 livres tournois for his journeys to Beaurevoir, Compiègne, Flanders and the Duke of Burgundy, to the last day of September 1430, Lang; Lowell has him at Beaurevoir once at least, from the same account. single. "He was paid 765 livres tournois for his exertions, which were continued to the last day of September 1430." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "Cauchon himself came to Beaurevoir" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly)
- 060.18 Event: from Beaurevoir she is taken by the Burgundians to Arras, in Burgundian land, where, as it seems, the Duke held his court (late October 1430, Lowell), then by the castle of Drugy to Le Crotoy, where she is delivered to the English. agreed for Arras and Le Crotoy; Drugy in Lang and France. "From Beaurevoir she was taken by the Burgundians as quietly as possible to Arras, and from thence to Crotoy. There she was delivered into the hands of the English" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "Joan was sent from Beaurevoir to Arras, where, as it seems, Philip of Burgundy then held his court." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly) "The result of his negotiations was the removal of the Maid to Arras, in Burgundian territory." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "From Arras she was taken to the Chateau of Drugy" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 10, scholarly)
- 060.19 Border: Le Crotoy, a fortress at the mouth of the Somme, English since it opened its gates on 3 March 1424 (Ramsay, in the campaign of early 1424); Lowell has it held by the English for about seven years. agreed. "Arras to Le Crotoy, at the mouth of the river Somme, a strong fortress which for about seven years had been held by the English." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 18, scholarly) "On the 3rd March Crotoy opened its gates" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 23, scholarly)
- 060.20 Event: at Le Crotoy she confesses to and hears the Masses of Nicolas de Queuville, chancellor of the church of Amiens, a prisoner there. single (Haimond de Macy's deposition, which Lowell and Lang follow). "kept her at Le Crotoy, where she was allowed to confess to the chancellor of Amiens, himself a prisoner of some distinction" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 19, scholarly) "Queuville, Chancellor of the Church of Amiens, a loyal Frenchman and a prisoner, who heard Jeanne in confession" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly)
- 060.21 Event: the University of Paris writes on 21 November 1430 to Cauchon and to Henry VI, reproaching the delay and asking that she be tried in Paris. single (the letters of the record, as Lowell and Lang give them). "on November 21 they wrote both to Cauchon and to Henry VI." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 19, scholarly) "The University of Paris, on November 21, accused Cauchon of want of zeal in the good cause" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly)
- 060.22 Border: Compiègne, which John of Luxembourg was besieging while she was his prisoner, holds out under Guillaume de Flavy and is relieved on 25-26 October 1430; the Burgundians retreat and leave their artillery. agreed. "enabled the city to hold out till, on October 25-26, they were rescued by a combined movement of" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "On October 26, Jean de Luxembourg had to follow the retreat of his troops, mortally wounded, and he left his artillery in de Flavy's hands." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion, Jean de Luxembourg, scholarly)
- 060.23 Event: when she came to Rouen. disputed: in November, Lang; probably in the first days of December, Lowell; before the close of 1430, Ramsay. "They brought her to Rouen in November, and incarcerated her in a tower of the ancient castle of Philip Augustus." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "After a short stay at Le Crotoy, Joan was therefore sent to Rouen, probably in the first days of December." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 19, scholarly) "Before the close of 1430 she was safely im-" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly)
- 060.24 Event: from Le Crotoy she is taken by boat across the mouth of the Somme to Saint-Valery, then by Eu and Dieppe to Rouen, in the account of Père Ignace de Jésus-Maria's history of the counts of Ponthieu (1657), which Quicherat prints with the warning that Ignace is the only author to trace this road and is to be distrusted until originals confirm him; France follows Ignace, with Dieppe "as is supposed". single (Ignace, by Quicherat 5 and France; Lowell and Ramsay give only Le Crotoy to Rouen). "descendit à Saint Valéry" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, letters and acts, Le Crotoy to Rouen, November 1430, from Père Ignace, primary) "à Dieppe, puis enfin à Rouen" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, letters and acts, Le Crotoy to Rouen, November 1430, from Père Ignace, primary) "Peut-être sera-t-il bon de se défier de son témoignage" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, letters and acts, Le Crotoy to Rouen, Quicherat's introduction, scholarly) "Jeanne was taken out of Crotoy at high tide and conveyed by boat to Saint-Valery, then to Dieppe, as is supposed, and certainly in the end to Rouen." (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 10, scholarly)
- 060.25 Event: her prison is a tower of the castle of Rouen, the castle of Philip Augustus on the Bouvreuil hill: near the postern gate, a great tower about a hundred feet high in Lowell, a tower looking on the open country in France. agreed. "Joan was imprisoned near the postern gate in one of these towers, a great mass of masonry one hundred feet high" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 19, scholarly) "She was a prisoner in the Castle, in a great tower." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions at Rouen 1455-6, Maugier Leparmentier, primary) "She was conducted to the old castle, built in the time of Philippe-Auguste on the slope of the Bouvreuil hill." (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 10, scholarly)
- 060.26 Event: the iron cage. disputed: Thurston has her kept at first in an iron cage, chained by neck, hands and feet; Lowell says it is not certain the cage was used; France and Lang that no witness saw her in it; Massieu that at night she was chained by the legs to a beam. "where she was at first kept in an iron cage, chained by the neck, hands, and feet" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "It is not certain that this fearful instrmnent of torture was put into use." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 19, scholarly) "In her cell was a heavy iron cage ; one witness saw her in the den, ironed, but not in the cage or huche." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly) "But no one ever found Jeanne in it." (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 10, scholarly) "at night she slept chained by the legs with two pairs of iron chains" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Jean Massieu, primary)
- 060.27 Event: though tried by a church court, she is kept in the castle, a secular prison, guarded by English soldiers in her cell, and not in a church prison. agreed. "commanded Jeanne to be kept in the secular prison and in the hands of her mortal enemies" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Martin Ladvenu, primary) "she was throughout illegally confined in the Castle of Rouen, a secular prison, where she was guarded by dissolute English soldiers." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "half a dozen common soldiers, who had both the leisure and the disposition to mock her" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 19, scholarly)
- 060.28 Person: Richard Beauchamp, Earl of Warwick, captain of the castle of Rouen. agreed. "Warwick was captain" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 19 note 3, scholarly) "Earl of Warwick, who was governor of the castle." (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 10, scholarly)
- 060.29 Person: Pierre Cauchon, Bishop of Beauvais from 1420 by the Burgundian party, councillor of Henry VI, expelled from Beauvais in August 1429 when it went over to Charles VII, and since then living at Rouen. agreed. "raised by them to the see of Beauvais, in 1420, he had been expelled by the people in August, 1429." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29 note 2, scholarly) "Expelled from Beauvais with the English (August, 1429), Cauchon fled to Rouen" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion, Pierre Cauchon, scholarly)
- 060.30 Border: in 1430-1431 Beauvais is held for Charles VII, so the trial of its bishop sits at Rouen, in English Normandy. agreed. "Still, as Beauvais was in the hands of the French, the trial took place at Rouen" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "soon after July 14, Pierre Cauchon, Bishop of Beauvais, expelled from his see by the arms of France" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly)
- 060.31 Event: Cauchon's title to judge her: she was taken at Compiègne, in his diocese. disputed: the record, Henry VI's letter and Thurston have Compiègne in the diocese of Beauvais; Lang gives Canon Dunand's view that it lay in the see of Soissons. "she was captured, taken and arrested in his diocese and within the limits of his spiritual jurisdiction" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, preliminary documents, the letters of the chapter of Rouen, primary) "she was claimed by the bishop in whose diocese she had been taken" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet A book 2 ch. 105, Henry VI's letter to the Duke of Burgundy, primary) "lay in the Diocese of Beauvais" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "the Chanoine Dunand avers that Compiegne was not in the see of Beauvais but of Soissons." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 21, scholarly)
- 060.32 Event: by letters given at Rouen on 3 January 1431 Henry VI orders her delivered to Cauchon for trial, keeping the right to take her back if she is not convicted; Cauchon opens the trial six days later. agreed for the delivery (the king's letters in the record; his letter to the Duke of Burgundy); single for the date (the record, which Lowell follows). "at Rouen, January 3rd, in the year of grace 1431, and the ninth of our reign" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, preliminary documents, the letters of Henry VI, primary) "we delivered her up to be tried and punished by the usual ecclesiastical judges" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet A book 2 ch. 105, Henry VI's letter to the Duke of Burgundy, primary) "English had formally delivered to him their" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 20, scholarly)
- 060.33 Event: the chapter of Rouen, which holds the spiritual jurisdiction while the archbishopric is vacant, grants Cauchon territory in Rouen and its diocese for the trial (28 December 1430, France). single (the record; Ramsay and France follow it). "Follows the tenor of the letters from the venerable chapter of the Cathedral of Rouen granting us the said bishop territory during the vacancy of the archiepiscopal see" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, preliminary documents, primary) "The Rouen Chapter having granted a territorial faculty" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "Footnote 2154: 28 December, 1430." (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France vol. 2 ch. 10, scholarly)
- 060.34 Event: the trial opens on Tuesday 9 January 1431 at Rouen, in the house of the King's Counsel, with abbots, canons and doctors summoned. single (the record; Lowell and Lang follow it). "And on Tuesday the ninth day of January in the year of our Lord fourteen hundred and thirty-one" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, January 9th 1431, the first day of the proceedings, primary) "Cauchon opened his court in the royal council chamber at Rouen for the trial of Joan of Arc." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 20, scholarly) "On January 9 a solemn deliberation on her case has been held by Doctors in canon law and in theology" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 22, scholarly)
- 060.35 Person: Jean Le Maistre, Dominican, vicar for the diocese of Rouen of the Inquisitor of France Jean Graverent, the second judge: he first declines for want of jurisdiction (20 February 1431); Graverent's commission, dated at Coutances 4 March, received 12 March, has him sit from 13 March. single (the record; Ramsay and Lowell follow it). "Tuesday, February 20th 1431. The Vicar of the lord Inquisitor refuses to act" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, February 20th 1431, primary) "The Commission from the Inquisitor-General was dated Coutances, 4th March" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29 note 1, scholarly) "on March 12 Graverent sent a commission which removed completely the legal scruples of his vicar Le- maitre" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 22, scholarly)
- 060.36 Person: the assessors, almost all French, most of them theologians and doctors of the University of Paris; forty-three sat at the first public session (Lowell). single: Thurston for the University, Lowell for the count. "Throughout the trial Cauchon's assessors consisted almost entirely of Frenchmen, for the most part theologians and doctors of the University of Paris." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "on Wednesday, February 21, 1431, he held the first public session of the court. Forty-three assessors attended." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 20, scholarly)
- 060.37 Event: the English crown pays the court: Henry VI taxes twenty sous tournois a day to each of the Paris doctors sitting at Rouen, paid by the receiver-general of Normandy (receipts of March and 9 April 1431, the king's order of 2 April 1431), and the English government gives Jean Le Maistre twenty saluts d'or on 24 April 1431 for his attendance. agreed. "à chascun de eulx vint sols tournois par jour pour aider à supporter les fraiz et despens" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, letters and acts, the king's order for Jean Beaupère, 2 April 1431, primary) "On April 24, 1431, Jean Le Maistre received from the English government a gratuity Of 20 salus d'or" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Champion, Jean Le Maistre, scholarly)
- 060.38 Event: what the court had to prove: she is summoned as "vehemently suspected of heresy"; Henry VI's letter reports the judgment of the University that she was superstitious, a sorceress, a blasphemer and a schismatic; Thurston: the English could not put her to death for beating them, but could have her sentenced as a witch and a heretic. agreed. "summon the said Jeanne so vehemently suspected of heresy to appear before us in the chapel royal of the castle of Rouen" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the citation of 20 February, primary) "this Joan was superstitious, a sorceress of the devil, a blasphemer of God and of his saints, a schismatic" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet A book 2 ch. 105, Henry VI's letter to the Duke of Burgundy, primary) "They could not put her to death for having beaten them, but they could get her sentenced as a witch and a heretic." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)
- 060.39 Event: the first public session, Wednesday 21 February 1431, at eight in the morning, in the chapel royal of the castle of Rouen. single (the record; Ramsay and Thurston follow it). "On Wednesday, February 21st, at eight o'clock in the morning we the said bishop repaired to the chapel royal of the castle of Rouen" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, February 21st, the first public session, primary) "produced for examination on the aist February." (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "it was only on 21 February, 1431, that Joan appeared for the first time before her judges." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)
- 060.40 Event: from 22 February the public sessions sit in the Robing Room at the end of the great hall of the castle. single (the record). "On Thursday, February 22nd, we entered the Robing Room at the end of the great hall of the castle of Rouen" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, February 22nd, second session, primary)
- 060.41 Event: from Saturday 10 March she is questioned in her prison chamber before a few judges; the examinations end on 17 March, twelve days of questioning from 21 February (Ramsay). single (the record; Thurston and Ramsay follow it). "Saturday, March 10th First session in prison" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, March 10th, primary) "Cauchon decided to conduct the rest of the inquiry before a small committee of judges in the prison itself." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "that day and the 17th March she was interrogated on twelve different days" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly)
- 060.42 Event: questioned on the voices, she says the first came when she was about thirteen, at noon in summer in her father's garden; that she saw St Michael with angels, and St Catherine and St Margaret. single (her answers in the record, which Ramsay follows; the Bourgeois has the same saints from the charges read at Rouen). "at the age of thirteen she had a voice from God to help her and guide her. And the first time she was much afraid. And this voice came towards noon, in summer, in her father's garden" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, February 22nd, primary) "She had seen St. Michael and his angels as clearly as she saw her judges there" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "elle est toute certaine que ce est sainct Michel et saincte Katherine et saincte Marguerite qui à lui parlent souvent" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois, 1431, item 575, primary)
- 060.43 Event: the sign given to the king. disputed: in the record of 13 March she says an angel brought a crown, given to the Archbishop of Reims in the king's presence; Ramsay says she kept silent on the sign; Thurston says most authorities read the angel and crown as an allegory to guard the king's secret, she herself being the angel, while others doubt the record. "Asked how the angel brought the crown, and whether he placed it on the king's head, she answered that it was given to an archbishop, namely the archbishop of Reims" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Tuesday, March 13th, in prison, primary) "On the subject of the sign given by her to Charles VII she was resolutely silent" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "Most authorities (like, e.g., M. Petit de Julleville and Mr. Andrew Lang) are agreed that she was trying to guard the king's secret behind an allegory, she herself being the angel" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)
- 060.44 Event: the man's dress: she will not put it off without God's leave; she took it by God's command; Thurston holds she kept it to protect her modesty among her guards. single for her answer (the record, which Ramsay follows); the reason is Thurston's own. "And I answered I would not put it off without God's leave." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the public examinations, 3 March, primary) "but she had taken it by God's command, and could not leave it off" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly) "It was undoubtedly for the better protection of her modesty under such conditions that she persisted in retaining her male attire." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)
- 060.45 Figure: the promoter Jean d'Estivet's articles of accusation, read to her by Thomas de Courcelles on 27 and 28 March 1431 in the room near the great hall. single for the dates (the record for 27 March; Lang has Courcelles read the articles on 28 March); disputed for the count: seventy articles (Murray, Lang, France; Lowell counts seventy with the introduction and conclusion), sixty-nine (Ramsay). "The following Tuesday after Palm Sunday, the 27th day of March, in the room near" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Tuesday, March 27th, the ordinary trial, primary) "The Seventy Articles prepared by the Promoter, which form the Act of Accusation for the Trial in Ordi- nary, were read to Jeanne by Thomas de Courcelles, on Tuesday, March 27th." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Part I, the trial in ordinary, primary) "Seventy, including the introduction and conclusion." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 23 note 2, scholarly) "the seventy Articles made by Estivet were read to the prisoner" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 23, scholarly) "njne formaj Articles were exhibited against Jeanne" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1 ch. 29, scholarly)
- 060.46 Event: the charges are cut down to twelve articles (by Nicolas Midi, Murray's note and Champion), sent to the doctors on 5 April 1431 and then to the University of Paris, which condemns her. agreed for the twelve articles and the University; single for the date (the record). "Thursday, April 5th.
