The digest is submitted to the assessors" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Thursday, April 5th, primary) "Seventy Articles were afterwards reduced to Twelve by Mattre Nicolas Midi" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Part I, the trial in ordinary, editor's note, primary) "Meanwhile, the twelve propositions were submitted to the University of Paris, which, being extravagantly English in sympathy, denounced the Maid in violent terms." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "they sent them for the opinion of our beloved daughter the university of Paris" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet A book 2 ch. 105, Henry VI's letter, primary)
- 060.47 Event: she is admonished in private on 18 April, while sick, and in public on 2 May in the room near the great hall of the castle, and does not submit. single (the record; Thurston follows it). "On Wednesday, May 2nd, in the year of Our Lord 1431, we the said judges held session in the room of the castle of Rouen near the great hall of the castle" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Wednesday, May 2nd, public admonition, primary) "Certain formal admonitions, at first private, and then public, were administered to the poor victim (18 April and 2 May)" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)
- 060.48 Event: on 9 May 1431 she is brought into the great tower of the castle and shown the instruments of torture, with the torturers present; the judges put off the torture, and on 12 May decide against it. agreed for the threat of torture in the castle (the record, Leparmentier); single for the dates (the record, which Thurston follows). "Jeanne was brought into the great tower of the castle of Rouen before us the said judges" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Wednesday, May 9th, she is threatened with torture, primary) "I was summoned to the Castle of Rouen, with my assistants, to submit Jeanne to torture." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions at Rouen 1455-6, Maugier Leparmentier, primary) "On 9 May she was threatened with torture, but she still held firm." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)
- 060.49 Event: Thursday 24 May 1431, in the cemetery of the abbey of Saint-Ouen, south of the abbey church, she is set on a scaffold before the judges and a great crowd, and Guillaume Erard preaches. agreed for the place (the record, Leparmentier); single for the date (the record, which Lowell follows) and for the words south of the abbey church (Lowell). "we the said judges repaired in the morning to a public place, in the cemetery of the abbey of Saint-Ouen at Rouen, where the said Jeanne was present before us on a scaffold or platform. First we had a solemn sermon pronounced by master Guillaume Erart" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Thursday, May 24th, primary) "The cemetery of St. Ouen, just south of the magnifi- cent abbey church, was the place chosen for the ceremony of Joan's sentence." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 24, scholarly) "I was present at the first preaching at , St. Ouen" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions at Rouen 1455-6, Maugier Leparmentier, primary)
- 060.50 Person: Henry Beaufort, cardinal of England, great-uncle of Henry VI, sits with the judges at Saint-Ouen, with the bishops of Thérouanne, Noyon and Norwich. single (the record; Lowell follows it). "the very reverend father in Christ Henry by divine permission priest of St. Eusebius and cardinal of the Holy Roman Church, commonly called cardinal of England" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Thursday, May 24th, primary) "Cardinal Beaufort, the great-uncle" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 24, scholarly)
- 060.51 Event: she abjures before the sentence is read to its end, and makes a cross with a pen Massieu hands her. agreed for the abjuration; single (Massieu) for the pen. "The sentence of which the greater part was read before Jeanne's abjuration" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, May 30th, the sentences, primary) "but before it was read her courage seemed to fail her, and she said she was willing to return to the church" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet A book 2 ch. 105, Henry VI's letter, primary) "before she left the Square, she abjured, and made a cross with a pen which I handed to her." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Jean Massieu, primary) "Quant elle vit que c'estoit à certes, elle cria mercy et soy revocqua de bouche" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois, 1431, item 578, primary)
- 060.52 Event: the words she abjured. disputed: the record inserts a long formula of abjuration; witnesses of 1450-1456 say the schedule read to her was short: about six lines, Taquel; a few lines, five witnesses with Massieu, in Thurston; Thurston holds the terms will never be known. "It was about six lines of large writing ; and Jeanne repeated it after Massieu" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry, Nicolas Taquel, primary) "In the official record of the process a form of retraction is in inserted which is most humiliating in every particular." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)
- 060.53 Event: she is sentenced to perpetual imprisonment, "with the bread of sorrow and water of affliction", taken back to the castle and not to a church prison, and that afternoon puts on a woman's dress. agreed. "condemn you for salutary penance to perpetual imprisonment, with the bread of sorrow and water of affliction" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Thursday, May 24th, the mitigated sentence, primary) "Take her to the Castle whence she came" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Jean Massieu, primary) "fut sa robe ostée et vestue en habit de femme" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois, 1431, item 578, primary)
- 060.54 Event: on Monday 28 May, the day after Trinity Sunday, the judges find her in man's dress in her cell; she says her voices told her she had damned herself to save her life, and that she had recanted only for fear of the fire. agreed for the man's dress; single for her words (the record). "On Monday following, the day after Holy Trinity Sunday, we the said judges repaired to Jeanne's prison to observe her state and disposition." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the trial for relapse, Monday, May 28th, primary) "because, in prison, she had resumed a man's dress" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Martin Ladvenu, primary) "mais aussitost qu'elle se vit en tel estat, elle recommença son erreur comme devant, demandant son habit de homme" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois, 1431, item 578, primary)
- 060.55 Event: why she put the man's dress on again. disputed: the record has her say she took it of her own will, as more fitting among men, because the promises made to her (Mass, her chains taken off) were not kept; Massieu says that on Trinity Sunday the English guards took her woman's dress and left her only the man's; Thurston reports the later charge of a trap laid with Cauchon's connivance. "she answered that she had taken it of her own will, under no compulsion, as she preferred man's to woman's dress." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, the trial for relapse, Monday, May 28th, primary) "Then one of the English took away from her the woman's garments which she had on her, and they emptied the bag in which was her man's dress" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Jean Massieu, primary) "owing, it was afterwards alleged, to a trap deliberately laid by her jailers with the connivance of Cauchon" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)
- 060.56 Event: Ladvenu and Isambart de La Pierre testified in 1450 that after the abjuration she said she had been ill-treated and molested in prison, an English lord among them, and had taken the man's dress again for that. single (the witnesses of 1450, the same enquiry). "she was violently treated in the prison, molested, beaten, and illused ; and that an English lord had insulted her." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Martin Ladvenu, primary)
- 060.57 Event: on Tuesday 29 May the judges and assessors, met in the archiepiscopal chapel of Rouen, find her relapsed and cite her to the Old Market for eight the next morning; Thurston counts thirty-seven judges. single (the record; Thurston and Lang follow it). "The next day, the Tuesday after Holy Trinity, May 29th, we the said judges assembled in the archiepiscopal chapel of Rouen" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, Tuesday, May 29th, primary) "On 29 May a court of thirty-seven judges decided unanimously that the Maid must be treated as a relapsed heretic" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "The Maid was cited to appear at the Old Market, on May 30." (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 25, scholarly)
- 060.58 Event: on the morning of 30 May Ladvenu, sent by Cauchon, tells her she is to die and hears her confession; she receives communion; to Cauchon she says "Bishop, I die through you." agreed. "I was in the prison during the morning with Brother Martin Ladvenu, whom the Bishop of Beauvais had sent to her to announce her approaching death" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Jean Toutmouille, primary) "She was, however, allowed to make her confession and to receive Communion." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "Bishop, I die through you." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Jean Toutmouille, primary) "Alas! I die through you, for had you given me over" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Martin Ladvenu, primary)
- 060.59 Figure: the escort from the castle to the Old Market. disputed: more than 800 soldiers with axes and swords, Massieu, who walked beside her; several score English soldiers, Lowell. "accompanied by more than 800 soldiers, with axes and swords" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Jean Massieu, primary) "Guarded by several score of English soldiers, accompanied by the sergeant and by Ladvenu, she was led from the castle to the Old Market" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 24, scholarly)
- 060.60 Event: Wednesday 30 May 1431, about nine in the morning, at the Old Market of Rouen near the church of Saint-Sauveur, she is set on a scaffold, one of three, and Nicolas Midi preaches. agreed. "towards nine o'clock in the morning of the same day we the said judges repaired to the Old Market of" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, May 30th, final sentence pronounced before the people, primary) "About nine o'clock Joan reached the Old Market, and was brought upon the platform near the Church of St. Saviour." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 24, scholarly) "There were three scaffolds ; on one the Maid was exhibited, and preached at" (archive.org-download-maidoffrancebein00languoft-maidoffrancebein00languoft-djvu.txt.txt, Lang ch. 26, scholarly) "qui fut le XXXe jour de may oudit an XXXI" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois, 1431, item 572, primary)
- 060.61 Event: the final sentence declares her a relapsed heretic, cut off from the Church and given over to the secular power. agreed. "must be cast out of the unity of the Church, cut off from her body, and given over to the secular power" (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, May 30th, the final sentence, primary) "they delivered her over to the power of the state." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 24, scholarly)
- 060.62 Event: the bailli of Rouen gives no sentence: as soon as the church's sentence is read she is taken down and handed to the executioner, which the witnesses of the retrial held irregular. agreed (Guesdon, acting for the bailli, Ladvenu and Leparmentier; Lowell from them). "without any interval of handing her over to the Bailly, without more ado, and before either the Bailly or myself, whose office it was, had given sentence, the executioner seized her" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions at Rouen 1455-6, Laurence Guesdon, primary) "should have passed sentence of death, but in the confusion this formality was omitted" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 24, scholarly)
- 060.63 Event: she asks for a cross; an Englishman makes her a small one of wood, and Massieu has the cross of Saint-Sauveur brought; she calls on Jesus in the fire to the last. agreed. "with great devotion she asked to have a Cross : and, hearing this, an Englishman, who was there present, made a little cross of wood" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, the first enquiry 1449, Jean Massieu, primary) "She asked for a cross, which, after she had embraced it, was held up before her while she called continuously upon the name of Jesus." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)
- 060.64 Event: the Bourgeois's report from Paris: when her dress had burned the fire was drawn back so that the crowd could see her body, and then the executioner burned it to ashes. single (the Bourgeois). "puis fut le feu tiré ariere, et fut veue de tout le peuple toute nue" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Bourgeois, 1431, item 578, primary)
- 060.65 Event: her ashes are thrown into the Seine. agreed (Guesdon says the executioner did it, Leparmentier had heard it said; Lowell and Thurston report it). "After she was dead, the ashes that remained were col- lected by the executioner and thrown into the Seine." (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, depositions at Rouen 1455-6, Laurence Guesdon, primary) "After death her ashes were thrown into the Seine." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly) "It is said that Joan's ashes were thrown into the Seine." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 24 note, scholarly)
- 060.66 Event: whether she said on her last morning that her voices had deceived her. disputed: the judges' information of 7 June 1431, added to the record, has her say so; Lowell sees no reason to think that testimony altered in the recording; Thurston says a doubt must always be felt, and gives Manchon's word that to the last she said her voices came from God. "Truly I see they have indeed deceived me." (sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu-basis-joanofarc-trial.asp.txt, subsequent documents, Thursday, June 7th, primary) "reason to suppose that the testimony taken June 7 was altered in the recording." (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell ch. 24 note, scholarly) "About this last speech a doubt must always be felt." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, scholarly)

# Research for Joan of Arc

## 070 after-her

Coverage: primary (the Journal d'un bourgeois de Paris for 1431 and 1436; Monstrelet's Chronicles in Johnes's translation, with the continuation of book 3 for Formigny and Castillon; Murray's translation of the rehabilitation with his introductory note; Quicherat 5 for the Orléans chronicle of the feast and the city accounts; the bull Divina disponente and the homily of 16 May 1920 in AAS 12) and scholarly (Ramsay's Lancaster and York, vols. 1-2; Oman's Art of War, vol. 2; Beaucourt's Histoire de Charles VII, vols. 2 and 5; Lowell; Thurston; Renoux; France's Life, read only for where to look), with Wikipedia for the order of events. Ramsay 1 and 2 are one work, Beaucourt 2 and 5 one work, and Monstrelet A and B one chronicle whose book 3 is its continuation: no author is used with two distinct works, so no later work withdraws an earlier one. Unread: the two modern studies of the Orléans feast and W. P. Müller on the rehabilitation (bot walls, see sources.md "Unread"), and Pernoud's Retrial (lent only). The Truce of Tours is read in Ramsay 2, in Monstrelet B (book 2, ch. 275, the text of the truce) and in Wikipedia, and the 1793 suspension and 1803 renewal of the Orléans feast only in France's Life and Wikipedia. The English holds of 1430 to 1436 round Paris and on the Bresle (070.55-070.66) are read in Ramsay 1 and 2, Monstrelet A and B, the Bourgeois with Tuetey's notes and Beaucourt 2; a search of these for Étampes finds only Salisbury's taking of it in June 1425 (Beaucourt 2), so no source read gives who held Étampes from 1430 to 1436.

- 070.1 Event: Henry VI is crowned King of France in Notre-Dame de Paris by Cardinal Beaufort, the Cardinal of Winchester, in December 1431. disputed for the day: Sunday 16 December in the Bourgeois and Ramsay; the 17th in Monstrelet. "le XVIe jour de decembre … vint ledit roy Henry du pallays royal à Nostre-Dame de Paris … sacré de la main du cardinal de Vincestre" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, §592, 1431, primary) "On Sunday, the 16th December, he was crowned King" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 29, scholarly) "On the 17th of that month he went from the Palace in great pomp, and attended by a numerous body of nobles and ecclesiastics, to the church of Notre Dame, for his coronation" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, Henry VI to Paris, primary)
- 070.2 Event: John, Duke of Bedford, regent of France for Henry VI, dies at Rouen in the night of 14-15 September 1435, while the congress sits at Arras; the news reaches Arras on the 16th. agreed. "died at Rouen on the night of the I4th-I5th September" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 32, scholarly) "On apprit le 16 septembre que le régent Bedford avail cessé de vivre" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, ch. 12, Le congrès d'Arras, scholarly)
- 070.3 Event: the Congress of Arras: the English envoys leave Arras on 6 September 1435, and Charles VII's envoys and Philip the Good, Duke of Burgundy, conclude a separate peace, sealed in the duke's letters of 21 September 1435 in the abbey of Saint-Vaast. agreed. "Le même jour, 21 septembre, le duc de Bourgogne donna ses lettres patentes" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, ch. 12, Le congrès d'Arras, scholarly) "A PEACE IS CONCLUDED BETWEEN CHARLES, KING OF FRANCE," (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 187, the treaty given at Arras on the 21st of September, misprinted 1436 in this edition, primary)
- 070.4 Event: at Arras Philip the Good swears peace with Charles VII and the cardinals absolve him of his oaths to the English: the Anglo-Burgundian alliance ends. agreed. "the Duke swore 'good peace' with King Charles; and was absolved by the Cardinals from his oaths to the English" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 32, scholarly) "déclarant nuls et non valables les serments faits par" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, ch. 12, the cardinals' summons of 20 September, scholarly)
- 070.5 Border: with Arras the English stand alone against France and Burgundy; Ramsay dates the war's last stage from the treaty. agreed. "With the Treaty of Arras the Hundred Years' War … enters on its last stage" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 32, scholarly) "France already had [[Kingdom of Scotland|Scotland]] as an ally and England was left isolated" (Congress_of_Arras.wiki, Treaty of Arras, wikipedia)
- 070.6 Event: Saint-Denis, taken by the French on 1 June 1435, is besieged by the English and Burgundians and its garrison marches out on 4 October 1435. single: Ramsay gives both dates, the capture of 1 June in a note; Monstrelet tells the French storm of the town without a date. "The garrison signed articles, and on the 4th October marched out with all the honours of war" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 32, scholarly) "the French won the town of St. Denis" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 174, primary)
- 070.7 Border: after Arras the English lose Dieppe (29 October 1435) and the Pays de Caux but Caudebec, and early in 1436 Pontoise, Charenton, Brie-Comte-Robert, Saint-Germain-en-Laye, Corbeil and Vincennes round Paris. single (Ramsay). "the whole of Caux was speedily wrested from the English, only Caudebec … early in 1436 Pontoise, Charenton," (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 32, scholarly)
- 070.8 Event: in April 1436 the French and Burgundian force under Richemont storms Saint-Denis and routs Sir Thomas Beaumont's sortie from Paris near Épinay; English survivors in the Tour du Velin at Saint-Denis are taken and killed. disputed for the day of Beaumont's defeat: 10 April in Ramsay; the Thursday before the Friday entry into Paris in Monstrelet; the Bourgeois has the tower taken on the Friday after Easter. "On the 10th April Sir Thomas Beaumont was sent out towards Pontoise" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 32, scholarly) "they attacked the town of St. Denis" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 198, 1436, primary) "ilz se mirent dedens Sainct-Denis en une tour c'on nomme la tour du Velin" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, §691, 1436, primary)
- 070.9 Person: Arthur de Richemont, Constable of France for Charles VII, leads the force that recovers Paris, with the Bastard of Orléans (Dunois) and the lord of L'Isle-Adam. agreed. "le conte de Richemont qui estoit connestable de France de par le roy Charles, le bastart d'Orleans, le signeur de l'Isle-Adam" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, §692, 1436, primary) "the count de Richemont, constable of France, the bastaid of Orleans" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 198, 1436, primary)
- 070.10 Event: on Friday 13 April 1436 Richemont's force comes to the Porte Saint-Jacques; the citizens let L'Isle-Adam in by ladders and open the gate, and the constable enters Paris. agreed. "On Friday morning, 13th … April, the Constable de Richemont, the Bastard of Orleans, … Porte Saint-Jacques. The gates being locked, friendly hands let down ladders from the walls" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 32, Loss of Paris, scholarly) "en cellui vendredy d'après Pasques, vindrent devant Paris … à la porte Sainct-Jaque" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, §692, 1436, Easter being 8 April, primary) "When the French and Burgundians were come before Paris, to the gate of St. James" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 198, primary)
- 070.11 Event: the English garrison under Lord Willoughby and the Bishop of Thérouanne shuts itself in the Bastille Saint-Antoine, treats with the constable and marches out under safe-conduct on 17 April 1436, hooted by the Parisians. agreed for the Bastille and the 17th; single (Monstrelet) for their going by land and water to Rouen. "s'en fouirent tous vers la porte Sainct-Anthoine et se bouterent tous dedens la forteresse … le mardy XVIIe jour d'avril l'an mil IIIIc XXXVI" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, §693-695, 1436, primary) "seeing that all was lost they retired to the … On the 17th April they were allowed to march" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 32, scholarly) "They had a passport from the constable, under which they went by land and water to Rouen." (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 198, primary)
- 070.12 Event: the Truce of Tours, a general truce concluded at Tours in May 1444, at the time of Margaret of Anjou's betrothal to Henry VI by proxy there on 24 May. agreed for a general truce made at Tours in May 1444; disputed for the day: four days after the betrothal, that is 28 May, in Ramsay, and 28 May in Wikipedia; the text of the truce in Monstrelet (Johnes) is given at Tours on the 20th of May, which may be a misprint. "days after the betrothal a general truce was signed" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 5, scholarly) "concluded by their envoys on 28 May 1444" (Truce_of_Tours.wiki, lead, wikipedia) "at Tours, the 20th day of May, in the year 1444" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 275, the text of the truce, primary)
- 070.13 Border: by 1444 English rule in France is limited to Normandy and a strip of Gascony. single (wikipedia, citing Wagner). "English rule in France was limited to Normandy in the north and a strip of land in Gascony in the southwest" (Truce_of_Tours.wiki, Context, wikipedia)
- 070.14 Event: the truce breaks: on 24 March 1449 an English force from the Norman garrisons under François de Surienne seizes and sacks Fougères in Brittany, and French captains take Pont-de-l'Arche, Conches and Gerberoy in reprisal. agreed. "the rich town of Foug^res in Brittany, the seat of a considerable woollen manufacture, was seized and sacked" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 7, scholarly) "en représailles de la surprise de Fougères, certains capitaines de l'armée royale" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 1, scholarly)
- 070.15 Event: Rouen rises on 19 October 1449; Somerset signs a capitulation on 29 October, marches out on 4 November, and Charles VII makes his entry on Monday 10 November 1449. agreed (Ramsay, Beaucourt); Lowell, who sets the entry in October 1449, dates it by the capitulation. "On the 4th November Somerset marched out of Rouen" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 7, Fall of Rouen, scholarly) "Le lundi 10 novembre, Charles VIT faisait son entrée" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 1, scholarly) "In October, 1449, Charles entered Rouen" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly)
- 070.16 Border: at the close of 1449 English Normandy is cut down to Fresnay, Domfront, Vire, Falaise, Honfleur, Caen, Bayeux, Saint-Sauveur and Cherbourg. single (Ramsay). "Thus at the close of 1449 the English dominion in Normandy had been cut down to Fresnay," (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 7, scholarly)
- 070.17 Person: Sir Thomas Kyriell leads the last English relief army, landed at Cherbourg about 15 March 1450, joined by Sir Matthew Gough's men; John, Count of Clermont, son of the Duke of Bourbon, commands in Lower Normandy for Charles VII. agreed. "The Count of Clermont, eldest son of the Duke of Bourbon, who had the chief command in Lower Normandy" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 8, scholarly) "armée anglaise, commandée par Thomas Kyriel" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 1, scholarly)
- 070.18 Battle: Formigny, 15 April 1450: Clermont, then Richemont coming from Saint-Lô, defeat Kyriell's army between Carentan and Bayeux; Kyriell is taken and Gough escapes to Bayeux. Oman, Ramsay 2 and Beaucourt 5 give the positions and movements of both sides, and Monstrelet's continuator the heralds' account. agreed for 15 April (Oman, Ramsay, Beaucourt); Monstrelet's continuator dates the battle the 18th. "the disastrous field of Formigny (April 15, 1450)" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, Battle of Formigny, scholarly) "up a defensive but rather cramped position in front of Formigny, not across the road from Isigny" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 8, Battle of Formigny, scholarly) "Le 15 avril, à la première heure, le comte de Clermont" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 1, scholarly) "near a village called Fourmigny, between Carentan and Bayeux, on the 18th of the same month" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), ch. 29, primary)
- 070.19 Battle: the course of Formigny: the English stand with a stream and orchards behind them, archers in front behind stakes, a wing-guard under Gough thrown out beyond the left flank (Oman; Ramsay rests the English right on a bridge, and Monstrelet's continuator has the constable's archers attack their left wing at a bridge); after three hours of skirmishing the French bring up two culverins, which English archers rush out and take; Richemont's arrival decides the day. disputed on Richemont's attack: Oman brings him on the English flank; Ramsay has him send a detachment to his left to join Clermont and advance himself against the English centre; Monstrelet's continuator has his archers attack the English left wing at a bridge and Kyriell fall back to the stream and the village. "appeared on the flank of the English position with three hundred ances garnies" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly) "while he himself advanced against the English centre" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 8, scholarly) "Kiriel and the remainder retreated toward the rivulet" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), ch. 29, primary)
- 070.20 Figure: strengths at Formigny. disputed: English some 4,500 (Kyriell's 2,500 at most, the rest from the garrisons) and Clermont about 3,000 with two guns, Richemont 1,200 more, Oman; Kyriell 2,500 and the garrison detachments perhaps 1,000, Ramsay, who halves the French writers' figures; Kyriell four to five thousand, Beaucourt; the French not more than 3,000 and the English six to seven thousand by the heralds' report, Monstrelet's continuator. "Some four thousand five hundred English had been collected" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly) "with his 2500 men, a substantial army as things went … which may have made up 1000 men" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 8, scholarly) "Kyriel, à la tête de quatre à cinq mille hommes" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 1, scholarly) "than three thousand combatants, and the English fit)m six to seven thousand" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), ch. 29, primary)
- 070.21 Figure: English dead at Formigny. disputed: 3,774 counted by the French heralds the next day, Oman and Beaucourt (3,773 in Monstrelet's continuator, buried in fourteen pits), with 1,200 to 1,400 prisoners, Beaucourt; Ramsay calls the English reckoning of 3,000 an exaggeration. "their heralds counted next day three thousand seven hundred and seventy-four English corpses" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly) "Le relevé des morts a été fait : on en compte trois mille sept cent soixante-quatorze" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 1, scholarly) "three thousand seven hundred and seventy-three were slain" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), ch. 29, primary) "Even in England the loss was reckoned at 3000 men, but that must have been an exaggeration" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 8, note, scholarly)
- 070.22 Figure: French losses at Formigny. open: Oman says they cannot be ascertained, the French annalists naming twelve men-at-arms; Beaucourt gives ten or twelve men. "What number of the French fell we are unable to ascertain" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly) "les pertes des Français ne sont évaluées qu'à dix ou douze hommes" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 1, scholarly)
- 070.23 Event: Caen, besieged from early June 1450, capitulates by articles of 24 June; on 1 July Richemont escorts Somerset out and Dunois takes possession, and Charles VII enters on 6 July; Somerset sails for Calais. agreed. "On the ist July the Constable de Richemont escorted Somerset" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 8, Fall of Caen, scholarly) "Par un traité en date du 24 juin, le gouverneur anglais s'engagea à livrer Caen" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 1, scholarly)
- 070.24 Event: Cherbourg, the last English hold in Normandy, surrenders after a month's siege on 12 August 1450, and English Normandy is at an end. agreed. "the French entered Cherbourg … we have not now a foote of londe in Normandie" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 8, The last of Normandy, scholarly) "Cherbourg fut évacué le 12 août" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 1, scholarly)
- 070.25 Event: the French conquest of Gascony in 1451: Bordeaux treats on 12 June and is delivered on 30 June to Dunois; Bayonne, besieged from 6 or 7 August, is entered on 20 August (Ramsay; Beaucourt has the capitulation signed on the 20th and Dunois entering on the 21st). disputed for Bordeaux: 30 June in Ramsay and Beaucourt; August 1451 in Oman. "On Wednesday, 30th June, Dunois finally entered in state" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 10, Loss of Aquitain, scholarly) "un dernier délai jusqu'au 30 juin" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 2, scholarly) "over when Bordeaux fell in August 1451" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly)
- 070.26 Border: with Normandy and Gascony taken, Calais alone is left to the English on the mainland, after 1451 and again at the war's end in 1453. agreed. "Après la Normandie, la Guyenne était conquise … plus aux Anglais que Calais" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 2, scholarly) "by 1450 he had conquered all northern France except Calais" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly) "The English primarily focused on reinforcing their only remaining possession, [[Calais]]" (Battle_of_Castillon.wiki, Background, wikipedia)
- 070.27 Person: John Talbot, Earl of Shrewsbury, commissioned King's Lieutenant of Aquitaine on 2 September 1452, leads the last English expedition to Gascony. agreed. "September, however, he received a formal commission as King's Lieutenant of Aquitain" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 10, scholarly) "the English government sent the veteran Lord Talbot" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly)
- 070.28 Event: Bordeaux calls the English back: Talbot lands in the Médoc on 17 October 1452, the Bordelais rise, and Bordeaux opens to him and takes the French seneschal Coëtivy. agreed for October 1452; the day Bordeaux was occupied differs: the 20th by the English constable's accounts, Ramsay; the 23rd, Beaucourt. "On the 17th October Shrewsbury landed in Medoc" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 10, Bordeaux recovered, scholarly) "Le 23 octobre, Bordeaux était occupé par Talbot" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 9, scholarly) "He landed in October 1452 at the mouth of the Garonne" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly)
- 070.29 Battle: Castillon, 17 July 1453: the French, besieging Castillon on the Dordogne from 13 or 14 July, have entrenched their artillery park in a camp east of the town by the Lidoire under Jean Bureau; Talbot, come from Bordeaux, takes the abbey (later the priory of Saint-Florent) and attacks the camp, and is killed; the French win. Ramsay 2, Beaucourt 5 and Monstrelet's continuator give the positions and movements of both sides, Oman the attack in outline. agreed. "on Tuesday the X7th July he drew near to Castillon" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 10, Battle of Castillon, scholarly) "Il trouva les Français retranchés dans un vaste camp situé à deux kilomètres à l'est de la ville" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 9, scholarly) "The earl and his companions arrived at the siege on the 17th of July, about day-break" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), Shrewsbury slain, primary) "Castillon, the last battle of the great French war" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly)
- 070.30 Battle: the course of Castillon: the English fight at the palisades for about an hour under the French guns, the Breton men-at-arms (Montauban and La Hunaudaye) come down from the Mont Horable on the English left flank, and the French sally from the camp. agreed (Ramsay, Beaucourt, Monstrelet's continuator); Oman tells the column blown apart by the guns and names no Bretons. "At the last the Breton contingent came up and took the English on the left flank" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 10, scholarly) "les sires de Montauban et de la Hunaudaye descendent avec leurs Bretons" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 9, scholarly) "under the command of the lords de Montauban and de la Hunodaie, were sent for to relieve the French" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), Shrewsbury slain, primary) "the column of attack was blown to pieces by the French artillery" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly)
- 070.31 Figure: Talbot's force at Castillon. disputed: 500 men-at-arms and 800 archers, from Aeneas Sylvius, Ramsay's most likely estimate; about 7,000, Beaucourt, who lists the chroniclers' 800-1,000 horse and 4-5,000 foot (Berry, Chartier) up to 10,000 (Basin); 800-1,000 horse followed by four to six thousand foot, Monstrelet's continuator; Oman gives the army Talbot brought in 1452 as less than 3,000. "The most likely estimate of Shrewsbury's force is that given by Aeneas Sylvius … and 800 archers" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 10, note, scholarly) "à la tête d'environ sept mille hommes, et marcha droit sur" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 9, scholarly) "four or six thousand English on foot" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), Shrewsbury slain, primary) "Lord Talbot with a very small army, less than three thousand" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly)
- 070.32 Figure: the French at Castillon and their guns. disputed: about 1,800 lances with francs-archers, Beaucourt; 1,600 to 1,800 men-at-arms and archers, Monstrelet's continuator; the Bureau brothers had 700 labourers or sappers, Monstrelet's continuator and Ramsay; the guns more than a hundred, it is said, Oman, and three hundred, Beaucourt. "Les forces réunies devant Castillon s'élevaient à environ … avec un corps de francs archers" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 9, scholarly) "sixteen to eighteen hundred men-at-arms and archers" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), Shrewsbury slain, primary) "brothers Bureau were said to have 700 sappers and" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 10, scholarly) "with more than a hundred pieces of artillery" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly)
- 070.33 Person: Talbot is killed at Castillon with his son, Viscount de L'Isle. agreed for his death and his son's; the manner differs: mortally wounded by a cannon-ball, Oman; his palfrey felled by a cannon-ball that shattered his knee and he trodden underfoot, Ramsay; a culverin shot in the leg and French archers, Beaucourt; his hackney struck down by a culverin-ball and he killed as he lay, Monstrelet's continuator. "Talbot, who had been mortally wounded by a cannon-ball, was killed, and so were his son" (archive.org-download-historyofartofwa0002char-historyofartofwa0002char-djvu.txt.txt, Oman, book 14 ch. 2, scholarly) "Earl's palfrey was laid low by a cannon ball, which shattered" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 10, scholarly) "Un coup de couleuvriue l'alteint à la jambe" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 9, scholarly) "hackney of lord Shrewsbury was struck down by a culverin-ball" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), Shrewsbury slain, primary)
- 070.34 Figure: English dead buried after Castillon, four or five hundred, Monstrelet's continuator; eight hundred to a thousand fled into Castillon. single. "buried the dead, consisting of four or five hundred on the side of the English" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3 (continuation), Shrewsbury slain, primary)
- 070.35 Event: Castillon yields the day after the battle, and Bordeaux, blockaded from 1 August, surrenders by a convention of early October; the French take possession on 19 October 1453, which ends the war. agreed. "On these terms Charles' officers took possession of Bordeaux on the 19th October" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 10, End of Hundred Years' War, scholarly) "c'est seulement le 19 que les bannières de France floltèrent sur les tours de Bordeaux" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 5 ch. 9, scholarly)
- 070.36 Event: after taking Rouen, Charles VII commissions Guillaume Bouillé, in February 1450, to enquire into the trial of 1431; Bouillé hears seven witnesses at Rouen on 4 and 5 March 1450 (Murray's heading of the first enquiry, 1449, is old style), and the matter goes no further. disputed for the day of the commission: 13 February in Murray's introductory note; 15 February in Beaucourt; February in Lowell. "I3th 1450, therefore, he issued a Declaration empowering one" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray, Introductory Note to the Rehabilitation, p. 371, primary) "le 15 février 1450, des lettres de commission sont données à Guillaume Bouillé" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 6 ch. 1, scholarly) "In February, 1450, while he was still near Rouen, Charles issued a commission to William Bouille" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly)
- 070.37 Event: in late April and May 1452 the papal legate Cardinal Guillaume d'Estouteville, with the inquisitor Jean Bréhal, opens a second enquiry at Rouen and hears some twenty witnesses. disputed on whose move it was: on the legate's own authority, Beaucourt; at the formal request of Isabelle d'Arc, Murray's introductory note; Lowell has the petition urged in Charles VII's name for five years and put in the mother's name only in 1455. "Là, agissant de sa propre autorité, comme légat du Pape" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 6 ch. 1, scholarly) "the Enquiry, at the formal request of Isabel d'Arc, mother of the" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray, Introductory Note to the Rehabilitation, p. 372, primary) "For five years it had been urged in the name of Charles" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly)
- 070.38 Event: Pope Calixtus III's rescript of 11 June 1455 authorises the revision and names as judges the Archbishop of Reims, Jean Jouvenel des Ursins, the Bishop of Paris, Guillaume Chartier, and the Bishop of Coutances (Richard de Longueil in Murray and the bull, Richard Olivier in Lowell), who take the inquisitor Bréhal with them. agreed. "Par un reserit en dale du 11 juin 1455" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 6 ch. 1, scholarly) "issued June 11, 1455, directed John Juvenal des Ursins, archbishop of Rheims" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly) "Callisto tertio, Pontifici Maximo, obtulit et die u n d e c i m a" (vatican.va-archive-aas-documents-AAS-12-1920-ocr.txt, the bull Divina disponente, AAS 12, p. 524, primary)
- 070.39 Event: the retrial opens on 7 November 1455 in Notre-Dame de Paris, where Joan's mother, Isabelle Romée, with her sons Jean and Pierre, presents the papal rescript and her petition in tears before the judges; a second public sitting follows on 17 November. agreed. "The Case was solemnly opened on November 7th, 1455, in the Church of Notre Dame at Paris" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray, Introductory Note to the Rehabilitation, p. 373, primary) "Le 7 novembre 1455, au matin, dans l'église Notre-Dame de Paris" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 6 ch. 1, scholarly) "On November 7 of the same year, the court held its first sitting in the cathedral of Paris. Isabel appeared with her sons" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly)
- 070.40 Person: Isabelle Romée, Joan's mother, the plaintiff of the retrial, had lived at Orléans for fifteen years by 1455, Lowell. single for her living at Orléans. "in which city she had been living for fifteen years" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly) "Isabelle Romée, mère de Jeanne d'Arc, comparut devant eux en habits de deuil" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 6 ch. 1, scholarly)
- 070.41 Event: witnesses are heard in 1455-1456 at Domrémy and Vaucouleurs (January and February 1456), Orléans (February and March), Paris (April and May) and Rouen (December and May), and Jean d'Aulon at Lyon on 28 May 1456. agreed. "witnesses were heard, in January and February, at Domremy … at Orleans ; twenty at Paris, in April and May ; nineteen at Rouen" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray, Introductory Note to the Rehabilitation, p. 376, primary) "Many witnesses were examined … Some depositions, also, were taken at Paris" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly) "pariterque Aureliae, Parisiis ac in Rothomagensi" (vatican.va-archive-aas-documents-AAS-12-1920-ocr.txt, the bull Divina disponente, AAS 12, p. 524, primary)
- 070.42 Figure: the number of witnesses of 1455-1456. disputed: 34 at Domrémy and Vaucouleurs, 41 at Orléans, 20 at Paris and 19 at Rouen, with d'Aulon, Murray's note (115); considerably more than a hundred, nearly 150 with the enquiries of 1450 and 1452, Lowell; 123, the bull of 1920. "forty-one, in February and March, at Orleans" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray, Introductory Note to the Rehabilitation, p. 376, primary) "Considerably more than one hundred depositions were taken at this time" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly) "centum ac viginti tres testes, cuiusvis aetatis et conditionis" (vatican.va-archive-aas-documents-AAS-12-1920-ocr.txt, the bull Divina disponente, AAS 12, p. 524, primary)
- 070.43 Event: on Wednesday 7 July 1456, at eight in the morning, in the great hall of the archbishop's palace at Rouen, the judges pronounce the sentence, read by the Archbishop of Reims: the trial of 1431, the abjuration and the execution are null; it is published that day at Saint-Ouen and the next in the Old Market, where a cross is set up. agreed. Murray's translation of the sentence itself closes with the 7th day of the month of June, against his note's 7 July and every other source. "following Wednesday, July 7th, for the pronouncement of the final Sentence" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray, Introductory Note to the Rehabilitation, p. 376, primary) "Le 7 juillet 1456, à huit heures du matin, dans la grande" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-5-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-205-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 5, book 6 ch. 1, scholarly) "July 7, at about eight o'clock in the morning, in the great hall of the archbishop's palace at Rouen, the judges pronounced sentence" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly)
- 070.44 Event: the words of the sentence: the processes and sentences of 1431, with the abjuration and execution, are declared null, non-existent and without effect in Murray's translation, null, invalid and void in Lowell's; Thurston calls the court an appellate court constituted by the pope. agreed. "followed null, non-existent, without value or effect" (archive.org-download-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-jeannedarcmaidof028754mbp-djvu.txt.txt, Murray, Sentence of Rehabilitation, p. 327, primary) "have been, are, and shall be null, invalid, and void" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly) "reversed and annulled the sentence pronounced by a local tribunal under Cauchon's presidency" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, the rehabilitation, scholarly)
- 070.45 Event: the procession of 8 May at Orléans: ordered after the relief of 1429 by the bishop, Dunois and the townsmen, a procession with candles to the Augustins with a station wherever the fighting had been; the city accounts pay for it in 1435, and it is kept, almost without a break, to the twentieth century. agreed. "fut ordonné estre faicte une procession le huitiesme … la sollempnité de la procession des Tourelles" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, the Orléans chronicle of the feast and the city accounts for 1435, pp. 296 and 308, primary) "Another chronicler of Orleans, writing about thirty years after the siege, gives an account of the foundation of the festival of the eighth of May … the procession has gone on for more than four hundred and fifty years" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 8, scholarly) "Aurelianensis civitas die eius liberationis, idest octava mensis" (vatican.va-archive-aas-documents-AAS-12-1920-ocr.txt, the bull Divina disponente, AAS 12, p. 525, primary)
- 070.46 Event: on 8 May 1435 the city of Orléans pays two men for their scaffolds where they played a certain mistère at the bridge during the procession; the Mistère du siège d'Orléans, a play of some 25,000 lines by an unknown author, survives in an early sixteenth-century manuscript of the Vatican. single (Quicherat 5, for the payment and for the play's length, author and manuscript); that the play of 1435 was the Mistère du siège rests on Wikipedia (Hamblin) alone. "que ilz firent certain mistère" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, city accounts of Orléans, 1435, p. 309, primary) "est conservé à la Bibliothèque du Vatican parmi les manuscrits de la reine de Suède" (archive.org-download-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-ProcesDeCondamnationV5-djvu.txt.txt, Quicherat 5, Le Mystère du siège d'Orléans, p. 79, primary) "by 1435, a play, {{lang|fr|Mistère du siège d'Orléans}}" (Joan_of_Arc.wiki, Legacy, wikipedia)
- 070.47 Event: by a later order of the court, a procession at Orléans a fortnight after the sentence of 1456, attended by two of the judges. single (Lowell). "A fortnight later, upon further order of the court, there was a procession at Orleans also" (archive.org-download-joanofarc00lowe-joanofarc00lowe-djvu.txt.txt, Lowell, ch. 25, scholarly)
- 070.48 Event: Aeneas Sylvius Piccolomini, later Pope Pius II, wrote of her as a maid inspired by the divine spirit; the bull of 1920 quotes him (and, beside him, Antoninus of Florence, who has her led by the spirit of God). agreed. "keen observers, like Eneas Sylvius Piccolomini (afterwards Pope Pius II), though still in doubt as to her mission" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, after the rehabilitation, scholarly) "ficem Maximum, scribentem : « Puella sexdecim" (vatican.va-archive-aas-documents-AAS-12-1920-ocr.txt, the bull Divina disponente, AAS 12, p. 524, primary)
- 070.49 Event: in Shakespeare's day she was still held a witch in England; by the early nineteenth century English writers (Southey, Hallam, Carlyle, De Quincey) honoured her. single (Thurston). "In Shakespeare's day she was still regarded in England as a witch in league with the fiends of hell" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, after the rehabilitation, scholarly)
- 070.50 Event: the Orléans feast, stripped of its church ceremony in 1791, is discontinued in 1793 and restored by the First Consul in 1803. single (France's Life, read for where to look; the Wikipedia sentence on 1793 cites France, and the one on 1803 cites Warner, unread). "d'Arc at Orleans, shorn of ecclesiastical pomp in 1791, was discontinued in 1793" (archive.org-download-thelifeofjoanofa19488gut-19488.txt.txt, France, Introduction, scholarly) "In 1803, [[Napoleon Bonaparte]] authorized its renewal" (Joan_of_Arc.wiki, Legacy, wikipedia)
- 070.51 Event: after his panegyric at Orléans on 8 May 1869, Bishop Félix Dupanloup has the bishops present sign a letter asking the Holy See for her canonisation; the diocesan tribunal opens at Orléans in 1874. agreed for 1869 (Renoux, Thurston); single (Renoux) for 1874. "prononça le 8 mai 1869 et dans lequel il affirma sa conviction que … tous les évêques présents signèrent une lettre" (academie1744-rouen.fr-wp-content-uploads-2024-09-La-canonisation-de-Jeanne-dArc.txt, Renoux, Un élan orléanais, scholarly) "introduced upon occasion of an appeal addressed to the Holy See, in 1869, by Mgr Dupanloup" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, the beatification, scholarly)
- 070.52 Event: Leo XIII signs the decree introducing the cause on 27 January 1894, and she is called Venerable; Pius X declares her virtues heroic on 6 January 1904 (Epiphany). agreed. "décret d’introduction de la cause fut signé le 27 janvier par Léon XIII" (academie1744-rouen.fr-wp-content-uploads-2024-09-La-canonisation-de-Jeanne-dArc.txt, Renoux, Les étapes romaines, scholarly) "decimus tertius, Praedecessor Noster, die vigesima septima mensis" (vatican.va-archive-aas-documents-AAS-12-1920-ocr.txt, the bull Divina disponente, AAS 12, p. 525, primary)
- 070.53 Event: the brief of beatification is published on 11 April 1909, and Pius X beatifies her in St Peter's on 18 April 1909 (Low Sunday). agreed. "La cérémonie de béatification fut célébrée à Saint-Pierre de Rome le 18 avril 1909" (academie1744-rouen.fr-wp-content-uploads-2024-09-La-canonisation-de-Jeanne-dArc.txt, Renoux, Sur les autels, scholarly) "Quae in Basilica Vaticana, dominica in Albis eiusdem anni millesimi" (vatican.va-archive-aas-documents-AAS-12-1920-ocr.txt, the bull Divina disponente, AAS 12, p. 526, primary) "the process ended in the decree being published by Pius X on 11 April, 1909" (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, the beatification, scholarly)
- 070.54 Event: Benedict XV canonises her in St Peter's on 16 May 1920; the bull Divina disponente bears that date. agreed. "La canonisation fut fixée au 16 mai 1920 à Saint-Pierre de Rome" (academie1744-rouen.fr-wp-content-uploads-2024-09-La-canonisation-de-Jeanne-dArc.txt, Renoux, Sur les autels, scholarly) "B. IOANNAE DE ARC, HABTTA IN BASILICA VATICANA, DIE XVI MAII MCMXX" (vatican.va-archive-aas-documents-AAS-12-1920-ocr.txt, the homily of the canonisation, AAS 12, primary) "St. Joan was canonized in 1920 by Pope Benedict XV." (newadvent.org-cathen-08409c.txt, Thurston, last paragraph, scholarly)
- 070.55 Border: Aumale, lost to the French in 1429, is won back by the English in 1430: Ramsay has it won back in July, with Château-Gaillard in June and Étrépagny; Monstrelet has the Earl of Suffolk take the castle from the lord de Rambures after a siege of twenty-four days, in the chapter of the year Torcy fell (August 1430 in Ramsay); Beaucourt has the English take Torcy, Aumale, Étrépagny, Conches and Blangy after Kyriell's raid of January 1430. agreed for the recovery in 1430; single for July (Ramsay, whose notes cite Wavrin, Cochon and the Bourgeois). "Chateau Gaillard was won back in June, and AumAle and Etrepagny in July" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 28, scholarly) "The earl of Suffolk, about this time, laid siege to the castle of Aumale" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 76, primary) "ils s'emparèrent de Torcy, Aumale, Étrépagny , Conches et Blangy" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 1, p. 35, scholarly)
- 070.56 Border: Blangy, in the county of Eu, submitted to Charles VII in 1429 and is retaken by the English in 1430 (Beaucourt, the line of 070.55); in August of a year Monstrelet tells before his chapters of 1434, the Burgundian Count of Saint-Pol, with English captains, quarters at Blangy to besiege Monchaux, held for Charles VII. single (Beaucourt for the retaking; Monstrelet has a camp there, not a garrison). "he fixed his quarters at a large village called Blangy, in the county of Eu" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 145, primary) "Aumale, Étrépagny, Torcy, Blangy furent soumis" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 1, p. 34, of 1429, scholarly)
- 070.57 Border: Aumale is still English after Paris opens in April 1436: Monstrelet tells, after the chapter of Paris, that the English were driven out of Aumale, which surrendered to a gentleman of Charles VII, at the season the constable besieged Creil. single (Monstrelet). "the English were driven out of Aumarie" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 200, primary)
- 070.58 Border: Sens is in Charles VII's obedience from 1430: Beaucourt has the king stay at Sens in August and September 1430, and quotes Jean Jouvenel on the estates of Reims, Laon, Châlons, Beauvais, Senlis, Troyes, Sens, Melun and Montargis coming to him at Chinon about Christmas 1430. single (Beaucourt). "le Roi resta à Sens, où il passa les mois d'août et de septembre" (archive.org-rt-histoire-de-charles-vii-v-2-Beaucourt-20Histoire-20de-20Charles-20VII-20v-202-djvu.txt.txt, Beaucourt 2, book 2 ch. 6, p. 267, of 1430, scholarly)
- 070.59 Event: the Bastard of Orléans takes Chartres by a stratagem of wagons said to carry salt, with friends inside the town, in April 1432; the bishop, Jean de Fétigny, is killed, and the city is lost to the English. agreed for April 1432; disputed for the day: the Saturday eve of Palm Sunday in the Bourgeois, which gives Easter 1432 as 20 April, so 12 April, the day of Ramsay's note; 20 April in Monstrelet. "early hour on the eve of Palm Sunday a train of waggons" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 30, scholarly) "le sabmedi ensuivant, vigille de Pasques flouries, fut prinse la ville de Chartres par grant traïson" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, the Bourgeois, 1432, item 604, primary) "On the 20th day of April, in this year, was won the noble city of Chartres" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 117, primary)
- 070.60 Border: Meaux, Henry V's last conquest, stays English from 1422 to August 1439: Ramsay counts its retention in 1432 against the loss of Lagny, and has it the only English stronghold east of Paris when Richemont besieges it from about 19 July 1439 and carries the town on 12 August; Monstrelet has it in English hands then and surrendered by Sir William Chamberlain after three weeks. agreed. "retention of Meaux, higher up the Marne" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 30, of 1432, scholarly) "The town of Meaux, which had resisted Henry V for five months, fell in three weeks" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 2, of 1439, scholarly) "the town of Meaux in Brie, then in the possession of the English" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 239, of 1439, primary)
- 070.61 Event: Talbot's campaign of 1434 north of Paris: from May he drives Amado de Vignolles from Beaumont-sur-Oise, follows him to Creil and takes it after a siege of six weeks (20 June, Ramsay), then Pont-Sainte-Maxence, Crépy-en-Valois (stormed) and Clermont-en-Beauvaisis, and turns back before Beauvais. agreed for Beaumont, Creil, Pont-Sainte-Maxence, Crépy and Clermont (Ramsay, Monstrelet); the Bourgeois has Beaumont retaken by force early in May 1434 and the army come back from Creil without doing more, while Tuetey's note gives, from the archives, the treaty of 13 June 1434 by which Creil opened on 20 June. "Following him to Creil Talbot recovered that place also (20th June)" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 31, scholarly) "Crespy in Valoia, which was taken by storm" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 157, of 1434, primary) "vint le conte d'Arondel et ung chevalier d'Angleterre nommé Tallebot, et reprindrent par force Beaumont" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, the Bourgeois, 1434, item 645, primary) "un traité pour la reddition de cette place fut passé le 13 juin 1434" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Tuetey's note 1071 to the Bourgeois, scholarly)
- 070.62 Event: La Hire takes the castle of Clermont-en-Beauvaisis from its governor, the lord d'Auffemont, by seizing him at a parley, and makes it his base in the Beauvaisis, in a chapter Monstrelet tells after one he dates to 1434 and before the Congress of Arras. single (Monstrelet; Ramsay, chs. 31-33, does not tell it). "La Hire laid hands on him, and forced him to surrender the castle" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 164, primary)
- 070.63 Event: before Dieppe falls the English lose Pont-Sainte-Maxence (June 1435, Ramsay's note) and the bridge of Meulan (September 1435, taken by escalade while Saint-Denis was besieged), both to the Bastard of Orléans in Ramsay's note. agreed for Meulan in September 1435 (Ramsay, the Bourgeois, and Tuetey's note from the archives); single for Pont-Sainte-Maxence in June 1435 (Ramsay). "Pont-Sainte-Maxence and Meulan had already been lost" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 32, scholarly) "prindrent le pont de Meurlan" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, the Bourgeois, 1435, item 677, primary) "Au moment de la surprise de septembre 1435, le capitaine anglais était Richard Merbury" (archive.org-download-journaldunbourge54182gut-54182-0.txt.txt, Tuetey's note 1093 to the Bourgeois, scholarly)
- 070.64 Border: Creil, retaken in 1434 (070.61), stays English until May 1441: Monstrelet has the constable besiege it in English hands and march away in disgrace, at the season after Paris opened; Ramsay has the English baffle an attack on it in a May after the siege of Calais of 1436; and in 1441 Charles VII's guns bring Sir William Peyto and his 160 men out on 25 May. agreed. "the constable laid siege to Creil, in the possession of the English" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 200, primary) "the English were able to baffle an attack on Creil" (archive.org-download-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-lancasterandyor00ramsgoog-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 1, ch. 33, scholarly) "Sir William Peyto and his 160 men marched out" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 3, of 1441, scholarly)
- 070.65 Border: Montereau stays English until October 1437: Monstrelet names Thomas Gerard governor of Montereau among the English and Burgundian captains who take Provins in a chapter told before his year 1434; Ramsay has the French besiege it from 25 August 1437, carry the town on 10 October and receive the castle from Sir Thomas Gerrard on the 22nd; Monstrelet has Charles VII come to the siege from Bray-sur-Seine. agreed. "Thomas GHrard, governor of Montereau-faut-Yonne" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue06johngoog-chroniclesengue06johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 2, ch. 152, primary) "on the 22nd Sir Thomas Gerrard surrendered the castle" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 1, of 1437, scholarly)
- 070.66 Border: Mantes stays English until 26 August 1449, when its people make the English commander yield to Dunois. agreed (Ramsay, Monstrelet's continuator). "on the 26th received the submission of Mantes, the people forcing the English commander to capitulate" (archive.org-download-cu31924088011436-cu31924088011436-djvu.txt.txt, Ramsay 2, ch. 7, scholarly) "On the 26th of this same month of August" (archive.org-download-chroniclesengue07johngoog-chroniclesengue07johngoog-djvu.txt.txt, Monstrelet book 3, ch. 15, the summons of Mantes, primary)

## Thin

010.61 The strengths of the two armies at Cravant (about 4,000 Anglo-Burgundians, about 4,000 Scots, a Dauphinist army two or three times larger) rest on Wikipedia alone (Smith and DeVries, Burne, Myers, none read); Monstrelet gives only the 3,000 Scots of the front ranks, and the losses. Ramsay ch. 22, Oman book 14 ch. 2, Beaucourt 2 book 2 ch. 1, Lang ch. 1 and Cagny were searched; Cagny's editor notes that Cagny does not tell Cravant at all. The Chronique de la Pucelle gives the dead and the taken and no strengths (010.61).

010.25 and 010.26 The modern counts at Agincourt (Curry's 9,000 English and 12,000 French; Sumption's 14,000 French; Rogers's 24,000) rest on Wikipedia alone; the older counts in the same lines have primary and scholarly sources.

020: None: no claim of the section rests on `wikipedia` alone. The Wikipedia article on the Herrings was searched for the place of the fight; its report of Cooper's case for Rouvray-Sainte-Croix has no footnote and is no source.

030.35 Only Wikipedia sets the march of 4 May past the bastille of Saint-Laurent, against Lang and the chronicle of the feast, which name the bastille of Paris; the Journal du siège, the Chronique de la Pucelle, Pasquerel, Ramsay and Lowell were searched and name none.

040: None: no claim of this section rests on wikipedia alone.

050: None. Every claim the section turns on has a primary or scholarly source; the Wikipedia lines quoted in 050.3, 050.37, 050.41 and 050.62 are kept only to record where the articles part from the sources.

060: None: no claim of this section rests on `wikipedia` alone. The coast road of 060.24 rests on Père Ignace (1657) alone, whom Quicherat, who prints him, warns against trusting until originals confirm him; France follows him.

070.13 The extent of English France in 1444, Normandy and a strip of Gascony, rests on Wikipedia (Wagner) alone. The Truce of Tours passage of Ramsay 2 (ch. 5) was read and gives no line of 1444; Ramsay gives the Norman places held at the end of 1449 (070.16).

070.46, in part: that the mistère played at the Orléans bridge on 8 May 1435 was the Mistère du siège d'Orléans rests on Wikipedia (Hamblin) alone. Quicherat 5 gives the payment of 1435 and the play separately, and was searched for both.
